CHAPTER 1: Introduces a 

Lady Down a Well

UST beyond the village of Pegomas, not more than a few hundred meters as you proceed along the highway leading to the sea, stood the Villa 

Chèvre d'Or. A wall, breast high, of bricks covered with plaster which, in patches, had peeled away, leaving a scattering of gory scars, enclosed its grounds. 

Iron gates hung open. In its garden, a cluster of dark cypress trees stood like mourners around a lichened marble faun whom the artist had petrified in the ecstasies of a Panic dance. To the north an olive grove grew, in tangled patterns of silver, where a breeze stirred leaves like ripples on a clouded lake. 

Pigeons flew, in endless maneuvers, from the grove to the roof of the villa and returned, with a fluttering of white wings, and a susserant chorus of cries.

The villa was uncertainly Moorish in design. Its arched doorway was set with blue-glazed tiles, and a lantern encrusted with bits of colored glass hung there at the end of a heavy chain. Incongruously, a red-tiled roof rose above its ivory walls to a modified gable. Two earthen jars stood at each side of the driveway, where dried geraniums still struggled to grow, without water or any attention. About the villa and the gardens there was an unmistakable air of neglect. Nothing had fallen into ruin, but everything seemed upon the verge of decrepitude, excepting only

the olives and the cypress trees that wore an appearance of immortality, aged yet unaging.

Just at the moment-it is about three in the afternoon of Tuesday, November 27, 1928-a small tan

Citröen stood outside the gates, parked so far off the highway that its wheels half settled into the drainage ditch that ran past the carious walls. No one was near. A moment's deduction, involving an inspection of the car and of the villa door, slightly ajar, might yield the information that some one was insidesome one, moreover, whose visit was to be so brief, and whose familiarity with the owner was so slight, that he did not even venture to drive in through the gates, but preferred to leave his car standing outside and penetrate the enclosure on foot. 

From the direction of Cannes a Buick approached, traveling at a moderate speed, slowing down when the parked Citröen came in view, passing it eventually at no more than a snail's pace, while the driver leaned perilously far out to examine it. He was a young man who would have been recognized anywhere in the neighborhood as an American, and yet of such pleasant and distinguished appearance that a British colonel, staying in Cannes and irritably nursing his gout, had been heard to remark of him, "American, you know, but not noticeably so. He might almost be from Manchester." That is praise.

It must be confessed at once that the American's approach at this particular moment was not at all accidental; and it must further be stated that his motives were sheer curiosity and a wholly involuntary admiration for the owner of the Citroen whom he had seen once or twice but whom he had never met.

Since Saturday, in fact, when he had arrived at the 

Grand Hôtel de la Reine de Grande Bretagne, he had been struck by the astonishing personality of the girl. Not precisely beautiful, perhaps (he had never admired mere beauty), she had impressed him, at a glance, as a girl in a thousand. A catalogue of her features, with which he had occupied his thoughts to the exclusion of more cosmic topics, was not impressive. Her hair defied description. It was not so lovely that words could not adequately express its glory; on the contrary, it was rather unnoticeable hair, and out of her presence he could not recall its exact color. He was compelled to check off: Item, hair, darkish-and proceed to the next. 

Item: eyes, darkish. They were not eyes at all, he told himself; certainly no mere physiological instruments of vision, compounded of nerves, muscles, and lenses. Nor could he precisely call them windows of the soul, as less accurate and more fanciful minnesingers had done. They were-well, they were the girl. She spoke to you, and expressed a variety of things with her eyes, and you were richly conscious of her communication, and wholly unaware of the eyes themselves. 

Item: nose, largish. Not Roman, or anything ghastly like that. He could not remember exactly the shape of her nose. He could only decide that it was not obtrusive. 

Item: mouth, large. Not unpleasantly so, of course. She was certainly neither the lily nor the ivy type. She defied classification. The American was forced, in his unsatisfactory summing up, to describe her in such general terms that no one, hearing him, could have picked her out of a crowd. Her one 3

distinguishing feature was a dimple in her left cheek; it appeared only when she laughed. As a consequence, he could not picture her without it, for she was always laughing.

She seemed, as far as he could discover, unmarried and unaccompanied. At the Reine she knew every one. That spoke volumes for her, for the habitués of the Reine constituted an exclusive society. 

The American was inclined to believe that not one of them was worthy to polish her shoes. His own strangeness had thus far prevented him from meeting her. He knew her name, and it surprised him, because he thought of her as a compatriot rather than French. Possibly she traced her ancestry to France, and preserved traditions in being called Elise Valery. 

Whatever the reason, he decided, after a while of thought, that he approved of the name.

Earlier that afternoon he had sat behind one of the huge windows of the Reine, idly watching the passers-by in the Rue d'Antibes. He had heard her speaking to an elephantine Englishman with sandy hair. There was no mistaking her musical contralto voice, which carried very distinctly. She said, "I'm looking at a villa, Colonel. It's out toward Pegomas. 

Will you drive out with me?"

The colonel refused. He was booked for golf with the major, he explained. The American decided it must be a very pressing engagement. Not otherwise could he comprehend the rejection of so promising an invitation. "I suppose I'll have to go alone, then," she had said. "Good-bye. I shall blame you if I am kidnaped."

It occurred to the American, a few moments later, 4

that he had not yet driven toward Pegomas, and knew nothing of the country in that direction. It was a warm, clear afternoon, with a few diaphanous windclouds like wisps of tulle gathering over the black hills of the Esterel. He had nothing to do. He realized suddenly that he rather wanted to drive to Pegomas.

So now he craned out of his Buick to inspect the tan Citröen parked before the Villa Chèvre d'Or, his hand poised to shift gears and drive on, in the absence of any excuse for lingering. And, as he did so, he heard her voice, unmistakably hers, from some remote region inside the villa. It came to him muffled by intervening walls, but there was no room for doubt that she was calling to some one. He listened. "Help! For Heaven's sake, help!" she called. 

After a pause, "Help, help!"

The American jammed on his brakes and brought his Buick into the ditch ahead of the Citröen, jumping out before the motor had gasped into silence. 

Grimly he raced through the iron gates, up the gravel driveway to the door. "Help!" "I'm coming!" he called back, raising his voice into a bellow that would have penetrated to the nether regions. "You're a hero, whoever you are!" "Where are you?" the American shouted, pushing his way into a darkened hall, blinking in the gloom, uncertain where to turn. "Are you in the hall?" Her voice was still indistinct, but there were no sounds of fear in it. Brave girl, he thought. 5

"Yes." "All right. Walk straight ahead. About a dozen paces. Be careful of the stairs." 

He carefully stepped forward ten paces, and began to make exploratory motions with his toe. Suddenly descending steps yawned in front of him. "I've found them," he announced. "Are you all right? Where are you? What's happened?" "I'm safe for a minute. No-I think I'm slipping -I'm-Oh!" 

Somewhere in the darkness below him he heard faint scrambling sounds, then a ripping, grating noise, like loose stones falling from a height. She gave a little scream, and a moment later there came a thump and a splash. He broke into anxious perspiration. "What's the matter?" he shouted, wild to rush into the gloom ahead of him but restrained by an ounce of caution. It would never do to barge down there and trip over something, and break his neck.

He could rescue no one if he were sprawling, wounded, in some black hole. ... "What's happened?" "I'm drowning, I think. Not immediately, though. Where are you?” "I don't know! Why the devil didn't I bring a flashlight?" "Why didn't you? Have you got one?" "In the car outside." "Go back and get it." "Not on your life, I won't! Do you think I want you to drown while I look for a fool flashlight?" "I'm all right. Soaked through. Water up to my -wait a minute-yes, up to my neck. There are a

dozen bullfrogs in here, if there's one. How in the world can French people eat the things?"

Delirious, he thought, in a cold sweat. "You'll get that flashlight, won't you?" she called. "You couldn't do a thing without it. It's dark as pitch, and you'd only fall in with me. That would be too ridiculous." "Maybe you're sure you're safe for a moment?" "Oh, surely! But do hurry! I think there are snakes, too. I felt something then just like one. 

There it is again-Oh!" She gave a howl of dismay, and the American turned and fled up the steps three at a time. In a minute that seemed eternal to him he hurried down the driveway to the Buick, rummaged through three side pockets with a hand that seemed to have swelled to unmanageable size, before he found what he was looking for, and raced back. Once more in the dark hall, he called out to find out whether she was safe. "It wasn't a snake. Only another frog. Have you the flashlight?"

He turned it on, throwing its light on the papered walls of a narrow vestibule. Just ahead, the stairs dipped down to a cellar. "I'm coming. It's all right. Don't worry! Keep cool! Whatever you do," he begged earnestly, "keep cool." "Are you trying to be funny? I'm half frozen!"

At the foot of the steps he paused and investigated. 

The cellar occupied a space equal in size to the area of the villa. There were no partitions, or doors other than the one that seemed to open upon the garden. 

The girl was nowhere in sight. "Where are you?" he shouted.

"Are you in the cellar?" "Yes." "Be careful. Go to the left. There's a trap door near the wall. And, whatever you do, don't fall through it."

He crossed the cellar to the wall upon his left. In one corner, a small iron plate had been lifted aside, exposing a three-foot-square opening in the floor. 

He directed his light into it and saw, six or seven feet below, a second floor level of cement. "There's no water," he yelled doubtfully. "Are you down there?" ""Way down," she answered. "There's a ladder."

He found a series of iron rods fixed diagonally across a corner of the opening, descending through a three-foot thickness of masonry. Below that, a chain ladder led to the lower chamber. With his flashlight thrust into a side pocket of his coat, he began his climb, coming at last into a small, square, damp chamber, with walls of brick patched here and there with moss. It was about seven feet by seven, unlighted except by his torch. At the far end-corresponding, he imagined, to the exterior wall of the villa-the cement had crumbled away, and an opening in the floor, large enough to admit a body, yawned blackly. Apparently there was yet another chamber below the one in which he stood. 

Thrusting the flashlight through the dehiscence, he repeated, "Are you down there?" "Yes. Thank heavens for the light. Hold it steady for a minute until I can see where I am."

He held the light as far down the hole as he could reach. He could see only a patch of brackish water,

reflecting his torch, agitated suddenly by some movement, so that the reflection was shattered and a hundred sparks seemed to hurtle apart and wink out. 

The girl was moving, and he heard splashing, and finally a gasp. "All right?" "Yes, thank you," she said reassuringly. Her teeth were chattering. "It's a well, I think. Perfectly round, with brick walls." "Is there a ladder? I can't see one from here.” "No luck," she replied. "There's no foothold on the walls at all." "How far down are you? Distances are deceptive from above this way." "Ten feet. Twelve feet. Fifteen feet," she hazarded. "Which is it? Fifteen?" "It looks like a hundred, if you must know. What are you going to do?" "I've got a towing rope in the car. If I make a loop in it, do you think you could hold onto it? It may be a long, hard pull." "No harm in trying," she said promptly. "If I stay here much longer, I'll get moldy. Luckily I've got gloves." "Gloves?" He was puzzled. "Oh, to protect your hands. I see. Can you hang on till I get the rope?" "If you'll tell me anything else I can do. "Right-o! Be right back." 

He was less alarmed now, and his second trip to the car was less frantic. He found the towing rope, and returned with it in a moment or two. In the subcellar he made a loop in it, and threw one end into the well, paying it out with a silent prayer that it

might be long enough. The distance could not have been so great as Miss Valery had believed, however, for when he had passed ten feet, with a good five more to spare, he heard her exclaim, "Ouch! I didn't see that coming! There! I've got hold of it now." "I'll have to be careful of the edge of the hole,” he warned. "It's still crumbling. Wait a minute. 

I've got an idea.”

He passed the end of his rope over the lowest rung of the ladder leading up to the cellar. These rungs were iron bars connected by heavy-linked chains. He did not doubt its strength, though he tested it by swinging his full weight upon it. The rung performed the duty of a pulley. "Now," he said, "put the loop around you. Sit in it, if you can manage. I'll have all I can do hauling you up. You'll need your hands to keep you away from the walls." "Just a minute. This isn't so easy. Have you ever tried crawling into a loop in five feet of water? 

Filthy water," she added. "No, thank the Lord! That's one experience I've missed. How about it? Can you make it?"

Frantic sounds of scrambling and splashing drifted up to him from the well. Then, "That's it, I think. Golly, it's cold down here! 

Are you sure you can pull me up?" "I can," he promised grimly. "It may be slow.

The friction at the edge of the hole may be pretty bad. But don't worry.' "I won't. But if you want to win my eternal gratitude, don't drop me."

He started to pull, slowly, a few inches at a time, ΙΟ

taking another turn or two of the rope around the rung so that it might not slip back, watching meanwhile the edge of the hole illuminated by his flashlight which he had laid upon the floor near by. Once or twice he paused for breath, and to inquire if she were quite all right. "All's well," she announced. "I'm dripping wet. 

Here's one sport dress the chambermaid will inherit." "That's bad luck. Never mind. It might be worse." "Of course it might. Who are you, by the way? 

I've forgotten to ask." "I'm- Oh, hell, I can't talk and pull at the same time. Wait awhile." 

A pause, during which the rope snaked over the ragged edge of the hole, fraying badly but not dangerously. The girl began to giggle. "I feel exactly like Mohamet's coffin," she declared. "But I tell you one thing; no one would make a pilgrimage to see me the way I look now. 

Not that I'm not worth it."

He said nothing. By now she was approaching the opening. "Another six inches and you'll have to stop," she said. He heaved once more, fastened the rope securely to the ladder, and returned to the hole. She swung directly below the lip of the opening, against the brick wall. Two kid-gloved hands were raised cautiously toward him. He grasped them, and was surprised at the strength in them. "Steady, now! Upsy daisy!"

Bracing himself against the side wall of the subcellar, he pulled her up until her knees were floor high, II

and she could, for a moment, rest there, while he caught her under her arms and dragged her back.

She sank finally upon the cement floor, a dripping Aphrodite in sodden clothes that emitted uncomfortable sucking sounds as she moved. She was breathless, and blue with cold. "Do you suppose there's a fireplace upstairs?" she asked, shivering. "Pray for a fireplace!"

While she stood up and began wringing water out of her skirts, he gathered up his rope and flashlight. "Do you feel strong enough to climb the ladder?" he inquired solicitously, eyeing her disarray with concern. "Or shall I . . .?" "You've done enough hauling and carrying," she interrupted. "Of course I'll climb. The exercise will warm me. Listen to these shoes squelch. Aren't they awful?"

Ahead of him, she climbed the ladder and drew herself up into the cellar. When he had followed her, she said, "We'd better put the cover back over that opening or some other innocent visitor will meet a watery fate. Then come and find a fire.”

The iron cover restored, he followed her to the upper floor, lighting their way with his flashlight. 

Miss Valery left behind her a wet trail, leading into a large, low-ceilinged living-room. To her delight, a large open fireplace occupied one end wall. A fire was invitingly laid. There was nothing needed but to touch a match to it.

When the blaze had begun to creep up through the kindling to the two logs above, he moved about the living-room, drawing open the heavy hangings that concealed the French windows. Sunlight streamed in, paling the firelight. 12

Miss Valery watched him with interest while she stood with her back to the flames, still shivering almost beyond the point of being able to articulate. 

He made efforts to avoid looking at her; she seemed so bedraggled with her knitted skirt hanging a good five inches lower than it should, weighed down with water, her hair-he realized now that it was the color of chestnuts-stringy and escaping in shapeless tufts from a light green sport hat that had slid to one side where it hung on desperately, like a crumbled ruin. 

Her stockings-he tried most of all not to see them -had become disengaged, and hung limply around the tops of her shoes. "Do I look that funny?" she asked at last. He blushed and tried to stammer. "Because you're breaking your neck to keep from looking at me." "Can't I get you something?" he asked desperately. "Couldn't you put a curtain around you, or something?" "A curtain? You haven't a scrap of imagination. I won't get back my self-respect until I'm dressed like a human being again. And dry." "I wish I knew what to do," he wailed. "I can't go back to Cannes and get things for you. You'd die of pneumonia in the meantime." "You've thought of that, I see." "Do you suppose there'd be clothes in any of the rooms upstairs?" "No. The house is empty. For rent." "Oh. Bad luck. Look here, isn't there a town near here? Pegomas, or something?" "Town? Have you ever seen it?” "Afraid not. I'm a stranger here." "I forgot that. Well, there are about five hundred 13

inhabitants, if that many. It isn't even a village. It's a cluster of farmhouses." "I see. Well, surely there are women there. And where there are women, there are clothes. In the meantime, perhaps the curtain. . . . "Oh, give me the blessed curtain, if it would make you any happier! I don't suppose you carry any safety-pins with you?" "No. I'm a second-rate hero, I'm afraid. No safety-pins, no dry clothes. Not even the inevitable brandy. I'm sure a drink of brandy would do you good." "It probably would. I wish you hadn't mentioned it." She stood closer to the fire which, by now, was roaring and crackling pleasantly. A hissing sound emanated from her clothes, and thin clouds of steam had begun to rise. "Which curtain do you want?" he asked. "The one over the door there looks warmest."

Without waiting for her assent, he stripped it from its rod and passed it to her. It was a velvet portière, heavily lined and encrusted with stiff gold braiding. 

It looked warm, however, and after feeling its texture somewhat dubiously, she draped herself in it. "There," she said. "That satisfy you?" 

He controlled an impulse to laugh. The portière was eight feet long, and she had wrapped one end around her as an improvised skirt, permitting the upper portion to hang over one shoulder as a cape. 

Had she not still worn that ridiculous hat, she might have looked vaguely Roman. "I'll hurry back," he promised, "with clothes and brandy. I suppose you need everything. I have an overcoat you might wear." 14

"How much do you weigh?" "A hundred and seventy-five. The coat might be a little large." "You're not fat." "No. Just heavy. Brawny. All brawn, no brains; otherwise I'd carry safety-pins with me. Now, are you all right to be left?" 

She laughed, her color returning, her teeth no longer chattering. "Of course I'm all right! I'm not an infant. I won't get into any more mischief while you're gone, if that's what you mean." He blushed again, and grinned. "Do you know what to get?" "Well-er-I suppose-everything. The tout ensemble." "That will do very nicely." "Would you like a peasant's wife, too?" "Heavens, what for?" "To help you dress." "Oh, certainly not! The clothes are enough. And if you don't hurry, I won't even need the brandy." 15

CHAPTER 2: Describes a Mantle for Aphrodite ses

Te 

HE VILLAGE of Pegomas lies nine kilo meters inland from the sea, on the banks of the brook called Mourachone which straggles through over a rocky bed to join, a little farther along, the Siagne River. Two roads cross here. A scattered group of farmhouses, white-walled and redtiled, set in dirty enclosures, forms the outskirts. At the crossroads there is an open square shaded with thick chestnuts, where idlers play an endless game of bowls. On several days a week the square becomes a market place, crowded with booths and carts. The square is lined with convenient tobacconists and a tiny, unattractive café.

The American, when he drove his Buick into the village, turned instinctively toward the café and drew up before it. The proprietor, a heavy, placid 

Provençal, supplied him with a flask of brandy, and listened attentively, and without visible astonishment, to his further request. "For a young lady. A young American lady," the

American explained. "She must have clothes, or else she cannot return to Cannes." "She lives in Cannes, and comes to Pegomas to seek a couturière," the proprietor remarked, making change. "That, monsieur, is certainly the best story 

I have heard this year." 16

A MANTLE FOR APHRODITE 

The American grinned appreciatively. "It happens," he said, "that Mademoiselle by accident became immersed. Where can I turn for dry clothes?"

The proprietor waved his hand toward the square, as colorful as a gypsy camp in its market-day aspect. "Surely there you can find what you require. Not to suit American ladies, perhaps. But what covers one may serve to cover another. N'est-ce-pas?" 

The American agreed. "The difficulty is," he said, "that I-to be precise, mon ami, Mademoiselle's requirements are for a complete outfit. My personal experience is limited to the exterior; you comprehend?"

The proprietor did comprehend, with an expansive smile. "It is plain that you are an American. A Frenchman-I say it with pride-would not be at a loss in such a situation. I know what you need. I have a wife. If she ..."

It was a wife or daughter that the American had been angling for. This one produced-she was a feminine version of her husband, large, genial, and tranquil the situation was once more explained. 

Madame knew precisely what was needed, and 

Madame, the American in tow, sailed into the market square for such an orgy of buying as her heart had not delighted in, in years. She cruised from booth to booth, choosing here a pair of stockings, there a curious garment whose nature the American did not inquire into, holding them up one at a time for his approval and his judgment as to size. He found that 

Madame conceived of the needy young lady as built on lines similar to herself. He was forced, with the 17

appearance of each new article, to demand one at least four sizes smaller, and even then he envisioned Elise draped with them very much as if they had been grain sacks. 

Madame's eye for color, furthermore, was more curious than commendable, and the resources of the market, while quite sufficient for Madame's needs, did not correspond to his conception of the contents of, say, M. Worth's, or M. Lanvin's establishments.

He and Madame did their best; but he was curious to see how Elise would look when she had assumed these garments. 

Satisfied at last, he suggested that Madame accept some trifling gift as a token of appreciation for her invaluable services. Madame became ecstatic, and chose from a particularly kaleidoscopic counter a vermillion shawl covered with magenta roses and twining, leafed stems. She had longed for that shawl for months, stealing out each market day to feel it and drape it around her shoulders, her whole being compounded of longing and an unconquerable terror lest some one else buy it and thus remove it beyond even the sight of her admiring eyes.

She returned to her café, arrayed in the splendors of Sheba, and the American, his bundles on the seat beside him, turned his car once more toward the villa, and bore his treasures thither in triumph.

Elise had taken the opportunity, during his absence, to remove her soaked clothing, and arrange the curtain about her more discreetly. The fire blazed merrily, and she sat before it, on a large cushion on the floor. "Well," she greeted him, "did you have any success? I was really worried for fear you'd . . .” 18

A MANTLE FOR APHRODITE 

"I'd what?" He threw down the bundles couch. "-you'd forget something or other." "I had assistance." "Really? A-a girl?" upon the "A settled married woman with a secret passion for red, and who probably weighs a great deal more than I do. Her taste. . . well, I leave that to your imagination. You'll see for yourself when you start unwrapping these things. But they're dry; they have that advantage."

She rose to her feet, looking more Roman than ever now that the portière had no hat to battle with. "I can't thank you enough, Mr. Marshall. Really, you've put yourself out terribly for me." "That's not fair," he protested. "You know it was a pleasure. . . .” He stopped abruptly. "You know who I am?” "I've seen you around the hotel," she said hurriedly. "Some one pointed you out to me; I forget who. Shall I open the bundles?" "I hope they're not too-paysan," he said, passing over the package which contained the dress, and edging toward the door. "While you look at them I'll hunt up a glass and pour you some brandy. I suppose you still want some.' "I do. Thanks again."

He made his way down the dark hall to a door opening into a kitchen. He knew enough of kitchens to find glasses immediately. He was somewhat worried for fear there might be no water running in the faucets; it was usual, he remembered, to pay for one's water when one rented a villa. But his fears were unfounded. A faucet spluttered and cast out a shower 19

of rusty spray, coughed, and settled down to release a steady stream. He fussed around, washing glasses and making work to fill the time until she was ready. 

After awhile she called him, and he carried two glasses and a water pitcher into the living-room. "How do you like it?" she asked, turning around in front of him. He was, to say the least, surprised. 

She wore a dress which had caught Madame's eye: a garment of some heavy material, a deep maroon in color. It had long sleeves and a high waist, very pronounced and exaggerated by a narrow belt of the maroon cloth that, on a stouter woman, would have divided the wearer into hemispheres. The skirt, puckered under the belt, expanded like a bell, and hung to within a few inches of the floor. The neck was high, and the gown was trimmed with a Vshaped collar of imitation lace, and tight lace cuffs. 

Under it, Elise wore black cotton stockings. "All I need," she remarked, "is a cameo pin, and a pair of lace mitts." "They had both at the market. Shall I go back for them?" "You'll do nothing of the sort! I look picturesque enough as it is. Have you ever wondered what your grandmother looked like in her hey-day?" "Often. I think I'm proud of my grandmother." "Nonsense! She was a sight! Not yours, particularly. Very likely yours was very much nicer than the rest. But my grandmother. Is that the brandy?

That's much too much. About half that, and mostly water." "There. That it?" "Perfect." She took a glass and sat down upon the sofa before the fire. "I didn't think an hour ago 20

A MANTLE FOR APHRODITE 

that I'd be sitting here dressed like Gammer Gurton, becoming convivial with a perfect stranger. Who are you?" "You know my name," he said, leaning against the mantelpiece. "Robinson Marshall, isn't it? You come from

Virginia-one of the Lynchester Marshalls, if I'm not mistaken. Wasn't your mother one of the Pollard Robinsons of Washington?" "You leave me with nothing to say. Must I show my pedigree before we continue our acquaintance?" "You Virginians are so amusing about your genealogies, aren't you? It means so much to you. Now, I haven't any genealogy. I don't even know where my grandfather was born-either of them." "French, wasn't one of them? Valery sounds 

French, so does Elise." "I assume he was French. I was born in Baltimore. We are almost neighbors, when you think of it."

Marshall declined to take advantage of the opening, purely because of timidity. He said, a little lamely: "Funny we never met.' "Isn't it? Possibly you may have met my brother 

Norman, though. I believe he knows a good many 

Virginians." She hesitated. Then, abruptly, she put down her empty glass and rose. "I must be getting back to the hotel. She laughed. "Shall I restore the 21

glasses to the kitchen while you rehang the portière and put out the fire?" "I can do all three. Are you warm enough in your ensemble, or shall I get you my coat?" "I'm quite warm, thanks, after the brandy. As it is, I insist upon getting into the hotel by a back door 

I know. No one ever sees you that way. If I wore your coat besides, I wouldn't dare go in at all." "May I see you after your tea to find out how you are? CHAPTER 3: Concerns a Raphael and a del Sarto M 

ARSHALL, in the Buick, followed Elise to the hotel and conducted her through the alley entrance she insisted upon using. After he left her at the door to her room, he found that it was barely five, and that at least three hours must pass before he could reasonably expect to see her again-possibly more, if she dined late. Time promised to hang upon his hands. He knew one or two people in Cannes, but he felt no urge to share their company just at the moment. Eventually, and lonely, he strolled down the Croisette, where the wind, promised earlier by the cloud wisps, was whipping up the sea and flinging a fine spray over the promenade. Opposite the casino he became chilly, and entered, lounging idly into the Ambassadeurs, where he took a table and ordered tea.

This early in the season the room was half empty. 

A dozen couples danced in the middle of the room; a dozen more drank tea or cocktails at tables bordering the dance floor. He saw Elise, apparently quite recovered from her misadventure, dancing with the elephantine Englishman whose antics, had he been leading any one else, would have been ludicrous. He had no sense of time; he danced with a precision that seemed to involve mental counting-"One-twothree-four, one-two-three-four!"-and he bent for- 23

ward at that portion of his figure which should have been his waist.

Colonel Poplin, Marshall supposed, and felt disposed to dislike him, for no adequate reason other than that he was making Elise look ridiculous. He watched him with a frown, and proceeded to dislike, in order, his sandy, thinning hair brushed back so carefully, his watery, pouched eyes, into one of which a monocle had been jammed, his reddish nose, his heavy lips and stained teeth-the man seemed to have only four or five, which protruded—his cheeks, which hung jowl-like, and his faint marking of freckles. Poplin capered and chattered in an expansive good humor, and Elise laughed and seemed to like it. 

Once she saw him and waved to him. Poplin ogled him for a moment; then the two of them seemed to discuss him. Elise, Marshall decided, was telling him the story of her mishap. It seemed to amuse him. 

He haw-hawed audibly, above the tinkling of a tango. Marshall suddenly desired no more tea, paid, and stamped back to the hotel.

He was in a less violent mood when Elise met him after dinner. She had certainly survived her tea. 

She wore a white evening gown, beaded in rose patterns, and looked surprisingly fresh and lovely. "Hello," she said. "Where shall we sit?" "There's only one place to talk quietly," he said. "I know. The bar." 

At the Grand Hôtel de la Reine de Grande Bretagne, the bar is hidden away at the bottom of a flight of steps. There are two rooms: the bar proper, and a second room with a table or two, dimly lighted and 24

comfortable. Seated there, Elise ordered coffee and 

Marshall, cointreau. "I saw you at the Ambassadeurs," Marshall said finally. "Was that Colonel Poplin?" "It was. What do you think of him?” "Must I tell you?" 

Elise laughed. "No, I think you'd better not, if that's how you feel. He's a dear, though, even if he is the worst scandalmonger in Cannes. And they breed in Cannes like guinea pigs. But I refuse to listen to him. He likes you," she added. "That's a compliment." "Is it? Thanks." Marshall did not sound enthusiastic. "Dances well, doesn't he?” "I hate sarcasm. He dances like a rhinoceros, and you know it." "Then why ܡܕ "I told you: he's the president of our dramatic society. I'm the leading lady. I have been, for two years now. Really, it is amusing to putter around, and pretend it's for charity." "Who's the leading man?" Marshall wanted to know. "You won't believe me. It's Colonel Poplin." "Him? Leading man? Now please don't take me for a greenhorn like that." "It's a fact. This year I persuaded him to direct, and not act. He's had bad throat trouble, and it didn't take very much coaxing. We need a new leading man." "I see. Who will it be? Some one with moreshall we say, appeal?” 

Elise looked up at him with amused eyes. Her dimple became very pronounced. 25

"You know how scarce eligible young men are in 

Cannes," she remarked. "I nominated one Robinson

Marshall, and the nomination was seconded, and unanimously carried." "Accepted. With unbounded joy. But I can't act." "Neither can Colonel Poplin." "Now that that's settled, what do we act in? "Well, if you really are helpless, perhaps Colonel

That leaves you no excuse not to begin."

He noticed a change in her mood. She stirred her coffee meditatively, staring at her cup rather than at him. "I might as well tell you the whole thing. You may think me a fool, but I'll risk that." 26

"That's no risk. I won't do that. I promise." 

She glanced up and smiled. "I've been here several winters," she began, at last. "Two years ago I met a M. Trabeaux, a dear old man, almost seventy, with snow-white hair and a goatee. He was a marquis, I think, but he never used his title. You would have loved him; every one did. He owned the villa out there on the Pegomas road, the Chèvre d'Or. I often drove out there to tea; he did a great deal of entertaining. About a month ago, when I arrived here for the winter, I learned that he had died, and that his heirs-a nephew, I think, who does something in Paris-had put up the villa for sale. It's a lovely place-you saw that to-day—but it's rather far out of Cannes, and for that reason the price they're asking is low. "Now comes the fairy tale part of it, and I warn you that if you don't want to believe it you don't have to. M. Trabeaux owned the villa only a few years-two or three, I don't remember which. Before he had it, it belonged to a man-I don't remember his name-who was very well known as an art dealer. He was a thoroughly reliable dealer. I've looked him up and found out that much about him, but he got into trouble once or twice by trying to smuggle paintings and art objects of one sort or another out of Italy. You know how strict the Italians are about that. He was caught once, or, rather, his agent was, and there was no end of trouble about it. "He was killed in an automobile accident, and the villa sold to M. Trabeaux. But there are two very curious things that have turned up since his death. 

One of them is that a man who was suspected of being the agent of this dealer-I'll remember his name in 27

a minute; you'll know it at once-bought two paintings in Florence, from a private collection. The pictures were sold on the understanding that they were not to be removed from the country, but were to be hung in a collection in Rome. But as soon as the sale was concluded and the paintings were on their way to 

Rome, they vanished completely. Two empty crates arrived in Rome, and the pictures had disappeared without leaving a trace. "Now this agent had worked for-Henri Chavins, that's what his name was. Remember him?” "Chavins? Isn't he the man who detected the spurious Rembrandt in the Rijks museum in Amsterdam, and managed to bring the original to light in a pawnbroker's in London? I remember something about that." "That's the man. Well, this agent had worked for 

M. Chavins before, and the Italian authorities began to be suspicious. But they couldn't prove anything.

The deal was apparently quite bona fide, and the paintings actually stolen. Finally the buyer confessed that he had been paid a substantial sum to claim that he had bought them; actually, he believed the paintings had been sold and smuggled out of Italy to a foreigner. The agent was hunted up, but he had suddenly disappeared, too. They couldn't trace him anywhere. "The Italian authorities sat tight and waited for those pictures to reappear. They didn't. When M. 

Chavins was killed, the villa was searched, and though he had several valuable works, there was no trace there of the missing pictures. He died intestate, and had no heirs that could be found. The villa and its contents were sold at auction-that's when M. 28

Trabeaux bought it-and the French government, of course, pocketed the money. "Now the other odd thing that comes to light is this: M. Trabeaux told me, last year, that he was planning to build a wing to the villa, and for that reason he had secured from the original architect the plans for the house. He examined them thoroughly, and he discovered a curious thing. The villa is an oblong, and the plans showed a cellar exactly the same in size and shape. They didn't show anything, however, about the subcellar which M. Trabeaux knew was under it. And he began to investigate that subcellar, out of curiosity. It was just a square chamber that had never been used, as far as he could tell, with only the opening in the cellar floor admitting to it. He felt all around the walls and found that, on the inner side-that is, under the cellar-there was a distinct hollow sound, as though there might be another chamber sealed up. Also, the floor sounded hollow." "The well?" Marshall interrupted, with interest. "The well, of course. Only then there was no hole in the floor over it. Now, M. Trabeaux knew about

M. Chavins, and he put two and two together, so he explained to me, and decided to open that wall, just in case those two paintings were hidden back there. But his illness prevented him from doing it, and he died before he had any chance to investigate. "He told you about that inner chamber?" "Yes." "Did he tell any one else?" Marshall demanded. about it. But he made me promise not to tell any one else, because he wasn't at all sure what his legal position would be if the pictures were there. Mr. Marshall. As far as I can see, the paintings were legally bought by M. Chavins, and consequently were his property. "Very well. If the paintings belonged to M.

"Of course I would. But I hate to have you bother going all the way upstairs for them." "Nonsense! It's no trouble at all. And you don't know how much your advice means to me.' 

Marshall, no less susceptible to flattery than any other man, sat in the bar in a pleasant glow of satisfaction while Elise departed on her errand. When she returned, he had ordered sidecars. She slipped into her seat beside him and handed him two photographs, twenty by fourteen. He examined them with interest.

The first, bearing in Italian the title "The Madonna of the Crystal," was a Raphael. In an oval frame, the Virgin supported the Child against her shoulder, holding in her right hand a small crystal globe into which the Infant was peering. At her sides St. John and St. Joseph knelt to adore her. The second, a Trinity, was the del Sarto. It was circular, and bore, as if emerging from a black cloud, three faces, similar in features, yet varying in expression: 

Christ, in the moment of passion; the Father, benign and omniscient, and, overshadowing them both, an etherialized, all-pervading Holy Ghost. "What do you think of them?" Elise asked, as he inspected them. "I should say your estimate of their value is much too low. They're magnificent. You're certain they're genuine?" I wonder-Miss Valery, would mind very you much if I'd borrow these for a day or so? I'd take the best care of them."

CHAPTER 4: Portrays a 

Rehearsal

T TWO O'CLOCK on Wednesday afternoon, Elise joined Marshall in the Citröen and directed him to the Boulevard Carnot. They drove a kilometer along its plane-shaded length, and drew up, at her instructions, before a white villa in a garden of palms, where a number of other cars were already parked. "It's the Collins' villa," Elise had explained. "You know of him even if you don't know him." "I'm afraid I haven't even that pleasure." "Yes, you have. He's Lloyd Collins, the playwright." "Oh, is that who he is? Yes, I know of him." "We're to rehearse here. It's his new play, you know. We're to try it out for him before he stages it in London.” "That's interesting." "He's married," Elise added. "You'll like his wife."

Mrs. Collins greeted them effusively at the door. 

She was a bantam woman, with Titian bobbed hair and sharpish features. She contrived to add an inch or two to her stature with the aid of high heels; and buzzed perpetually, like a humming bird. There was an unmistakable air of the theater about her, in spite of the fact that she had never graduated from the 37

amateur stage. Yet ambition begets vanities; and to look at her, you would surely suspect Dukane Collins of being Sybil Thorndyke's closest rival. "Here's Elise! No, you're not a bit late. Commander Craft hasn't come yet. And this is Sylvester,' she exclaimed, eyeing Marshall with frank approval.

Marshall cleared his throat to protest, but Mrs. 

Collins forestalled him. "You're to be our Sylvester!" she babbled on. "It's 

Lloyd's play, you know, and I followed every stage of it. You'll be a perfect Sylvester. You won't mind my calling you that, I'm sure. I never can remember people's names. I'll always think of you as Sylvester."

Collins himself had approached and drawn them into the vestibule. He was tall, blond, shy, and placid; the direct antithesis of his wife. He shook hands with Marshall, smiling happily, and urged him into the living-room, which had been cleared of furniture and reset, so that chairs and tables occupied absurd positions, facing the sliding doors with artificial unanimity. "Dukane will introduce you," he said superfluously. Dukane Collins had already thrust her arm through Marshall's and was conducting him on a little tour around the room, introducing him to a dozen people whose names slipped his mind as fast as she pronounced them. He saw Poplin, in a gray suit that might have been tailored for a trained bear, and next to him a statuesque woman with graying hair combed back and bobbed, and rather tragic eyes, whose name, he believed, was Mrs. Temple. His impression of her was confused-an unbeautiful but expressive face, a black gown fastened at the shoulder with a sham- 38

rock-shaped pin of brilliants, and black slippers with glittering buckles.

Captain Temple, who was seated on the corner of a mahogany table that had been dragged into the middle of the floor, and who did not rise when he shook hands, seemed to be her husband. He next met Colonel Seaford Bassington-Cowan, whose wrinkled face was accentuated by a monocle, never absent, a combed and waxed moustache, mouse-colored, and the vaguest suggestion of a goatee. "He's our comic relief-aren't you, Colonel?"

Dukane buzzed. Marshall mentally decided that, whatever his good points, Colonel Bassington-Cowan could never be considered either comic or a relief. 

He smiled pleasantly, examining Marshall critically before he spoke. "I," he said, "am the butler. I drop things. I'm beginning to wonder, this soon, how I shall ever see anything on the stage without my monocle. Butlers don't wear monocles.' 

There was Mrs. Bassington-Cowan then, a woman who reflected his wrinkled skin, but otherwise suggested Elinor Glyn, who wore an unbelievable dress of ochre tweed and a hat so covered with black satin ribbons and poppies that its original shape and texture were indistinguishable. Marshall discovered later that she was psychic, and that she had dabbled in everything mystic, beginning with yogi and working up or down, as the case might be.

The last of the present company was an unpleasant 39

man, resembling, more than anything else, the proprietor of a fish market. His face was heavy and exhuded sarcasm, his moustache was of that formlessness that approximated a disfiguring hirsute growth. 

Colonel Poplin, when forced to describe him, invariably did so by insisting that Major Pontifex Porey was no gentleman. That much was obvious. Beyond that, Porey possessed an acerbate disposition and an excellent opinion of himself. Like Lloyd Collins, he wrote plays, and if he achieved less success than his present host, he considered that merely a reflection upon the mass intelligence of audiences. 

Mrs. Collins accompanied her introductions with a cascade of comment, and eventually left Marshall to the mercies of Mrs. Bassington-Cowan, who welcomed him with a Giaconda smile and said, "American, of course?"

Marshall occupied a chair drawn close to hers, the two intended to substitute for a sofa. "Yes. I imagine there's no mistaking that." "You don't talk like one," she admitted graciously. "Most Americans shriek at the top of their lungs, and talk through their nose. You don't. You have a pleasantly modulated voice. You must have been educated in England."

Marshall concealed his amusement and replied, "No. In America." "Not really!" The Bassington-Cowan eyebrows rose and fell. "There is something familiar about you, Mr. Marshall. Haven't we met before?" "I doubt it, Mrs. Cowan. I am sure I wouldn't forget you if we had." "Bassington-Cowan. My husband is a cousin of 

Lord Bassington-Cowan. Perhaps," she added, 40

"we've met on psychic journeys. Are you psychic at all?" "I'm afraid not." "You are not a scoffer, I hope," she remarked in an admonitory tone. the doorbell, returning in a moment convoying none other than Commander Craft himself.

My function is to turn Miss Valery out into the snow. "Over my dead body," said Marshall. "Exactly. Over your recumbent body, at any rate. 

You have to recover for the final curtain. The inartistic happy ending!" 

Craft shook his head. "Incidentally," Marshall inquired, "must I develop an Oxford accent? As your grandson . . .” "Don't worry. Good old Collins arranged that.

He's put in a line explaining the situation. Something like, 'Oh, he's irresponsible! He was educated in America.' I have an idea that if your accent is too 43

Yankee, they'll bring you on in blackface to sing a mammy song." 

Dukane Collins fluttered by, rounding them up. "Everybody here? Now, you have your parts.

"Here is where Miss Valery makes her snowbound exit," Craft remarked, sotto voce, hobbling to the makeshift sofa and settling himself upon it. "Don't bother about the business," Dukane continued. "We'll go through the lines and the positions just to familiarize ourselves with them."

The rehearsal began, with a thousand interruptions. Mrs. Collins, who had assumed the rôle of director, thought up business as they went along and, contrary to her original plan, paused every moment or two to inject it. Collins stood meekly in the background, a copy of the play in his hands, following the action but saying nothing. Those who were not occupied on the stage sat waiting their cues in the vestibule where a few spare chairs had been placed for their convenience.

Marshall found that his part was a long one, and that there were few moments when he was not on the stage. During the second act, when, ostensibly, he was dashing about Mayfair in a taxi searching for an errant Miss Valery, he found the opportunity to retire to the vestibule for a moment or two to relax.

Only Elise and Major Porey were not on the stage at the time; Elise, to his regret, was conversing in low tones with Mrs. Bassington-Cowan (whose presence at the rehearsal was to be explained only by her 44

excess of wifely pride in the colonel's histrionics) on subjects particularly feminine, to judge from the gestures accompanying their dialogue. A REHEARSAL 

"Yes. Do you know it?" "M. Trabeaux's old place, isn't it? I knew him fairly well. The place is for sale, I've heard." "So Miss Valery tells me." The question of Porey's intentions caused him acute anxiety. Since hearing Elise's story of the paintings, 47

he had subconsciously come to consider the adventure as much his as hers, an unmannerly presumption that her attitude of confidence and good fellowship had served to induce. Porey's interference just now would be most annoying. And he wondered whether

Porey's elaborate explanations of his motives might not be a trifle too elaborate. Could he be aware, too, of that unexplained chamber under the cellar? 

He dismissed the idea as fantastic. He was becoming avaricious, and suspicious of every one. Porey was merely a meddlesome busybody. To ignore him seemed the best policy. 

His meditations were interrupted by Mrs. Collins's penetrating voice. "Sylvester! You're supposed to be hammering at the door!"

He rose moodily, with a glance at Elise, and made his entrance. 48

O MARSHALL'S deep regret, he had no opportunity, either that afternoon or that evening, Too to have a few moments' private conversation with Elise. He felt a pressing need for it. He was as anxious as a little boy to tell her about Porey's annoying interest in the villa, and by sharing his worries with her, to ease them. He wondered whether she knew that Porey considered buying, and, if she were aware of it, whether she believed that Porey knew uncomfortably much about the subcellar regions of the Chèvre d'Or.

But Elise, unconscious of his mental turmoil, and acting only out of the fullness of her heart, suggested that the Temples drive back to the hotel with her, since they had not brought out their own car. Marshall sat next to Mrs. Temple, therefore, and was forced to discuss, with what enthusiasm he could muster, the very fine adventuress rôle Lloyd Collins had written and assigned to her. She gushed over it, in the restrained way in which Mrs. Temple achieved all of her effects, saying little, yet conveying an impression of effusion with practically no effort. If 

Elise had not been sitting in the front seat, laughing over remarks of Temple's which he could not catch, he might have admired Mrs. Temple sincerely. 

To cap the situation, Elise, as soon as she had 49

Bassington-Cowans at the Grand Hôtel St. Louis near Le Cannet.

He wandered somewhat aimlessly about the lounges after dinner, drifting eventually to the bar where he found Temple, already several brandies to the good, perched on a high stool, indulging in another and idly shaking poker dice on the polished marble bar-top. He signaled to him to join him, and Marshall, lonely, and not otherwise engaged, accepted. "Idiotic rehearsal. Idiotic play," Temple announced, when a brandy and soda had been set in front of Marshall. "Was it or wasn't it?" "The first rehearsal is usually chaotic, isn't it?" "Oh, quite. I say, though, it is amusing, what?" 

Temple drank, long and contentedly. "Idiotic play, like all of old Collins's. It will be a ripping success. 

You watch."

They passed an hour in the bar, discussing banalities. Mrs. Temple, it developed, had joined the 

Bassington-Cowans for dinner, leaving Temple alone and bored. "I was asked, but while old Bassington-Cowan's all right, he's a beastly bore. And his wife's always taking psychic journeys. Temple bobbed his head. "You can spot 'em in a minute. Everlastingly branded."

Marshall found him amusing, though his original feeling of distaste was not entirely dissipated. As he drank more, Temple seemed to close in upon himself, saying less and less, losing no whit of his poise and dignity, but, like a self-centered cat, sitting in silence, purring to himself over ideas and fancies that he did not share with any one. He was not precisely sullen, but he was on the verge of becoming so. 

Finally he roused himself and slid off his stool. "The night is young. Come along to the casino for a little baccarat. Does that appeal to you? I've had a winning streak a week now."

Marshall excused himself, pleading sleepiness after the rehearsal, to which Temple merely nodded, his hands deep in his coat pockets. Odd chap, Marshall decided; perhaps not so superficial as he appeared.

His thoughts, however, did not linger on Temple. 

They were occupied with the Chèvre d'Or and its possible contents, and he decided, after awhile of restless tossing in bed, to settle as soon as he could one or two of the uncertainties in Elise's story. 

With that object, shortly after ten the following morning, he made his way to the Galleries Fleuries.

The morning was warm and sunny, and flower beds in the lawns were blooming in patches of scarlet and magenta. In the surrounding arcades, a few shops were preparing to open for the winter. Others, more timid, still slept behind dusty curtains, so that the Galleries appeared half deserted. In the center, where later smart Cannes would assemble at little 51

iron tables for its morning cocktails, workmen in soiled white overalls hammered and dabbed paint at 

Achino's tea pavilion which was still partly concealed behind excrescent scaffolding.

There were one or two people lounging through the arcades; and Marshall, the only one of them who seemed to have any urgent business in the world, brushed past them without noticing them. One tall, heavily built man, unexpectedly turning in from the Rue des États-Unis, caromed into him, and at once became voluble in his apologies, taking off his hat, restoring it in almost the same motion, and scurrying on through the arcades toward the Croisette and the sea.

Marshall eyed him with disapproval, muttering an irritable oath. Stupidly clumsy of the man; why didn't he watch where he was going? 

And above all, who was he? Marshall knew that peculiar hurrying, waddling gait, that straw-colored hair lighter than his own, and those shifty blue-yellow eyes whose color seemed to change like the color of an alexandrine in different lights. He had seen them recently, but he could not remember where. A shopkeeper, possibly; still, the man was undeniably 

English. His apologies had been muttered in English, not French. Marshall, who had reached his objective, paused before entering to glance in the direction the man had gone. To his surprise, the man was standing by the gate at the entrance to the Galleries, watching him curiously. Catching Marshall's eye upon him, he turned with elaborate unconcern, and vanished down the Boulevard de la Croisette.

And Marshall, puzzled, entered the exhibition salon of Gustav Meynitz, an art dealer whom he 52

knew, and upon whose judgment he felt he could rely.

M. Meynitz himself was very much in evidence in the salon, superintending the placing of a Matisse and a Van Gogh in advantageous positions. He was dumpy and corpulent, with large, hairy hands that he considered unesthetic and which he endeavored to hide under his coattails, bringing them forth only under compulsion. He welcomed Marshall with an unctuous smile. "Ah, M. Marshall! In Cannes! You have a villa, perhaps?" "No, M. Meynitz. I'm staying at a hotel." "Oh! Then you do not come to purchase." M.

Meynitz propelled a gilt chair in Marshall's general direction and invited him to sit down on it. "No. I've come for your opinion in a little matter, M. Meynitz. I'm fortunate to find you here and not in Paris. I know no one who could help me better." "Thank you, M. Marshall. Whatever you say.' 

Marshall unwrapped the photographs which Elise had lent him and passed them to Meynitz, who took them, pursed his pink lips, and squinted at them. "I'm anxious to learn the recent history of those two paintings. Can you help me?" 

Meynitz examined them intently. "Yes, I can tell you without trouble. I keep records of all sales of important works, for my own information. Please to wait here, and I will find out for you exactly what you wish to know. I imagine

I could tell you without examining my books. The paintings are shall we say, notorious? A good word, that. Now, just a minute." 53

He trotted off to invisible inner quarters and remained away a minute and a half. He reëmerged, beaming. "M. Marshall, you are interested in these paintings-why?"

Marshall, always cautious, and somewhat broadminded on the subject of little white lies, replied ingenuously, "You know I often write magazine articles, M. Meynitz. I believe the history of those two would make an entertaining article, don't you?"

Quite true, both statements, he consoled himself. 

M. Meynitz was quite satisfied. "So they would, monsieur! Very entertaining.

Fantucci. There are indisputable records of it. And the Raphael's history is unquestioned. They are genuine." "And their value? People," added Marshall, "are always interested in values." There was much more of this enthusiastic eulogy, which Marshall finally checked. "And their recent history?" he prompted. "Ah, this is interesting indeed! They were 54

purchased by the Princess Altissimi just before her death, and presented by her to the Condoveri collection in Rome. I knew the Princess well; she was a great lover of the beautiful, and interested in the Condoveri collection which she had helped to found. 

The Fantucci are impoverished; they were forced to sell their two best works, and they sold to the Princess, who was, I believe, a cousin by marriage, at a price considerably less than they might have brought in the open market. But they were assured that the 

Princess would not attempt to remove the works from Italy, where they belonged. "But, monsieur, when the paintings were boxed and crated and shipped, some one substituted similar crates, quite empty, and diverted the paintings. How it was done, they have never discovered. Oh, but it was clever, because those crates, one thought, were never out of sight of some responsible person's eye. 

And yet, somewhere, those two were stolen, and vanished quite away. "I see." Marshall was thoughtful. "I have heard rumors," he said cautiously-"only rumors, you understand, M. M. Meynitz protested in great indignation, that to think any such thing approached blasphemy. "The Princess was entirely above suspicion!" he declared stoutly. "Why should she cause them to vanish? Had she not bought them openly?" "I have heard that she bought them through an agent whose activities were not always above suspicion. Is it possible-I merely advance the suggestion, M. -is it possible that this agent desired to carry the paintings out of Italy, and, for that reason, arranged matters so that it only appeared that the Princess had purchased them, while, instead, some foreign dealer had done so?" 

In spite of Marshall's caution, M. Meynitz seemed tempted to throw a fit. "No, no, no! A thousand times no! The Princess, she would have been struck down before she would consent to such a scheme. The fragments of one of them were rescued and compared with photographs of the missing Raphael.

Journey HERE was no rehearsal that afternoon. Instead, Mrs. Collins, who seemed to have assumed full command of the direction, stage management, and costuming of her husband's play, had arranged a session, including the entire cast, at a costumer's and wigmaker's in Nice. Marshall, more eager than ever to speak to

The return train left Nice at five, when the winter sky was already darkening, and the lights of the city had begun to twinkle, one by one, as stars appear in the sky. Instead of boarding one coach as they had previously done, several of them entered a second. 

Marshall, following his plan, entered an end compartment, where an insignificant-looking Frenchman dozed in a corner, a newspaper spread over his chest, completely hiding everything but his peaceful face and a short pair of legs that did not quite touch the floor.
