sat back, ruminating. The little Frenchman still dozed in his corner, the newspaper covering him. He had not moved or awakened.

Marshall considered Elise's attitude. She was disappointed, no doubt, and would naturally try to pick holes in any unpleasant reports she might hear. He could not blame her, but at the same time he could not disguise from himself the fact that her position in regard to the paintings was far less secure than it had seemed to be at first. Even if the Princess Altissimi had been bribed, she was dead and could not testify one way or the other. The directors of the 

Condoveri collection surely had no hand in such a deception; they were the ostensible receivers of the paintings and consequently, if the Princess's intentions had extended to the point of making a record of them, the owners, unless it could be proved that M. 

Chavins or some other person had actually bought them. And Marshall, usually optimistic, could see no way of proving any such thing. Elise, he concluded, must be satisfied, if the paintings were eventually disclosed, with the altruistic restoration of the works of their supposed owners. 

The train drew into Antibes, hesitated, and pulled out again. They passed Juan-les-Pins and Golfe 

Juan, clusters of lights on the rim of the vast black plain of the sea. Marshall smoked in silence, and when, eventually, the lights of Cannes-Eden rose above the horizon, he threw his overcoat over his arm and strolled out into the corridor.

In France, trains, unlike road traffic, pass to the left. He stood before the end door in the dimly lighted corridor, peering out at the sea and the dark masses of palms and villas at the water's edge which 64

A JOURNEY 

they passed. The corridor remained empty. Behind him there was only the blank wall of a washroom and, beyond that, the compartment he had just left, deserted but for the sleeping Frenchman.

The train, approaching the Cap de la Croisette, a point of land jutting out to sea, swung inland amid thick clusters of pines through whose branches an occasional flicker of light penetrated. The tracks curved widely to the right, and the train rattled and shook its way along, flinging Marshall now and then against the door. Once he was forced to catch hold of it to preserve his balance. His hand came in contact with the lever which engaged the catch. To his surprise, he found it unlocked. As the door opened outward, he narrowly escaped an accident. He drew it shut hastily, and stood for a moment, in a cold sweat, wiping his forehead with a handkerchief.

The train emerged now from the belt of trees, and roared along an embankment, ready to plunge into the heart of Cannes. Marshall became aware of some one in the corridor behind him. People were stirring. In a moment, he knew, the corridor would be filled with passengers ready to alight. He did not turn. 

And, with a suddenness that found him totally unprepared, something struck him a terrific blow on the back of his head. He stumbled forward, his outstretched hand striking against the glass of the door.

But the glass seemed not to be solid. Through the pain and stunned feeling which followed the blow, he felt the glass falling away from him, and a second later, saw himself dropping into a black emptiness. 

As in a nightmare he dropped, his knees striking something sharp and movable, his momentum pitch- 65

ing him forward, rolling over and over down an endless slope, while, nearby, somewhere above him, he dimly heard the rattling of wheels. He remembered nothing more. 66

CHAPTER 7: Describes an 

Unmannerly Intrusion

T THAT point the P.-L.-M. tracks, emerging for a brief glimpse of the sea before they descend from their embankment into a sunken channel penetrating the eastern quarters of Cannes, cross, over a bridge, the Boulevard Alexandre III, which here turns left at right angles to the sea-front Boulevard de la Croisette, and climbs sharply to the pine-clad slopes that stretch back to the foot of the Californie hill. In 1928, this particular section of sea-front was deserted. On the sea side of the embankment there was nothing but an unlighted stretch of road, swinging in a wide curve southward to the point, sheltered by a sturdy sea wall. Two villas, both unoccupied and hidden behind pink-tinted walls and clusters of palms, shadowed the road. The scaffolded front of a new hotel in course of construction loomed gloomily near them. After you had passed under the bridge, the road forked. Three new buildings, deserted still, and, like the hotel, not yet completed, guarded the fork like gigantic city gates pierced with a hundred crenellations.

Marshall, in his fall from the Nice local, struck the gravel which crowned the embankment a yard or two this side of the bridge, and slipping, rolled down the incline into a thick undergrowth near the walls of one of the empty villas, on the sea side. 67

There he lay, stunned, for several minutes. He was unconscious of the screaching and clashing of a suddenly halted train above, its last coach stopping just beyond the bridge. He saw nothing of the bobbing lanterns, the anxious conducteur and his followers, who briefly made an inspection along the tracks and the embankment, but who, in their haste and their confusion, failed to discover the still form of Marshall lying in the concealment of high weeds.

The delay was brief; a moment or two later, the lanterns and their bearers had trooped back, and the train vanished into its sunken channel like a snake into its hole.

Marshall, after a while, struggled to painful consciousness. He lay jammed against the trunk of a mimosa tree, his arm doubled under him and throbbing as if a regiment of devils were driving white-hot toasting forks into it. For a moment he made no attempt to move, endeavoring to collect the scattered remnants of his wits, and disperse the mental fog which enwrapped him. 

There had been a train . . . a train · · He drew himself up on the elbow of his free arm, starting a cascade of fiery sparks that danced in his eyes. When they passed, he peered through the screening leaves and reeds at the deserted boulevard.

At last, aware that, but for the arm which alternately held no feeling whatever and seemed ready to burst in a throbbing agony, he was not shattered beyond repair, he struggled to his feet, resting against the tree until the sickening dizziness decreased. 

His clothes were in rags. What had become of his overcoat he had no idea. It was nowhere near him, as far as he could see in the darkness. Something 68

white and long lay at his feet, and he contrived, at the expense of another dizzy spell, to stoop and pick it up. A knitted silk scarf. Not his; he never wore scarves of that sort. Without conscious volition, he stuffed it into the tattered remains of the sidepocket of his jacket.

His legs, and knees particularly, were skinned, bruised, and bleeding. His hands smarted as if they had been scraped and soaked in an irritant. Altogether, surveying himself as well as he could, he decided that he had never in his life looked or felt more disreputable. 

What had happened? His first efforts at concentration brought on a splitting headache, and he desisted. Something about that damned train, he remembered. Train. 

Oh, there was no use worrying now about it. Get home; that was the first thing to do. Get home, and see about that aching arm. 

He stumbled out of the underbrush onto the

Croisette. It was poorly lighted and deserted at this dinner period, and he made his way along it unseen, turning gratefully, nevertheless, into the black-shadowed length of the Rue de Cercle Nautique, which led to his hotel. There he hugged the walls of villas, avoiding the few street lamps, acutely conscious of his appearance, grappling in a dazed way with the problem of how to pass through the lobby of the hotel without causing something approximating a riot. He remembered the back entrance Elise had shown him on the occasion when she herself had wanted to sneak in unobserved. Opposite the alleyway leading to it he paused to reconnoiter. There was no one in sight. The few servants who might 69

have been hanging about were busy, just then, in the dining room which was due to open in a few minutes. 

He traversed the length of the alley, and came into a dimly lighted cellar-like hall, where the crude, unpolished boarding of a freight lift caught his eye.

The lift was unattended, but it was one of those automatic affairs so common to Mediterranean hotels, which operate at the push of the proper button. Marshall hastily bobbed into it, shut the doors, and pushed the button marked 4. 

His good fortune did not desert him. The fourth floor hall, which might have been filled with guests descending to an early dinner, was, on the contrary, quite deserted. He managed to slip down to his room without being seen. Once inside, the curtains drawn and the light turned on, he surveyed himself in a mirror.

The effect was less ghastly than he had anticipated. 

Miraculously, his head had escaped injury. There was still a tender bump on the back of his skull where he had been struck, and a small cut on his forehead had bled slightly, giving him an awe-inspiring aspect of piratical ferocity. But his coat and shirt had been ripped in a dozen places, exposing his arms and a goodly portion of his back. His trousers had suffered as well, and when, with cautious but painful efforts, he had removed his clothing, he found himself a mass of abrasions and bruises.

He contrived, with acute agony, to bathe and dress again, in more civilized garments. To his relief, he found that, however painful his arm might be, it was not broken, and that fact cheered him considerably. 

Dressed again, and his ruined clothes consigned without regret to the wastepaper basket, he rested 70

for a moment on the bed and made another effort to think.

What had happened? He recalled now the dim corridor of the train and the winking lights along the sea that he had been watching through the carriage door. He remembered the swinging open of that door, and his haste to pull it to again. And then, bit by bit, the subsequent events returned to his mind: the blow, which had not been accidental, he felt certain, and the reopening of the door 

Who had hit him? Why? There had been some one in the corridor behind him, and he tried, without success, to recall from which direction this person had come. He-if it was a man-might have come down the corridor from one of the compartments, or he might have emerged from the washroom directly behind where Marshall had been standing. For that matter, it was equally possible that he might have come from the next coach. 

Marshall had paid no attention to him at all; he could not say. 

Who, then, could it have been? Some one who knew him, and deliberately pushed him off the train in an attempt to murder him? In Heaven's name, who would want to murder him? The supposition was idiotic; that couldn't be the explanation. Had he, then, been mistaken for some one else? That, too, upon consideration, seemed hardly likely. Marshall, with his massive build and his peculiar type of blondness, did not resemble many people even superficially. The attack could hardly be explained that way.

There was a possibility, of course, that it was accidental, this blow and its consequences. Yet he 71

doubted that. It had been struck too deliberately, with too excellent an aim. What had struck him, if not some implement in the hands of a man, consciously propelled?

He remembered the scarf, and, rummaging in the wastebasket, found it and spread it on his knees to examine it. It was vaguely familiar to him-a strip of knitted white silk, with two green stripes at either end. But he could not place it, and an examination of it did not reveal any identifying marks on it. 

So it became an additional mystery. Had it belonged to the man who struck him? And had-this appeared to Marshall, in his partial befuddlement, as a splendid bit of imagination-had it been wrapped around a stone or some such heavy object, and been, therefore, the very implement with which he had been struck? He regretted that he had not made a closer inspection of the ground around the tree where he had fallen. He might have found something else which might have helped to identify his assailant. 

There would be time enough for that in the morning, he decided. The patch of weeds into which he had fallen did not appear to be ground frequently disturbed by passers-by or intruders of any sort. Nobody would be apt to disturb it before morning.

At present he was in crying need of supper, and he tucked the scarf into his pocket, extinguished the light in his bedroom, and turned into the bathroom, extinguishing that light also.

He was there, half hidden behind the open door, when he heard a cautious fumbling at the knob of the outer door. The maid probably, he thought, come to leave towels. He was on the point of coming into 72

the room, when it occurred to him that the maid would certainly use her key, not knowing that the door was unlocked. Whoever was outside was doing nothing of the sort. The knob was turning very slowly; a moment later, through the crack of the bathroom door, Marshall saw the outer door swing open. His room was dark, the hall faintly light. 

Against the light he saw a man's figure briefly outlined. The man entered, and closed the door softly behind him.

Marshall's first impulse was to switch on the bathroom light and advertise his presence. Then a sense of caution prevented him from doing anything so foolish. He realized suddenly that this intruder might be none other than his assailant of the train. 

When two odd things occurred, one on the heels of the other, it was possible, at least, that they were in some manner connected. The man, if he was responsible for Marshall's fall, had evidently meant business. To switch on the bathroom light would expose Marshall as a possible target, if the man were armed, and do nothing whatever to render him visible and recognizable.

For a moment Marshall remained silent, watching. The man, evidently locating his objective in the glimpse of the room he had had in the hall light, crossed quietly to a chest of drawers against one wall, and fumbled for a moment in one of its drawers.

Marshall, cautiously tip-toeing out of the bathroom, was not given time to make a complete exit before the man had recrossed swiftly to the door. As he drew it open, Marshall threw caution to the winds. "Hold on here a minute!" he called. 73

The door was drawn open suddenly, and closed as promptly. Marshall sprang forward, found the switch, and turned up the bedroom lights. The room was empty.

Out in the hall there was no one. Almost next to his room, however, was the entrance to a flight of steps and the freight lift. By the time Marshall reached it, that was empty also. There were no sounds of ascending or descending footsteps, and the freight lift was several floors below, rising toward him.

He returned to his room, puzzled and annoyed. 

There seemed to be no sense whatever to this business. What could a man want in his chest of drawers?

In the particular drawer the man had gone to he kept nothing but collars, a spare razor, and a few odds and ends, gloves, and so on. He pulled it open now, and examined its contents. There was nothing missing; of that he was certain after a moment's inspection.

The affair puzzled him more than ever. 

But by now his appetite had vanished. He could think of nothing but this curious intrusion, and its possible bearing upon his misadventure. After a moment's thought, he came to a decision. Taking care to lock his door as he left-a precaution he usually neglected he reëntered the back hall, and pressed the button for the freight lift. He descended and made his way out of the alleyway to the Rue d'Antibes without being observed. By good fortune, he found one of Cannes' few taxis parked before an open public house, and managed to call out the driver by a vigorous assault on his asthmatic horn. 

The driver, finally summoned, mounted his seat, 74

wiping with grave ostentation the ends of his moustache. "Where to, Monsieur?" "To the Villa Lou Matagot," Marshall directed, and sank back into the darkness of the cab. 75

CHAPTER 8: Heralds a Borrowed Body HE VILLA LOU MATAGOT crowns an eminence rising boldly above the Route de Grasse. A steep road, threading its way between lichen-stained walls, leads to it, and then peters out, dwindling to no more than a mule track, which vanishes entirely among a group of farmhouses a few meters beyond. The villa's garden is sunken into a scarped terrace, sheltered by mimosas and climbing roses. Above it, the villa itself sprawls, like a sphinx, thrusting out two paw-like wings to protect a tiled court.

Here Commander Craft lived, in ideal bachelorhood. Marshall, whose personal problems prevented him from perceiving anything in its normal aspects, was struck, nevertheless, by the solid comfort and pleasing charms of Lou Matagot. A servant, when he had paid off his taxi, showed him into a large room, where a fire blazed in a tremendous raised hearth. The hearthstone jutted out into the room, piled with cushions, forming a seat. Beyond a sofa the room lay concealed in obscurity. A radio, well-modulated, dispensed Schubert. 

Marshall stood before the fire, warming himself.

The night was cold, and he had come without an overcoat. He had not waited long before Craft limped in. 76

A BORROWED BODY "This is splendid, Marshall! You must stay to dinner. I was about to sit down alone." "Oh, Lord! Dinner!" Marshall turned to him and groaned. "I'm sorry, Craft. It was stupid of me to come just now." "Not at all! I hate dining alone." "The fact is, Craft, I'm in a blue funk." "Oh, come, now!" Craft hobbled to the sofa and sat down. Marshall tried the hearthseat, leaning back against the cushions, and clasping his hands around his knees. "We'll have a cocktail, son, and then you'll unburden this weighted mind of yours."

He noticed the scar on Marshall's head, and frowned inquisitively. "This?" Marshall stroked the cut with a cautious finger. "That's the least of it, Craft. For a while I thought my arm was broken. It's not, of course. All right again now. I shouldn't have disturbed you." "No apologies! I feel complimented, highly complimented. What's the trouble?"

It was not so easy to tell. Marshall, in any light, emerged a rather foolish young man from the recent adventure. He had behaved, he believed, no better than half-wittedly, and he had run, at the first opportunity, to Craft, like a little boy to his elders, without attempting to work the problem out for himself. It was hard to explain that to Craft honestly, and still preserve his self-respect. He said, grinning a bit sheepishly: "I'm running to you with my troubles, Craft, like a big baby. I feel damn silly about it. But you've got ten times the brains I have. . . . And that's no compliment, I'm afraid."

No, Craft, this happened on the train. Just before we pulled into Cannes. You know that bridge over the Boulevard Alexandre III?”

Once begun, the story was not so difficult to tell. 

Craft's attitude of frank interest made it easier. "That is a story!" he said once. "Then what?"

Marshall brought the tale down to the moment of his arrival at Lou Matagot. "There you have it," he said at last. "What do you make of it?"

Craft did not reply at once. He poured himself a second cocktail and sat, without drinking it, staring into the flames. "Let me understand this clearly," he remarked. Well, that's a handicap.

A BORROWED BODY wasn't, or he would have spoken to me. It wasn't very light in the corridor, you know, but not so dark he might not have recognized me.”

"Give me a day or two and let me try it. You say I'm the only person who's seen you since the accident to identify you?" "Absolutely. Unless our man recognized me in my room." "Probably not. Thought you were another visitor or something. And even if he did, he'd be all the 89

more curious about it if you failed to reappear. Stay here to-night, at any rate," he urged, "and let me have to-morrow to nose around. Do you agree?"

Put that way, Marshall saw no way to refuse with grace. Craft was a good sort; you could trust him absolutely. And it was rather pleasant to feel that you didn't have to bother your muddled head about things while he was watching out for you. "All right. One day, anyway."

So it was arranged. They discussed plans after dinner, while the radio picked up London and gave out the latest reports on the King's illness, and interspersed between them thumping choruses of "I Can't 

Give You Anything But Love, Baby," and "Just 

Like a Melody Out of the Sky." Later Marshall retired to a south room and a comfortable bed, and read himself to sleep with a detective story that served, at the same time, to divert him and to cause him periodic moments of introspection when he compared his own predicament to that of the fictional hero.

He found a meditative Craft at breakfast the next morning. His host, with an Englishman's love for kidneys and bacon, and the other accessories of an

English breakfast, had not adopted the French habit of tea and rolls. A lavish feast was spread and served on the refectory table. Craft himself was cheerful but not communicative during the meal. Afterwards he drew Marshall into the walled garden, warm under a bright sun. "Now, old man, here's a shock for you," he said, leaning upon his cane, and passing to him a copy of the Eclaireur de Nice of that morning. "Between you and me, this begins to look like a bloody business. 

Read that." 90

A BORROWED BODY

The news item was in French. Roughly translated, it read: 

"AMERICAN FOUND DEAD ON P.-L.-M. 

TRACKS 

"Cannes, Nov. 29.-The mysterious death of an

American is being investigated by the authorities of this city, following the discovery and identification of a body on the tracks of the Paris-Lyons-Mediterranée railway this evening, shortly after the passing of the Nice-Marseilles local train at 5.47 P. M. The body was found lying beside the roadbed three kilometers beyond the railway station, at a point where the line runs parallel to the sea-front through land which is being reclaimed and is not at present built upon." 

"The town dump-heap, to be exact," Craft interrupted at this moment. "If you've ever driven along there toward La Napoule, you'll know the placestretches of marshy reed-grown land, dotted with heaps of rusty tins and refuse. A foul place. No villas, nothing right along there except the railway tracks and the highway to La Napoule. But, Marshall, old man, this is the other side of Cannes, after you leave the station, not before you come into it, where you fell. However, read on." "The body was found by a railway employee, a brakeman inspecting a line of freight vans drawn up on a siding. By documents and a passport in the pockets, the body was identified as that of an American visitor, a Mr. Robinson Marshall, staying at the 

Grand Hôtel de la Reine de Grande Bretagne. Inquiries at his hotel disclosed the fact that Mr. Mar- 91

shall had left earlier in the afternoon to visit Nice, and had not been seen since. There was, among the papers found on the body, half of a return ticket between Nice and Cannes, indicating that he had intended to alight here. Why he did not do so, and how he came to meet his end are questions which the police are attempting to answer." 92

CHAPTER 9: Produces the Conducteur's Evidence 

LITTLE later on that morning of Friday, November 30, Lieutenant-Commander Craft and Marshall were shown, with flourishes and elaborate courtesy, into the office of the Examining Magistrate. The Magistrate, a majestic man with superb whiskers, who radiated an atmosphere of quiet power, shook hands with them ceremoniously, and introduced M. Bellecour, the Commissary of Police, who was seated near him. "Now, you have something, I understand, to tell us of the so-regrettable death of the American," he said. "I did not understand your names. Those first, if you please." "I am Lieutenant-Commander Craft, of the Villa

Lou Matagot. This gentleman is Mr. Robinson Marshall."

The Commissary and the Magistrate united in a raising of eyebrows and an opening of mouths with surprise. "But, monsieur!" "But, messieurs!" "I assure you I am," Marshall interposed. "There are several people in Cannes who can identify me. I would show you my passport in support of the statement, but you seem to have it in your possession." "This," said M. Bellecour, at last finding his 93

tongue, "is astonishing! Perhaps Monsieur knows, then, whose body we have found on the tracks, and have mistaken for his?" "No. No idea." Marshall shook his head. "But we came, M. le Commissaire, to tell you our story." "If you please," the Magistrate broke in, "let us not be confused. Let us consider the matter officially, with proper observance of formality. First, then, you, monsieur, claim, to be the man whose body we believed we had found. Commander Craft, what is your rôle in this little drama?" "Friend and advisor, M. le Juge." "I see. M. Marshall, your story, please."

Marshall told it in passable French. Of Elise and the Chèvre d'Or he said nothing, for reasons which seemed sufficient to him. He spoke only of the strange 

Frenchman in his compartment on the return from Nice, his few moments alone in the corridor, the unlocked door, and finally of the blow and his fall. As an afterthought he mentioned the intruder in his room-somewhat reluctantly, as if he felt that was his business and not that of the police, and only spoke of it out of courtesy. The two officials held their questions until he was through. "Now, monsieur," the Juge d'Instruction began, at last. "I must ask you at once whether you knew any one in Cannes who may have felt a grievance toward you.' "No one, I assure you. I know only Commander

Craft well. The others I know are merely acquaintances." "You have no theories, then, to explain this attack on you?" "None whatever. Commander Craft and I dis- 94

cussed the matter last night, before we knew of the finding of this body. We could not account for it. I had no money or valuables about me, except, perhaps, seventy dollars in traveler's checks, and a hundred francs or so. Besides, the man who struck me made no attempt to rob me. And to suppose that I have a secret enemy is quite absurd. Nothing so romantic, I'm afraid." "Mistaken identity, perhaps?" "It must be so, M. le Juge. And yet, I do not exactly look like many other people, do I?"

The Magistrate, after a general survey, admitted smilingly that he did not. He rang, meanwhile, for a sergent-de-ville, who put his head around the door, received rapid instructions, and popped out again. "You fell exactly where the Boulevard Alexandre 

III turns away from the Croisette?" the Juge continued. "The conducteur, who was on your car, is due here to be questioned in a short time. I would be delighted if you gentlemen could spare the time to hear his evidence, and permit him to identify you, if he is able. In the meantime. ." The sergentde-ville, after a bold knock upon the door, popped in again and placed a brown overcoat upon the desk. 

The Magistrate held it up. It was rather tattered. A long rip extended from collar to waist, a button had been torn off, and a pocket half ripped away. "Have you ever seen this overcoat before, M. Marshall?" "I should say from here that it is mine. Though 

I certainly didn't tear it that way." "Ah! And could you describe it, without examining it closer? Such as: Who is the maker? What was in its pockets?" 95

"I think I could do that. It was bought, readymade, at Finchley's in New York." "Correct. See, M. Bellecour, the label? Finchley. New York. And the pockets, M. Marshall?” "There were-let me see . . ." It is not easy to remember the items in your overcoat pockets, especially when, as in Marshall's case, you are in the habit of stuffing all manner of odds and ends into them. "I know that my passport was in the inside breast pocket. In the lower right-hand pocket-the one that's torn-I had a pair of tan leather gloves. 

And . . . I think that was all in that pocket. In the left, a package of Lucky Strike cigarettes and a little package of matches. And I may have had some loose change-three or four francs, no more. In the upper breast pocket I had my ticket." 

The Magistrate interrupted to ask, “You wore this coat yesterday to Nice?" "Oh, yes. I didn't wear it, I carried it. The afternoon was warm, but it becomes cold after dark." "Where was the coat when you were struck?" "I was carrying it over my arm. I don't remember what happened to it. I must have dropped it." "In the corridor?" "In the corridor. I did not see it when I recovered in the bushes." He suddenly remembered the scarf which he had found, and turned to the Magistrate to describe it. Craft coughed slightly and caught his eye. There was a distinct warning look in Craft's eye, as if he had read Marshall's thought. Well, this was Craft's day, Marshall decided, and said nothing about the scarf. "Where did you find the coat, may I ask?" 96

"The dead man was wearing it," said M. Bellecour, "when he was found." "Wearing it!" Craft sat up, blew out his cheeks, whistled, and subsided. "Who in thunder was the man?" Marshall demanded, of nobody in particular. "If Monsieur has told us all he knows, perhaps he will consent to take a look at the body?" Bellecour suggested. "Certainly. I'd be quite willing to. Craft, are you coming?" "Naturally."

The Magistrate rose and motioned them to follow him. Bellecour still sat in his chair, examining the brown coat, and poking his fingers reflectively through the rents and tears in it, very much like a corpulent and silent Mark Antony. The Magistrate, whose name was Porteus, led them down a stonetiled corridor to a dismal room lighted by two small and smudgy windows. There were two dark-stained tables of extraordinary length and height. One of them was bare; the other held the body, swathed in a blue cover. On a stand near by the clothing and objects found in the man's pockets had been neatly laid out in piles.

Porteus, with a gesture that he might have learned in a school of dramatic art, or that may have been no more than the normal expression of a character pleasantly dramatic in itself, withdrew the blue cover, exposing the body. Marshall had never considered himself squeamish, and he told himself, with scorn for his own timidity, that here lay nothing but a few pounds of idle flesh, an object neither for pity nor 97

for disgust. Yet he both pitied and felt a crawling disgust for the naked, battered body on the table. 

Its head had been injured; several nasty wounds had been torn in the scalp, and the face was lacerated by contact with rough-edged stones. The body was covered with bruises. "Do you know the man, monsieur?" Porteus demanded, watching Marshall's face for any sign of recognition. Marshall seemed more puzzled than anything else, and Porteus was compelled to wait in patience for a hesitant answer. "This M. le Juge, I hardly know what to say.

I have seen the man-somewhere. I don't know him, and yet, it seems to me I should know him. I saw him-wait, I know exactly where I saw him last! It was yesterday morning, in the Galleries Fleuries. I was there, calling on an art dealer on some private business. He came quickly around the corner from the Rue des États-Unis, and bumped into me. I had seen him before I can't remember where-but he seemed familiar. He apologized and hurried by. A moment later I saw him watching me from a distance as I entered the shop." "You have no recollection of him otherwise?" "No. I didn't think much about him yesterday. I meet a great many people, traveling as I do. I suppose I met him somewhere, as one does, and forgot him afterwards. Apparently he felt the same way yesterday, and stopped to look at me. I didn't think any more about it." "That is too bad! However, we shall publish a photograph in the Eclaireur and other papers, retouching it, of course, to hide the wounds. Perhaps some one will recognize it and come forward to iden- 98

tify the man. Still, I wish you could remember, monsieur, where you had seen him." "I'll try, monsieur. My own curiosity is very much aroused. How did he die? Concussion from a fall?” "I think not." Porteus became properly solemn. "The medical evidence is that he was probably dead when he fell. Mr. Marshall, look at his throat! 

Those marks; you see them?" He pointed them out with ghoulish relish. "I see them," Marshall murmured, as if he rather regretted it. "Monsieur, he was strangled to death! He wasand I say it deliberately-he was murdered."

They returned, in thoughtful silence, to the Magistrate's office, where Bellecour, with grave dignity, was offering a cigar to a stocky Provençal who was waiting for it with undisguised eagerness. "Ah, here is the Juge d'Instruction," Bellecour announced. "Perhaps you had better save it to smoke later."

The man put the cigar sadly in his pocket and eyed the Juge with dawning distaste. Porteus inspected him out of one eye, the other closed judicially. "Who is this?" he asked. "The conducteur?" "I am, M. le Juge. My name is Biondetti." "Very good. There are a few questions we should like to ask you. If you will seat yourself there . 

Craft and Marshall, as spectators, occupied a hard wooden bench near the door. Porteus and Bellecour sat, one on either side of Biondetti as if he were a dangerous character who might made a sudden break for liberty. The conducteur sensed the impression, and promptly lost a little of his assurance. 99

"Now, M. Biondetti. What is your name in full, and your address?"

These details settled, the conducteur was invited to tell his story in his own words, which he proceeded to do as well as he could, in spite of the constant interruptions of the Commissary or the Magistrate. 

They closely pressed him for information regarding his duties, his routes and trips between Marseilles and Nice, and a dozen other matters which, as far as Marshall could see, were quite immaterial. Yet it was obvious that Porteus and the Commissary were determined to see the case cleared up, and intended that no neglect on their part should be an excuse for failure. Biondetti eventually brought the story down to the events of the journey between Golfe Juan and Cannes. "I was sitting then," he said, "in the last compartment of the coach, which was otherwise empty, and was checking over my reports." 

It appeared, in answer to. Porteus's prompt inquiry, that these reports had nothing whatever to do with the taking of tickets. On that train tickets were not examined by the guard. You had your ticket punched when you entered through the gates to the platform, and you had no more to do with it until you descended at your destination, where it was collected by an official as you passed out of the gates. 

Just what reports Biondetti was checking did not develop. Marshall strongly suspected that they were racing charts; there were races of some sort or another in progress at Nice, and Biondetti appeared to be the sort who would be interested in them. "Continue, if you please," Porteus begged. 100

The guard, thrown off his course by these latest interruptions, was forced to repeat himself. "I was sitting, messieurs, in the last compartment as we passed the Cap de la Croisette. Then it is my duty to open the doors so that passengers may descend. I stepped out of my compartment, and started to walk the length of the corridor. At the other end I could see a man standing by the door already waiting to alight." "Could you describe this man?" "No, monsieur. The corridor is dimly lighted, and I had no time to observe him. He was tall; that is all I can say." "Would you recognize him again if you saw him?” "I don't know, monsieur. I might. Or I might not. Under the identical conditions, perhaps . . ." "The man is sitting in this room," Porteus announced, to Biondetti's confusion. The guard fidgeted in his chair, and stared at Marshall and Craft with intentness, uncertain which of them to choose. At last he decided to rely upon discretion. "It was too dark, monsieur. It may have been either of these gentlemen." "Very good, Biondetti!" Porteus beamed at him with delight, and stroked his beard. "You do not try to fabricate to make your story more entertaining. 

You are a good witness. I compliment you. Pray proceed."

Biondetti proceeded. 

"This man was swaying there by the door. Even when I first saw him he was staggering forward..." "Wait!" The Commissary raised his hand and barked. "Was this man alone? That is important, Biondetti."

ΙΟΙ

Biondetti, who had begun to take pride in his narrative efforts, shrugged his shoulders expressively. "You know our trains, monsieur. There is a long corridor on one side, and a platform extending from side to side at each end. A second-no, even a third and fourth-man might stand behind this gentleman at the door on that platform, and be hidden from me in the corridor. I did not see any one, but I cannot possibly say that there was no one. Vous comprenez?" "Quite. Tchk, tchk! Too bad. Then?" "This man I saw: he staggered forward and fell against the door. What horror I felt, monsieur, when

Tchk, tchk, and Porteus, narrowly escaping a total destruction of the effect, turned to Marshall and winked. "What did you do then?" Bellecour prompted. "Ah, but I was distracted! I hardly knew what I was doing. That door: it should never have opened like that." "Was it locked?" "Monsieur, it should have been," said Biondetti, uncompromisingly. "When was it last opened, to your knowledge?" "At Golfe Juan, a few minutes before." "Did you close it when the train left Golfe Juan?" "Before we left. The doors must be shut before the train is permitted to depart." "And did you lock it tight?"

Biondetti took his time before he replied. He per- 102

ceived that, in a moment or two, he might be in very deep water indeed, and he was reluctant to plunge. "Well, monsieur, it is just possible, just possible, I say, that the latch might not have caught." 

Porteus interrupted blandly. "The door," he said, "is locked with a key-a huge key that the guard carries with him. The key cannot be disengaged from the lock unless the bolt has been shot the full way, or not shot at all. And it is quite impossible to unlock that door without a key. I know that much. Now, Biondetti, be exact, please. Did you lock that door at Golfe Juan, or did you not?" "Monsieur, I assure you . . . " "Yes? Of what?" "Monsieur, I assure you. "That the door was not locked?" "Sometimes," said Biondetti, swallowing desperately, "we are, what you say, careless-not negligent, monsieur; no, never negligent. But perhaps just a trifle careless. On a local there are many stops. 

Every moment one must lock and unlock doors. One does not always remember all doors. In fact-" And after further mumblings and murmurings he confessed, to his everlasting shame, that, as a convenience to travelers who wished to mount or dismount before he could release them, he never locked the doors on the local run, though he was quite aware that such negligence was against the rules. "We understand that now. So it happened that this man you saw fell through that so carelessly open door and escaped death by a hair's breadth." Biondetti hung his head penitently, hoping that so sincere 103

and obvious a regret might, to some extent, mitigate his offense. "Now, what did you do?" "I thought. "Not, 'what did you think?' What did you do?" the Commissary corrected him. "I pulled the alarm, monsieur, and stopped the train at once." "Where is this alarm?" "In the corridor, almost beside the door." "Was there any one on that cross platform when you reached the door?" "I saw no one." "Biondetti,” Porteus interposed, "we believe that the gentleman, Mr. Marshall, did not fall of himself, but was assisted. In other words, he was pushed. 

Some one pushed him. Tell us, if some one had done that, could this person have escaped before you pulled the alarm?” "Yes, monsieur. Such a person had only to step into the washroom, or pass into the forward coach.

I would not have seen him then. I did not look into the washroom until we reached Marseilles, and I did not go into the next coach soon enough to catch any one. I rang the alarm and the train stopped. We had passed the trestle over the Boulevard Alexandre III. It was just before then that this gentleman must have fallen. "I took a lantern, and with another guard, went back along the line, searching." "How thorough a search did you make?" "Only brief, monsieur. We were at the edge of 

Cannes. The train was already a few minutes late. 

When we found no one, we went on, and at Cannes 

I gave out word to send up a searching party." 104

Biondetti did not think so, knowing guards and their ways of passing time, but he did not dare say so.

CHAPTER 10:

Second Rehearsal T TWO-THIRTY Craft arrived at the Col lins's villa alone, his plan of campaign already partially mapped out. Mrs. Collins, a little distracted, as much as to say that she was torn hither and thither by a thousand duties and responsibilities, greeted him, as she usually did, at the front door. "You've probably heard the terrible news already," she buzzed away, passing her hand wearily over her hair in a gesture worthy of Bernhardt. "About poor Mr. Marshall. We are overcome, all us, Commander Craft. It's really too tragic!" of "Mr. Marshall? Oh, the account of his death in the paper. I see." Craft hobbled into the vestibule and divested himself of his overcoat with Dukane's assistance. "Was that the Mr. Marshall who was here the other day? Sylvester?" "Sylvester! Think of it, Commander Craft! 

Fallen off of a railway! I don't know," she declared, coming to the problem uppermost in her mind, "what we shall do for a leading man now. Oh, well." Here she threw off hundreds of cares and tried to be her own bright self. "Oh, well, we must carry on, mustn't we? And perhaps, if the King does not recover, we shan't be able to give the play, anyway."

It was plain that Dukane Collins loved troubles and annoyances. They comforted her very much as 109

tranquillity comforts others; by overcoming difficulties, she achieved a glowing sense of power, and by encountering annoyances she developed a warm belief in her own importance. In these moments she burned with a light of her own, which put to shame the reflected glory of her husband's fame.

She led Craft into the living-room, set as before in some semblance of the stage plan. The Temples and the Bassington-Cowans had already come, and sat uncomfortably on chairs they were afraid to move for fear of destroying an elaborate arrangement. Mrs.

Bassington-Cowan, of them all, seemed most to relish the diversion of a tragedy. She had hovered for years on the verge of a stupendous psychic revelation and she felt vaguely that her time might have come; she envisioned herself receiving, in some mesmeric trance, perhaps, a message from Beyond, signed

Robinson Marshall, containing an exclusive interview. Her conviction radiated from her. Colonel 

Bassington-Cowan was less concerned. An additional wrinkling of his wrinkly forehead seemed to him a sufficient display of emotion. One did not weep in public over the loss of an acquaintance, you know.

Of them all, Mrs. Temple was perhaps the most serene. Robinson Marshall to her had been little more than a name. His absence from her presence was, as far as she was concerned, equivalent to his absence from the world; she could grieve over neither. A nice boy, possibly. On acquaintance, he might have been very pleasant indeed. As it was, she could hardly feel other than a mild regret, such as she might feel reading of a massacre in Somaliland or Beluchistan. Temple himself, as usual, betrayed no 

IIQ

more than an unreadable expression, a mingling of vapidness, slyness, mischievousness, and varnish.

Their babbling, exaggerated by their distance from one another, broke when Craft appeared. Mrs. Bassington-Cowan was the first to say more than a brief hello. She repeated, in a slightly awed voice, Mrs. 

Collins's opening remarks. "You've probably heard the news by now, haven't you? Isn't it too tragic? Too tragic for words? Such a dear young man!"

Lloyd Collins, so mild and so timid that his presence was hardly noticeable, murmured a pathetic, "I liked him," and said no more, hovering in the background and missing nothing, but apparently too timid to state any of his own opinions. "He was on the train with all of us, wasn't he?” 

Craft asked, choosing the most comfortable unoccupied chair and sinking into it. "I don't remember seeing him." "Don't you remember?" Mrs. Bassington-Cowan piped up, "he was sitting in the end compartment. I saw him there once, when we were getting aboard.” "Ah, was he? I wonder what happened?" "I can't imagine," murmured Colonel Bassington Cowan, in the tone of one who has not given much thought to it. "Fell, probably. Those doors are forever being left unlatched." "You think that's what it was?" Mrs. Collins inquired. "He didn't get off at Cannes, did he? I don't remember seeing him. I wonder where he was going, to fall off beyond the station."

If she had expected a reply, it was interrupted by the arrival of Colonel Poplin, who unwrapped a precious throat from two layers of scarves, and lumbered III

into the room, monocle in eye, staring with a fatuous grin at the assemblage as if he had never seen any of them before. Porey, intruding his arrival in any place, even when he was expected, had the air of an intrusion-at this moment, nodded to every one, and sat down next to Mrs. Temple, whose poise he had always admired. 

She was graciously unenthusiastic in her greeting, inclined to be distant. But Porey, in self-defense, had long been impervious to coolness.

Mrs. Collins, perceiving at last that every one who could be expected had arrived, left Elise and advanced, like a lecturer, to a position in front of them. "You have all heard of our loss," she began briskly. "It has been a terrible shock, and we are all terribly, terribly upset about it, aren't we? I've almost thought that it might be better to call off the play altogether, in respect to Mr. Marshall's memory."

Colonel Poplin broke out in violent protest. "Oh, I say! I say!" "Just a minute, Colonel Poplin. Let me finish.

I've almost thought that. But of course, we do this for Charity. Our motives are entirely charitable, aren't they? And it would not be quite fair to the needy to deprive them of our assistance. So❞

"Hear! Hear!" said Poplin, clapping his hands. "So of course, we shall go on. But we need a new leading man."

Poplin was nodding emphatically. Possibly he was considering with pleasure his own eligibility for the part. Commander Craft, in a quiet voice, proceeded to prick that balloon. "Mrs. Collins, may I make a suggestion?" "Please do, Commander Craft." "If we could wait perhaps a day or two. . . . Tomorrow is only the first of December, and we don't give the play until February. There is still ample time. I know a young chap-a splendid young fellow who could do young Marshall's part every bit as well as Marshall himself. I can vouch for him personally." "That sounds interesting," Mrs. Collins admitted. "When could he be here?" "I could have him here for Monday's rehearsal." "Splendid! That's only one day we'd miss. And until then, Major Porey, you can read the part, can't you? You are never on with Sylvester, are you? 

Then that is arranged. Now, shall we begin?"

The rehearsal began and straggled through to a weak finish. Porey was not a young hero. He was, morever, not a very good actor, even as amateurs go, and he was inclined to exercise the playwright's supposed privilege of criticising a fellow writer's work. 

Even Mrs. Collins's distraction was no longer assumed when, eventually, an imaginary curtain descended upon mumblings that were supposed to represent that fatal last line that must, under no circumstances, be uttered until the opening night. Mrs. 

Collins never quite understood the reasons for the 113

custom, but she was too much a daughter of the stage to question such high and holy mysteries. 

They gathered for tea afterwards in another room of the villa. Mrs. Collins poured, and Mr. Collins timidly made the rounds with a tin of Peak and Frean's biscuits. Chatter became general. It was not necessary for Craft to lead the conversation; one could speak of nothing but Marshall's supposed death. "Did any one see him on the train?" Mrs. Bassington-Cowan inquired, dipping her biscuit into her tea, and sucking it. "I did, when we were getting aboard." 

Elise hesitated on the brink of a confession. "I sat with him for awhile," she said, finally. "Isn't it just awful? To talk to him, just as I'm talking to you! And then, not ten minutes later. . . !"

Every one was very sympathetic. You couldn't help yourself when Elise looked distressed, and almost ready for tears. "A fine chap," Craft remarked in a firm tone designed to dam the flood. "He didn't mention to you that he was expecting to ride beyond Cannes, did he?" "We didn't speak of that at all. We talked about -oh, what one usually talks about. This and that. 

The weather. The King's illness." "Were you with him until you reached Cannes?" "Oh, no. Only as far as Golfe Juan. Then I went back to my compartment.' "Do you remember," said Craft, to the room in general, "when the train stopped on the embankment? What happened then, I wonder? Do you suppose that stop had anything to do with the accident?" 114

"I hardly think so," Temple expressed as his opinion. "Don't see any reason to connect the two at all." "No. I dare say you're right." Craft drank his tea, and placed his cup on a table beside his chair. 

Mrs. Collins promptly rescued it and refilled it. "Still, when two odd things happen together, one becomes curious. I wonder what did cause the stop?" "I thought, possibly, a cow. " Mrs. Bassington-Cowan suggested, only to be instantly silenced by an impatient grunt from her husband. "I told you, Betty, a dozen times, there are no cows loose in Cannes. They can't climb trestles, either. They can't do it." "Did any one look out and see anything?" Craft prompted, lest the discussion concern itself with the climbing propensities of cows. "I did, of course," Mrs. Bassington-Cowan replied at once. "I know. I was in the compartment with you. Did you, Poplin? Or you, Temple?" "Not I!" Colonel Poplin responded, his mouth full of biscuit. "I must have been asleep. Drowsy, you know, from the motion of the train." "I was wandering in the corridor just before then," 

Temple said. "When the train stopped, however, I was back in the compartment. I think it was only another train ahead. Nothing there, Craft. No more excitement to be stirred up, I'm afraid.” 

Mrs. Collins, her duties for the moment suspended, said suddenly: "Where were you, Lloyd? You weren't in the compartment with us then, were you?" "I was. You seem to forget. I was dozing, too," 115

"Oh, so you were. But wasn't that before we reached Golfe Juan, instead of before we reached Cannes?" "I couldn't say, dear. I didn't pay any attention." "No. You never do. I often say," Mrs. Collins announced, "that Lloyd would be completely bewildered if he went out alone. Like a little child, aren't you?" 

Mr. Collins smiled appreciatively. "If you say so, my dear." "He is. Really he is. Major Porey, what have you to offer to the discussion?" "Nothing, Mrs. Collins. I wasn't even in the same car." "No," said Elise. "He and I were together in another compartment. I can't imagine how we became separated from you." "It was odd," murmured Mrs. Collins, without conviction. If Elise had got into another car with 

Marshall, say, she might have understood it perfectly. But Porey-well, one woman's poison was another woman's sauce, or whatever the saying was. "But you left him for a while, didn't you?" "I did, yes. Mr. Marshall had some business to talk over with me." "Business?" Porey set aside his cup and looked interested. "The villa, Miss Valery? He told me you were thinking of buying it-the Chèvre d'Or."

board the train, as he certainly seems to have done, it seems to me somebody would have noticed him.

Every one but Major Porey and I had to pass him. 

Didn't any one see him?" "Dear me, no!" Mrs. Collins declared. "I didn't pay the slightest bit of attention. I'd supposed he'd got off ahead of me, if I supposed anything. And I hurried away as soon as I reached the gates, and came directly home. I had dinner guests and I had to see to the table. I didn't even wait for Lloyd. He had to stop at the Sporting Theater on his way, you know, to arrange for their stage for the dates we've set." "Funny how one does overlook a person," Craft observed. "I didn't see him, either. If I were asked to swear one way or another, I'd say he wasn't in that compartment when I passed. But I can't be certain. The only person I really saw was Miss Valery.

I was a bit late in getting off, and she was lagging behind." "I didn't see Marshall, but I saw you, Craft," 

Temple declared. "You had that guard chap handing you down, didn't you? Rotten to have a bad knee. I saw Poplin, too. Thought for a moment he was going my way, and I started to hail him." That's not at all bad. There wasn't much time before dinner." "No, I wasn't going to the casino." Poplin chuckled heavily. "I was to meet some one at the 

Lido for a cocktail." "Who? Do tell us!" Mrs. Collins begged archly. "I scent a scandal, Colonel Poplin!" 117

"My dear," beamed Poplin, to whom the idea of being the center of a scandal was rather flattering, "it was no one you know. A charming lady. Really charming. She left this morning for Egypt. We drank three cocktails to celebrate, and had a jolly good time." 

Elise appeared impatient at Poplin's digression.

In a puzzled tone, she said again, "Didn't any one see Mr. Marshall? You know, I think that's awfully peculiar! He must have been there!" "I didn't see him," Colonel Bassington-Cowan remembered. "Did you, Betty? Betty asked me particularly to find him and ask him to tea one of these days. I saw practically every one but Marshall. 

Odd, that." "Do you suppose," Elise persisted, "that he got off at the Cannes station, and then, for some reason, got on again?" 

Mrs. Bassington-Cowan squashed that idea at once. "Whatever he did, I'm sure I'd have seen him. I looked everywhere before Seaford and I left. Our car was parked in that little street just opposite the station, and we even waited there a bit before we drove back to Le Cannet." "No. I immediately turned down toward the lower exit, instead of the one where the rest of you left.

My sister was waiting for me in my car outside. I saw none of you at all. Why do you ask so particularly, Miss Valery? Is something worrying you?" "No. Only. . . . No, Major Porey. I was curi- 118

ous, that's all. Out of all of us, you would think one of us would have seen him." 

Craft laughed. "That shows how observant we are. We can stare at people and yet not see them. For example, Mrs.

Frenchman, in a very loud checked suit . . 

"Oh, come, Mrs. Collins! I say, that will never do!" Poplin chuckled. "I remember him distinctly, and he was wearing a black suit and a checked overcoat." "No, he wasn't. I remember particularly, because

"I don't believe so." This was one game, Porey's tone seemed to say, that he did not enjoy playing. "Perhaps there was. An Englishman, I think. Can't say more than that.” 119

"What have you to add, Miss Valery?"

CHAPTER 11: Conducts the Reader to the Sporting Club 

RAFT, instead of returning directly home after the rehearsal, drove into the narrow, cluttered Rue des Belges, and found a parking space. It was dusk, and the street was already lighted by dim street lamps and the open doors of a pub or two. The Sporting Club entrance blazed with light. Under its marquise, billboards announced a week's performances, at the Sporting Theater, of that charming perennial musical comedy, Rose- 

Marie, with a distinguished French cast. A yawning young Frenchman, in a chocolate-colored suit and a violet tie, was brushing his hair behind the grille of the booking office. Near him another booking office, beside a separate door, dispensed tickets to the club baccarat rooms. 

Craft limped past these tempters into a plushy foyer and followed a long, faintly lighted corridor to a suddenly descending flight of steps. This was the entrance to the Lido, a small and very popular tea and night club. At present, he found, when he had negotiated the steps, its smoky room was fairly crowded. Prismatic lights from a hidden source shone upon the murals, a series of gondolas silhouetted against a golden canal and a golden sky. 

Pink-shaded lamps burned in the stalls around the room. A dozen or more couples were dancing to " 123

Want to Be Loved by You," played with a curiously un-American rhythm by the Lido Club Jazz Babes. 

He hailed a waiter passing the steps with an empty tray. "Monsieur wishes a table?" "No. A friend of mine took tea here yesterday, and lost his cigarette case. I've stopped in to ask for it."

It was a feeble but plausible excuse, and Craft was rather proud of it. The waiter, with a sudden feeling of distaste for such a business, shrugged, and suggested a visit to the manager. He, personally, knew nothing of cigarette cases, and besides, Monsieur could see for himself that that couple in the corner was simply clamoring for their tea, and would not be silenced until they got it. The waiter hurried away. 

Craft waited patiently by the steps. Presently the head waiter approached. Craft explained his desires. "Perhaps you can describe the case," the head waiter suggested. "Yes. An oblong of white gold with bias stripes of black enamel." "I shall inquire." "I'd appreciate it if you would. Perhaps you'll remember the gentleman. He is an Englishman, rather heavily built, and dances awkwardly. He has thin reddish hair, and a round heavy, reddish face. 

Wears a monocle." "Was he here alone?" "With a lady, not of my acquaintance," Craft said. 

The head waiter departed. He was absent for several moments. Then Craft saw him speaking to several waiters, each of whom shook his head in turn. The head waiter returned, apologetic. 124

THE SPORTING CLUB 

"I'm sorry, monsieur. I can't seem to find any one who saw such a case. They do not remember the gentleman, your friend, either. You are sure he was here, and that it was here that he lost his case?" "No, to be honest, I'm not at all sure. I inquired just for luck. You say no one remembers seeing him here?" "I'm sorry, monsieur. But you see we are very crowded in the afternoons, and it is impossible to remember every one who comes in." "I understand." "Now, if you could describe the lady "Unfortunately I have not had the pleasure of meeting her."

It was darkened, and on the stage an undress rehearsal of portions of Rose-Marie was in progress. No one paid any attention to him as he found his way, in the semi-darkness, to a flight of steps leading up to the manager's offices and the balcony. 

At the office of M. Demuth, the stage director, he knocked. When there was no reply, he pushed open the door and came into a small room furnished with a few cane-bottomed chairs, and decorated with a collection of old programs and posters. Mr. Collins here yesterday? I was thinking of asking him to join me for tea at the Lido, and if I'd known he was with you, I'd have done so." "I don't know when he came." M. Demuth shook his head. "You know how timid Mr. Collins is. You and I know! Timid as a rabbit, ha, ha! He came—

Craft returned to his car, and drove toward the casino and the waterfront. When he had parked by the Croisette, he limped along under a ragged canopy of palms, pushing against a cool wind which had risen and was blowing across the harbor. A half-dozen late homegoers passed him, ignored him while their thoughts dwelt upon prospective banquets of garlic. 

Before the entrance to the casino, a fair-sized crowd was strolling, or examining the bright green and mulberry billboards announcing the evening's opera. Gounod's Sappho was to be sung, with a prima donna whose jaw, Craft recalled, often vibrated with passion. He remembered hearing

Sappho a week or two before; one of the priests, on that memorable occasion, had trod the boards with a most marvelous beard, but without his mustache. 127

But you might trust the French to meet an emergency, he reflected; the priest, with a sang-froid which Craft envied whole-heartedly, had surreptitiously torn off the end of his beard and had pressed it to his upper lip, wrinkling his nose to hold it in place. There was only one disadvantage in the subterfuge. The priest was unable to sing another note for fear of dislodging it. The spectacle, for Craft, had quite overshadowed every other detail of 

Gounod's Sappho.

He presented his card at the entrance, and moved through crowded halls toward the baccarat room. 

The place this evening seemed more than ever to be a gilt and crystal aviary stuffed with very noisy and very brightly colored parrots. A polyglot multitude lounged and pushed by mirrors and glass doors and rows of showcases displaying the most startling creations of Cartier and his fellow guildsmen. The Salle des Ambassadeurs, closing after the tea hour, was disgorging groups who were crowding into the gambling rooms for a brief fling before dinner. 

Craft paused just outside the doorway, trying to clear his head in the confusion. There were several people he knew near by. One or two of them nodded or spoke. CHAPTER 12: Considers the Evidence 

C RAFT returned to his villa a little later in a thoughtful mood. Marshall, who had been kept in involuntary idleness during the entire afternoon, not permitted to show so much as his nose beyond the villa walls, was exploding with impatience to discover what success Craft's tactics would have had. He hardly gave him time enough to struggle out of his overcoat before he fired a volley of confused questions. Craft grinned at him in silence.

A few moments later Craft settled himself on the sofa, and Marshall resumed his favorite perch on the raised hearthstone-the lip of Moloch, Craft called it-ready to listen. "The afternoon, my boy, is something of a success," Craft reported. "The entire cast are frightfully cut up about your supposed decease"-a harmless exaggeration, Craft considered, designed to mollify Marshall's tempestuous mood-"especially, it seemed to me, Miss Valery." "Look here, Craft, must you go on letting them hang mental crape on me? Is that fair?" "It seems to me not only fair but imperative. Whoever murdered the man who was identified as you, unless it was a case of mistaken identity on the part of the murderer, must know that you were not killed.

Your body would have been found when the search- 131

ing party beat through the underbrush at the foot of the embankment. A child would know that. I've no doubt he's consumed with curiosity to know what's up. When you are produced eventually, sound and well-and I intend to make your sudden appearance something of a sensation-he's bound to give himself away." "I know; it sounds all right, Craft, in theory. But will it work? Won't the shock be-well, rather shocking to Miss Valery and the others?" "You incurable duffer! Naturally it will, if I can stage-manage it properly. That's the whole point." "I don't like it, Craft. It doesn't seem altogether fair. Oh, well, I came to you and put myself into your hands. Go ahead: do what you like. I like you, Craft, and, believe me, I trust you. That's that." "Good man! Now, does a cocktail sound enticing, while we consider to-day's campaign?"

It did. Eventually it was served. "We might as well tabulate our findings," Craft said, locating a pencil and a notebook in his pocket. "I had to do a bit of delicate probing to learn anything at all. They're the most reticent group of people I've ever seen. Except Mrs. Collins. Always excepting Mrs. Collins, who, given a chance, could chatter indefinitely. She was all for giving up the play entirely in honor of your memory, Marshall.

Or pretended she was; it would have broken her heart if we'd agreed." "I'm flattered," said Marshall, without conscious sarcasm. "What did you find out? How was Miss Valery?" "Very worried. In semi-mourning, by the way." 

Marshall writhed. 132

"The poor kid! Couldn't we-Craft, I don't want to raise more objections, but couldn't we let her in on the fact that I'm still kicking?"

"Quite right. I'm surprised, though, Marshall, that we have anything as definite as we have. It was fortunate for us that the train stopped when it did. 

It gives us a starting point. People recall it. If it had not happened, I doubt whether we'd be able to place any one. They were all wandering back and forth, from compartment to compartment. Be thankful, Marshall, for small blessings. Now, here's the second list, and we shall have to consider it in connection with the first." (See Table II.) "That list is hardly more definite than the other," Marshall objected, when he had seen it. "I know. And yet there are points in it that can be checked up. Let's take these people one at a time, now, and study their cases a little more carefully. 

We needn't bother with myself. I put my name in merely for the sake of completeness. First, then, comes Bassington-Cowan. We see by our charts that he was missing from his compartment at the moment of the attack on you, and that he was not seen in the corridor a moment later. That means, of course, that he could have attacked you and hidden in the washroom until the train stopped and the conducteur was out of the way, and then strolled back. I'm sorry that, just now, we can't probe the matter any further. But we can't, without cutting up an unholy amount of unpleasantness. We'll have to limit ourselves to possibilities, and work from them. "Coming to the second list-while Bassington Cowan claims, in general terms, to have seen 'nearly every one,' he himself is seen only by his wife. There doesn't seem to be much doubt that the two of them drove home in their car. But it is just possible that 

Mrs. Bassington-Cowan is lying, for some reason. 135
