THE EVIDENCE 

Personally, I don't think she is. I've heard her give excuses for not accepting an invitation, and she does not lie convincingly. However, that's only an opinion. The point is, had Bassington-Cowan any conceivable motive for attacking you? His motive for killing our stranger, of course, we can't guess until we discover the man's identity. And we mustn't lose sight of the fact, Marshall, that the attack upon you and the later murder may not have been the work of the same man. There is nothing to connect them except a partial similarity of technique, and the really surprising fact that the dead man was wearing your coat. They probably are connected somehow. But don't forget that, because a person we may suspect could not have done one, he is not automatically eliminated from having done the other." "I see what you mean. Lord, it sounds a complicated business, Craft! Do you see any light?" "Not yet. I'm groping, that's all. How about 

Bassington-Cowan's motive?" "None, as far as I'm concerned, that I know of. 

I've met him once or twice before-at your London flat, if you remember." "I do. Is that all?” "Absolutely." "Very well. We'll check him off with a question mark. Now, there's Colonel Poplin." 

Marshall laughed. "I think," he said, "he'd have to be a good bit sprier before he could toss bodies off trains." "Don't underestimate Poplin," Craft cautioned. "He's an ox when it comes to clumsiness. And, if you've ever felt his muscles, you'll know he's ox-like strength, too. Not what he used to be when he was 139

younger, perhaps. But good enough. However, that's beside the point. Our lists show that he was probably asleep in his compartment under Mrs. Collins's watchful eye when you were struck. Allowing even for a possible inaccuracy in her statement-the two of them couldn't agree on the appearance of some chap who sat opposite them-that is probably correct. 

Poplin always sleeps when there is an excuse. As for his later movements, it's too bad the lady he took tea with has sailed beyond our reach. I inquired at the

Lido on the way home, pretending he had lost something, but none of the waiters remembered him. I could not describe his companion either, which was unfortunate." "Isn't the Lido in the Sporting Club building?" 

Marshall interrupted. "Yes. Why?" "Isn't that where Collins went, too? Don't you think it's odd they didn't meet?” "Not very, if one of them set out a moment sooner than the other. There are two or three routes you can follow from the station to the Sporting Club, all of about the same distance. And Poplin must have gone in the main entrance, on the Rue des Belges, to reach the Lido. There is a stage entrance around the corner on the Rue d'Antibes that would be quite as convenient for Collins." "I see. Didn't mean to interrupt. Go on." "Motive, in Poplin's case, is obscure, until we discover our victim's identity. So we must check his name, too, with a question mark. Now, there's Temple. But if you don't mind, I'll come to Temple last of all. There are curious circumstances in connection with him. Shall we consider Collins next?" 140

THE EVIDENCE 

"Collins? I wouldn't bother with him,” Marshall opined. "He's too mild to have anything to do with such a gory business as this." "Agreed. Collins is the gentle lamb, the tender blossom. I don't mean that disparagingly, either.

Collins is a fine chap, but he could as well massacre an army single-handed as push you off that train, or murder a man in cold blood. That is my personal belief, at any rate. I may be quite wrong. I am, so often. But, just to touch upon him in passing: He wasn't in his compartment at the crucial moment, and we don't know where he was. I'll ask him myself, of course, next time I see him. As for his appointment at the Sporting: when I was there I inquired into that, too. The train arrived in Cannes a minute or two after 5.47-it was a bit late, you recall the conducteur saying. Well, what time Collins arrived at the Sporting Theater no one knows, but M. Demuth, the manager, insists that he saw him at about quarter past six. He was busy, in some sort of conference, and was not disturbed until then. When he came out of his conference room, he saw Collins waiting meekly in the antechamber. Twenty-five minutes. Say, twenty, allowing five minutes to walk from the station to the stage door in the Rue d'Antibes. Motive? None, that I can see. That finishes 

Collins, and though he has no alibi for the moment of your attack, I don't think that we need consider him any further. "Mrs. Collins, of course, had neither strength nor opportunity to do anything so strenuous as knocking a sizeable fellow like you on the head and pushing his body off that train, without assistance. We'll drop her without considering her further. Now, there's 141

Porey. I know you distrust him, Marshall, and it may be that he has some reason to feel that you and Miss Valery are unduly interested in that villa at the same time that he is. But I can't see that that gives him a motive for attempting murder. If it's the villa, killing you would still leave Miss Valery in the field. 

Eliminating you would hardly improve the situation.

I can't think of any other motive just at the moment. 

But we mustn't forget that he may have one. Now, we know that he might have attacked you, as far as that goes. He could have slipped in from his car to do it. And nobody but his sister can say whether or not he got off the train at Cannes. The unsupported evidence of an interested party, and not worth much. 

So for the time being Porey must be one of the possibles. "Miss Valery we won't consider. While she may have had opportunity to strike you, if not to murder our stranger, there's the delicate question of motive.” "I know you're considering every possible person, Craft," Marshall protested. "And that's all right. 

But Miss Valery-don't be an ass! She no more would have struck me than I would strike her. And that's that." "Right-o! That is that. And that brings us finally to our friend Terrence Temple. Here is the sum of our information concerning him. He had, according to his own statements, and those of others, been out in the corridor before the train reached Cannes. 

He says he was in the compartment at the moment the train stopped. If that is true, it naturally eliminates him at once. He could not possibly have shoved you, and then returned to his compartment, without passing the guard, who stood by the door after he 142

THE EVIDENCE 

pulled the alarm until the train came to a standstill. 

The guard did not see him. But there is the possibility that Temple was not in the compartment at the minute he said. His statement is absolutely unsupported. No one denied it when he made it publicly, I grant you. On the other hand, no one agreed with it, and the only person in that compartment capable of supporting it was Mrs. Collins, whose testimony, you know, is honest as the day is long, but absolutely unreliable. The woman has eyes that seem to see nothing whatever. Poplin, and possibly Collins if he was there, as he says, were both dozing. "For the later period at the station, no one-unless Bassington-Cowan might have-saw him, though he saw both Poplin and myself. He went to the casino, he says. Well, let's see: I stopped at the casino myself, after I'd been to the Sporting Club, and made a few inquiries. There's no doubt that 

Temple had been there. Several people we both know saw him after six. He had won, as he said, a thousand francs or so. But again there's a long stretch of time unaccounted for, from 5.47 or a bit later to 

6.15. Now bear that in mind. "Next we have a rather startling bit of evidence against him. Marshall, that silk scarf belonged to him. He made no attempt to identify it when I held it up. But Mrs. Temple did identify it immediately. 

I don't know what funny work there was between them, but quite suddenly, after he'd given her a look that would kill, she remembered that she had had the scarf with her lately and had lost it somewhere. 

She wasn't on that train yesterday, and every one knows it. So, if she's telling the truth, how did it get there? And if the story is as tall as it sounds, did 143

Temple deny having the scarf with him, because he was afraid of the consequences if he didn't? In other words, did Temple strike you with a heavy object wrapped in his own scarf?" "But, good heavens, man, why? What had Temple against me?” "There's our pretty little problem. Had you ever met him before?" "No. Never. Never even heard of him before." "Don't know anything about him?” "Not a thing."

Craft paused, and watched, the flames roaring within his domestic Moloch. "I say, Marshall, I don't want you to think me a scandal hound. I'm telling you what I am, simply because it may help us to clear up a messy situation. 

Temple's reputation is not exactly lily-like. Out in 

India a few years ago I forget where; I only remember that it was before he married the present Mrs. Temple he got into trouble. The usual story -a woman, wife of one of the officers at his station. 

The business came to light somehow, and Temple did the only possible thing. He resigned his commission at once. But the night after, before Temple set out for the coast and England, some one fired a shot at the woman's husband, and put him out of commission for a while. There was no proof that Temple had been responsible for it. He had an alibi, of sorts, and because of the lady's reputation, and because the man was not permanently incapacitated, the matter was hushed up rather quickly. Temple's tried to live the business down, and I admire his pluck. I'd like," 

Craft concluded regretfully, "to admire his good sense, too. But I can't say that I can." 144

THE EVIDENCE 

He shrugged. "But there you are. That's Temple." "I see. I rather thought, myself, that he had a streak in him-call it a nasty temper, or impulsiveness, or what you will. But see here, Craft, even if all this is true, what does it amount to?" "A vague possibility, Marshall. Nothing more. 

From what we know or assume, it is possible that Temple, for some reason unknown, and perhaps, considering his temperament, not a very strong one, desired to put you away. Being in the corridor of the coach, he sees you alone by the door. That door accidently swings open, and he discovers that it is unlocked by seeing you frantically pull it to again. He advances down the corridor. If you had turned he would have addressed you casually. You didn't. 

With some object-I don't know what-wrapped in his scarf, he suddenly strikes you from behind. 

You fall against the door and out you go. At the rate the train was going, the chances are in favor of a serious accident. If you should recover, there is no chance at all of your identifying him. You did not see him. After you've fallen, he ducks into the washroom, waits until the guard gets out of the way, and then calmly strolls back to the compartment. The one mistake he has made has been in using his scarfor perhaps, in not holding onto it instead of letting it fall with you." "Ingenious, if unconvincing. Now, what about the body? Do you mean to tell me that Temple, after ridding the world of me, or thinking he did, remained on the train, and calmly proceeded to toss somebody else off beyond La Bocca? Isn't that a little redundant? Is this tossing off a habit of his? Oh, 145

Craft, old man, it doesn't make sense. Not a bit of it." "I'm afraid, Marshall, that you're confoundedly and annoyingly right. And that scarf business is a bit too simple, what? The man wouldn't risk it, unless he were an utter ass. And Temple, for all his fondness for tipping the elbow————__” "For what?" "For imbibing spiritous liquids, to put it in words of one syllable-well, for all of that, Temple's no fool. No, Marshall, it's that plagued motive we have to go after. Without that, there's not a thing we can do. So, beginning to-morrow morning, we shall play a new game called 'hunt the motive.' And may we find it!" 146

CHAPTER 13: Introduces a Man Named Gilbert HE Eclaireur de Nice of the following morn ing, Saturday, December 1st, bore a retouched photograph of the dead man, arranged, by the skill of some photographer, in an appearance not unlife-like. Captions under it, in both French and English, requested any one acquainted with the man to communicate at once with the Cannes police authorities. There was no hint, however, that the picture was that of the body, nor that it had been discovered that Robinson Marshall, far from being dead, was at present engaged, by proxy, in seeking out his own. murderer. A further note, under an unilluminating account of the failure of the police to make any progress, begged the unknown gentleman who occupied the compartment with Mr. Marshall to come forward for questioning.

During the morning, Porteus telephoned to the 

Craft villa. "The body has been identified," he announced, in a pleased voice. "And the unknown man in M. Marshall's compartment has promised to come in before one and tell us what little he can. It may not be much, but we must inquire into every detail of the case, n'est çe pas? Now, Commander Craft, would you and M. Marshall come over? To identify this man?" 147

Craft, who was no less delighted than Porteus, promised to come at once. With Marshall, he drove down shortly afterwards, and was shown into the Magistrate's office. Porteus, massive and impressive behind his beard, rubbed his hands together with satisfaction when they had sat down. "We begin to make progress, messieurs! It delights me to report that this morning's business looks very promising." "The body has been identified?" Craft asked, checking Porteus's exuberant flow of optimism. "It has, monsieur. Not an hour ago a handsome young woman came and asked to see me. Ah, but she was magnificent, that one! But that has nothing to do with the case. She gave her name and address, and said that she had recognized the man as some one she knew slightly, and wished to know, before she said anything more, what we wanted to know about him, and why. I said, 'Mademoiselle, can you prepare yourself for a shock?' and she said, 'But certainly, if it is not too dreadful,' and I said, 'Mademoiselle, this gentleman has died.' She was surprised, messieurs. Oh, very surprised, but not so very shocked. Then I said, 'Could you bear the ordeal of viewing his body, mademoiselle?' and she said, so, in a faint whisper, 'If-I-must.' Oh, and she was pale then, pauvre enfant! I said, 'It is better that you do,' and she came without another word."

Porteus spread his large, hairy hands on the top of his desk, and bent forward, beard protruded, to emphasize his climax. "But she was shocked when she saw him. I thought she would faint.” 148

"I don't blame her," muttered Marshall, who had vivid recollections. "And she clutched her throat, so," continued Porteus, showing them in vivid pantomime, "and said, "Yes, it is he! Oh, M. le Juge, how did he die?' And 

I-oh, but I was discreet! I said, 'Mademoiselle, it grieves me to tell you. I fear he was killed deliberately. But we do not know how, yet. So tell me who he is.' 'Please take me out of here, and I will tell you.' "We came back to this office, and she sat just there, as white as a sheet. "He is a M. Alfred Gilbert,' she told me, 'and I believe he lives in a villa in La Napoule. I have met him at teas in Cannes, and once at a bridge party.' ""And when was the last time you saw him, mademoiselle?' "Messieurs, what do you think she said? She said, 'I saw him Thursday afternoon, on the train from

Nice to Cannes. He sat in my compartment, and we chatted together." " "Really, M. Porteus, this is most interesting!" 

Craft declared, with enthusiasm. "Does she live in 

La Napoule as well?" "No, monsieur. In Cannes, at a hotel." "Then she did not ride beyond Cannes on that train?" "No. She alighted at Cannes." "Did she say whether this man, Gilbert, I think you said his name was, was still in the compartment when she left it?" "I asked her that, and she could not be sure. She is quite certain, however, that he intended riding as far as La Napoule. He mentioned that to her." 149

"I see. Could she tell you anything at all of M. Gilbert?" "Unfortunately, no. She knew him only slightly.

She had met him but once or twice. He seemed to be an Englishman, and she heard him speak often of London." "I see. I say, Marshall, you said the man was familiar to you. Does his name recall anything?"

Marshall had sunk into a brown study from which he roused himself to shake his head. · • "It does, and it doesn't. Gilbert-London . feel as if I've got something just out of reach. You know the feeling. Gilbert . . . Gilbert. Let me see that photograph again."

Porteus passed a copy of L'Eclaireur to him, and watched him as he studied the picture, frowning. "No. It's no use. London-wait a moment. His first name was Alfred? Al-Al Gorman! I've got it, Porteus! Craft! I've got it! Al Gorman! If that man's name is Gilbert, then I'm Santa Claus. He's 

Al Gorman!" "Hold on here," Craft begged. "Not so flighty, old chap. Slowly and sedately, now: who in the 

Dickens is Al Gorman?" "You should remember him. No. perhaps you wouldn't." Marshall drew his chair forward, and included Porteus in his audience, stumbling a little in his efforts to make his French both lucid and rapid. "It was a couple of winters ago in London. Do you remember Clarabelle Fontaine, Craft? The New 

York girl who was presented that spring? You do, of course; a downy little chicken, and hard-boiled as they come, when you got to know her. In American we are not proud of Clarabelle Fontaine. She's ex- 150

actly what you English usually consider American heiresses to be, and what they seldom are. We like her father even less. He is not a millionaire, not in pounds, at least, but he has more money than he should have, and he takes none of the credit of having made it himself. Inherited it from a brother. 

Crude sort of man, and Clarabelle's every inch his daughter. The day they landed in England every con man in the island brushed up his best tricks, and set out to cheat her, and relieve that father of some of his cash. And what duffers they were! "In honor of her presentation—and how she ever came to be presented I don't know, and won't inquire -her father wanted to buy her a necklace. He asked my advice; I'd known him slightly in America, and 

I was about the only person he did know at all well in London just then. Together we chose a necklace that had been displayed for some time in a show window of a certain jeweler's, and arranged that before the deal should be concluded the necklace should be brought to Mr. Fontaine's hotel for inspection. If he was satisfied with it, he would pay for it then and there in cash. "Now, these arrangements, M. Porteus, were made quite openly in the shop. Unquestionably other people standing nearby heard them, and learned Mr. 

Fontaine's name. We could only conclude later that some one then present in the shop must have overheard him giving these instructions. In any event, two evenings later, as had been arranged, a man called at Mr. Fontaine's rooms, while I was with him, and delivered a box containing the necklace, as well as a bill. Now, unfortunately, neither Mr. Fontaine nor myself knew anything about jewels. The 151

reputation of the firm we were dealing with was quite sufficient guarantee that the jewels were not only genuine, but valued at precisely what Mr. Fontaine paid for them. He accepted the necklace, therefore, and paid the cash for it to the messenger, who left his bill, receipted, and departed. "The following day I happened to be passing the jeweler's, and I don't mind telling you I was pretty surprised to see an exact duplicate of the necklace Mr. Fontaine had just bought in the window. I had understood that it was a unique design that would under no circumstances be copied. I stopped in, more out of curiosity than anything else, to inquire about it, and the salesman who had sold the necklace told me an odd thing. Their messenger had taken the necklace to the hotel at the time agreed upon, and had been met by a gentleman in the lobby as he was about to send up his name. This gentleman showed him one of Mr. Fontaine's cards and explained that he was Fontaine's secretary. Fontaine had changed his mind about the necklace, he said, and preferred to buy a bracelet. He would stop in a day or so later and choose it. The messenger left without delivering the necklace, returned to the shop, and the necklace was once more put in the window where I had seen it. "I explained what had happened, and the messenger was shown in at once, for me to look at. I knew immediately that he was not the man who had brought the duplicate necklace to us. Our man had been tallish and blond, with oddly colored eyes-this employee was short and quite dark. There was no chance at all of mistaking one for the other. And when I questioned him, he described the gentleman who posed as Fontaine's secretary as tall, heavily 152

built, and blond, with eyes of a curious bluishyellow. "What had happened was clear enough, and when later the jewelers examined the necklace Fontaine had accepted, it was definitely proved. Some one, possibly following Fontaine, had overheard him buying the necklace, and had got hold of a duplicate of paste, a bill form from this jeweler's, and one of their boxes, and had come to the hotel, waited until the authentic messenger had arrived, had stopped him and sent him away, and had then come upstairs, passed over the imitation, taken the cash, and disappeared." "Isn't two days rather a short time to prepare a duplicate of an intricate necklace?" Craft asked. "I suppose a skillful man might do it. But wouldn't he require drawings and exact specifications?" "Oh, yes. But, you see, what had happened was quite another story. The necklace was not an original design. It had belonged, at one time, to Lady Throssel, whose daughter sold it after her mother's death. 

About a year before, jewel thieves had made an attempt to rob Hanningsly Manor, where Lady Throssel was living at the time. They managed to get away with a few trifling rings, and a copy of the necklace, which Lady Throssel wore on most occasions in place of the genuine one, which she kept in a safety deposit box. It was that duplicate that was foisted upon Mr. Fontaine. Lady Throssel's daughter later identified it, and so did the jewelers who had made it. "To make a long story short, Scotland Yard soon enough traced the business to a man who had pulled the same game before, a con man known to them as Al Gorman. But discovering that Al Gorman was at 153

the bottom of the business, and finding Al Gorman himself, were two different things. Al seemed to have made his getaway the night he had passed the imitation necklace." "The bank notes: couldn't they be traced?" "Not on your life! They turned up, one by one, in places where they couldn't be traced-at race meets, in large shops, in a dozen and one places where anybody might have paid them in. And as far as Fontaine was concerned, that was the end of the affair. He had nothing to show but an empty jewel case and a big hole in his bank account. Naturally he hushed the business up-no man likes to be made to look ridiculous. "But the point, M. Porteus, is this. I recognize our friend Alfred Gilbert, without doubt, as the man who brought the necklace to Mr. Fontaine's room that night. In other words, I recognize him as Al Gorman."

Porteus, for a moment, studied the top of the desk before him. "So now we know who he is," he said at last, sighing. "We must investigate his villa at once, of course. In the meantime, it raises a pretty problem, does it not? What was this M. Gorman doing here? 

Was he merely hiding, or . . ." He shook his head. "This woman, now. I wonder. She knew him, she said, as a charming gentleman wintering at La

Napoule. But she was not so interested in him, messieurs, as she was in M. Marshall." · · "In me?" Marshall showed surprise. "In what way?" "When she had told me what she knew about M. 

Gilbert-Gorman-she spoke of you. 'And M. Mar- 154

shall,' she said. 'May-may I see his body, too?' All white, she was, still. But I shook my head. 'Mademoiselle has had enough of a shock for one morning,' I told her. 'And besides, M. Marshall's body is not here.' 'Then-where is it?' she asked. But I was foxy, messieurs. 'Why do you inquire, mademoiselle? Do you know him, too?' 'I have met him,' she said. Then she rose and left." "Curious." Marshall frowned. "What was her name, M. Porteus? You haven't told us that yet. If she knew me, I might be able to tell you something about her." "She was a lovely woman!" declared Porteus, with enthusiasm, while he consulted a note he had made. "She lives at the Grand Hôtel de la Reine de 

Grande Bretagne, and her name is Elise Valery." 155

CHAPTER 14: Introduces the Frenchman 

A in the First Compartment

MOMENT or two later, the Commissary knocked upon the door and came in, leading a chubby Frenchman with a face which, unadorned by its mustache, might have appeared cherubic. He glanced curiously about the room, inspecting Porteus and his two visitors gravely, seating himself finally, at Bellecour's invitation, and folding two pudgy hands over a paunch. "This is the witness, M. le Juge," Bellecour announced. "I have brought him in for questioning. 

Good morning, Commander Craft. Good morning, monsieur. You were good to come. Now, my friend, your name first."

Without embarrassment the Frenchman gave the information Bellecour desired. "I am Armand Conti, messieurs, and I am the proprietor of the Hôtel des Palmes at St. Raphael." "You were on the Nice-Marseilles train Thursday afternoon?" "I was, monsieur. I was returning to St. Raphael.

I had been in Nice ordering linens for my hotel from an agent there with whom I deal." "Do you remember where you sat in that train?" "Yes, monsieur. I sat in the middle of the train—

I cannot recall exactly which car-but I was in the first compartment forward of that car. This gentle- 156

man"-hereupon he pointed at Marshall-"was in the compartment with me as far as Cannes, if I recall correctly." "Was he alone?" asked Bellecour, who was anxious to test Conti's reliability as a witness before he came to more important matters. "No, monsieur. Not always. For a time a charming American lady sat by him, and they talked. I cannot understand English well enough to tell you what they were saying; I did not listen, monsieur. 

But I could not but admire the lady." "And I thought you were asleep!" Marshall put in. Conti turned large brown eyes upon him. "But no, monsieur. I dozed, perhaps, now and then; that was all. When the lady was there I was wide awake." "You say this gentleman here got out at Cannes?" 

Bellecour persisted, ignoring the interruption. "He left the train at Cannes, I suppose. He left the compartment just before, with his coat over his arm, and I did not see him again." "Were you dozing about that time, M. Conti? I ask, because it is of that time that I wish particularly to ask you." "No, monsieur. I was awake then. You may ask me what you like." "Eh bien! Monsieur here left the compartment just before you reached Cannes. Tell me, did you see any one passing your compartment door from somewhere back in the car just afterwards?" 

Conti was compelled to cogitate. "I saw the conducteur. He hurried by, going forward, and then, of a sudden, while we were still this side of Cannes, the train stopped." 157

"You saw only the conducteur pass? You are sure of that?" "Oh, I assure you, I am quite certain of that." "Did any one pass just before this gentleman left the compartment?" "Ah!" Conti shrugged his shoulders elaborately. "That I couldn't say. Possibly. Possibly not. I was not watching, and even if I saw some one, I have forgotten it now." "Well, that does not matter so much. It is to be regretted, of course." Bellecour, in his turn, shrugged. "The conducteur went by. What then?" "Then, monsieur, the train stopped for a while, and presently it started again, and the conducteur came back. I stopped him, because I was curious, and asked him if anything had happened. An accident, or something. He said no, so very emphatically that 

I did not believe him. But we went on, and I thought no more about it." 

When Bellecour shifted to questions concerning people who alighted or boarded the train at Cannes station, Conti, in spite of his best efforts, could remember nothing. People got off, people got on. He knew none of them, and recognized no one. The sum of his evidence, therefore, was that no one had followed Marshall to the platform just before he fell. Nothing could shake him on that point. Of nothing else could he be certain, and even that fact was rendered almost valueless because he could not say that some person might not have gone toward the platform ahead of Marshall.

It was a point that had not occurred to Marshall either, but, like Conti, when Bellecour pressed hm for an answer, he could be certain of nothing. His 158

impression was that the corridor had been empty, at least outside of his compartment, for some time before he left it. The corridor, he believed, was empty, too, when he did come out. More than that he could not say.

Shortly after, Conti was excused, and Craft and Marshall themselves took their leave, somewhat disappointed by Conti, but glad enough at least to eliminate him from the list of suspects. A known, and innocuous, hotel proprietor was a much more comforting element in the case than an unknown and possibly dangerous Frenchman.

But Craft found it impossible to draw out Marshall on the subject. He was too sunk in meditation over the identity of the girl who had identified Al Gorman. 159

ARSHALL was less tractable that afternoon than before when Craft begged him not to appear at rehearsal. His curiosity concerning Al Gorman, and, above all, Elise's knowledge of him, was so great that he had difficulty in controlling his patience. He was satisfied, as he admitted when 

Craft pressed him, that Porteus's report of the incident was correct, and, consequently, that Elise's knowledge of the dead man was merely that of an acquaintance, as she had said. But there was a curious phase of the matter that worried him, and, unable to contain himself, he spoke to Craft concerning it at luncheon. "You see, Craft, to put two and two together gives you something to think about. Take first of all Miss 

Valery's pipe dream about those two paintings, her firm belief that they are there, and her determination to buy the Chèvre d'Or and dig them up; take that, on one hand, and on the other hand the fact that she knows, even slightly, a man we know to be a fugitive from justice, and a man who's pulled a crooked con game more than once in his life. . . . I don't like it, Craft. You see what that gives us, don't you?" 

Craft nodded. "As you'd put it, dirty work at the crossroads. 

However, there's no use in jumping to conclusions. 160

THE SCARF 

Miss Valery, I believe, did not mention our friend

He might agree to put through the deal for her, demand a cash settlement on some excuse, and then clear out with about ten thousand dollars. Or any of a dozen other things."

Craft, I beleve in coincidence, and the strange workings of chance as much as anybody. I never put anything down now, without saying, 'Pencil, I place you on the desk beside my blotter.' Then I repeat the phrase three times, and, of course, I never forget. Gradually you get so that you no longer need to repeat the phrase. You subconsciously know where you put things."

Marshall, we have made inquiries, which I shall outline to you in a moment, to discover something about the man who committed the crime. We considered the case in this light: M. Gilbert, somewhere between Cannes and the place where his body was found, was strangled and flung out of a window of that train." "Just a moment," Craft interposed. "Why do you say between Cannes and the spot beyond La Bocca? 

Why not before Cannes?" "For what I consider a legitimate, and a good reason. Simply because at Cannes any one was apt to board the train. A man would not care to find himself with a dead body, obviously strangled to death, if a stranger should walk into his compartment. He would desire, naturally, to dispose of the body at once. If he killed the man before reaching Cannes, 177

he must have been alone, or else he would not have had an opportunity. Then, surely, he would have had the opportunity of pushing it out of the window at the same time. Since he did not do so, but pushed the body out beyond La Bocca, we believe that the crime was committed beyond La Bocca." "Quite logical, M. Porteus. But you speak of a window instead of a door. Is there any reason to believe it was a window?" "None at all. A mere matter of phrasing. Window or door, it is all the same." "But there is one point," Bellecour put in. "You fell, M. Marshall, out of a door to the left of the train, on the corridor side. Now, M. Gilbert's body was found on the right side of the train, between the tracks. That is consistent with our belief that he might have been pushed out of a compartment window-they are certainly large enough, and open wide enough—instead of having been dragged into the corridor, where anybody might have seen it." "I see. Sorry I interrupted." "Not at all! Well, once we believed that, we determined to find out where the man who committed the murder should have left the train. We know that 

M. Gilbert was going to La Napoule. He lived there; he told Miss Valery he was going there. But we find on him half of a return ticket to Cannes, not to La Napoule." "Couldn't he have alighted with it at La Napoule, and there paid excess?" "He could, certainly. But he lived in La Napoule, and knew he was returning there. Why should he even purchase a ticket to Cannes?" "I confess I don't know," Craft admitted. Mar- 178

shall, whose rôle so far had been one of polite but mute attention, made a suggestion. "Where was this ticket found, M. Porteus?" "In the upper breast pocket of the overcoat, M. Marshall." "The overcoat, however, was mine. I had my ticket in that pocket, too, if I recall correctly. I believe that was my ticket, not M. Gilbert's." "That," said Bellecour triumphantly, "is what I have been trying to explain to M. Porteus. But he is pig-headed." "But then M. Gilbert had no ticket!" Porteus protested. "None at all!" 

Bellecour faced him, with elaborate patience. "Did we find one thing on him that identified him?" he asked. "One single thing? His pockets were absolutely empty, absolutely! Not so much as a sou did we find. But in the overcoat! Ah, that was different. You see?" "You mean," Craft said, hesitantly, "that the murderer cleared out his pockets?" "Exactly, monsieur. That is just what I mean." "And did not clear out the overcoat pockets?" "Yes. Because in them there was nothing to identify the man as Alfred Gilbert-or Gorman.' 

"But why do that?" "He does not know himself," Porteus declared vindictively. "Don't listen to him!" "I admit," said Bellecour placidly, "that the matter puzzles me. Now, however, we are discussing the tickets, and if we are not careful, we are apt to stray from the subject and ramble all over the place. The point we approach is this: When a passenger disembarks at any station along this particular line, he must 179

present his ticket for collection, or pay excess. Tickets sold to-day between local stops are available for three days. Distance tickets, of course, are valuable for any length of time. But local tickets are either collected within three days, or they are never collected. "Now there are four possibilities. One: that our unknown murderer was aware that M. Gilbert would take that train on Thursday from Nice to La Napoule, that he bought a ticket also to La Napoule, waited for his opportunity, murdered him, and then left the train at La Napoule with his ticket. That points to premeditation. Secondly, this murderer may already have been bound for La Napoule, and murdered Gilbert on the spur of the moment, seeing an opportunity and having, naturally, a motive. Third, there is the possibility that the murderer was not going to La Napoule at all, but to Cannes or some station before one reaches Cannes. In that case, seeing Gilbert, he did not get out at his station, but waited, killing Gilbert, and then, at

La Napoule, left the train with Gilbert's ticket. 

Fourth, there is the possibility that he was going beyond La Napoule, that he did so, and got off at Agay or San Raphael, or even went as far as Marseilles. "Any one of those solutions may be correct. The fourth, I think, is least likely to be so; but that is only a personal opinion. I hardly believe that the man would remain on board the train any longer than necessary after committing the crime, for fear that somebody might have seen something that might arouse suspicions, and thus cause trouble for him. But, as I said, that is only an opinion. 180

"As for the others, any one may be correct. But listen to what we have learned. "We traced all tickets bought on Thursday between Nice and Cannes, to every destination from Cagnse, which is the next stop after Nice, as far as Marseilles. We also checked up on which of these tickets had been collected. The full reports are not in yet, of course, because most of the tickets are still valuable to-day. But to-night their time expires, and by to-morrow morning we shall know definitely which ones have been collected. In the meantime we know this: that, of the tickets bought from Nice to

Cannes on that day, all but two have already been collected. One of these was that one of yours,

M. Marshall, found in the overcoat. The other has not been turned in or traced. It might be—we dare not say must be one bought by the murderer, who intended to alight at Cannes, and did not do so, but traveled on to La Napoule. "Then we made inquiries at La Napoule. And there all tickets from Nice were collected on Thursday. There are none missing at all, not even the return half of one bought that morning. So that, if

M. Gilbert did truly buy a ticket to La Napoule, the murderer must have taken it from his pocket, and used it himself in alighting." "But do not forget," Porteus pointed out, "that we do not know that he did buy a ticket to La Napoule. We have only Miss Valery's word that he intended going there." "He lived there!" Bellecour exclaimed, as if that settled it. "I know. I know. But if he was in Cannes that 181

morning, he may have bought a return to Cannes, intending to pay excess." "And subject himself to that annoyance, when he might so easily have avoided it by buying a proper ticket?" 

Craft interposed soothingly. "No doubt we shall know eventually what happened," he said. "Right now we can only speculate.

But if M. Gilbert did have a ticket to La Napoule, which seems likely, then the murderer must have ransacked his pockets, found the ticket, and used it himself. And it shows that the murderer, in that case, did not have a ticket to La Napoule himself. Is that so?" "Quite," said Bellecour, but Porteus shook his head doubtfully. "There is another thing, messieurs, which is of undetermined significance, but I report it to you for what it is worth. We have communicated with the cleaners of the cars in Marseilles, and have talked to the two women who cleaned the cars we are interested in, when we managed to learn the car numbers from the railroad officials. The women's names were Rondel and Gannet. And the things they picked up!" Bellecour raised his eyes to express his astonishment, which had not yet subsided. "In the washroom of car 61453, the coach you were in," he read from a list which had lain on

Porteus's desk, "a man's hat, black felt, widebrimmed such as a Frenchman would wear, but no 

Englishman. Imagine leaving a hat! A new one, too, the cleaner said. A large brown button from a man's overcoat. Also two long pieces of twine-or one piece, broken in the middle. In the first compartment-yours, M. Marshall-several little paper 182

bags from shops in Nice, and an unopened package of cigarettes. In the next compartment, a twenty-franc note and a woman's handkerchief with the initial D." "Dukane Collins, probably," Craft nodded. "What else?" "You will not believe me when I tell you what was found in the next compartment. Yours, Commander Craft." "What?" "A whole fish. A big fish. Think of it!" 

Craft laughed. "No, M. le Commissaire, I left no fish behind me! 

And as astonishing as it is, I doubt whether that will have anything to do with the solution of our little mystery. But how careless people are!" 183

CHAPTER 17: Reveals a Flame colored Negligée B ELLECOUR arose finally from his chair. "M. Marshall, I'm afraid we've taken up a good deal of your time," he said apologetically. "M. Porteus and I are on our way now to the villa which M. Gilbert occupied in La Napoule. 

Would you care to spare the time to come with us?

You have partially identified M. Gilbert as the man 

Gorman. Possibly we may find evidence at the villa to substantiate the identification. You could, undoubtedly, help us in that." "I'd be glad to come, if I wouldn't be in the way. 

Commander Craft will, of course, come, too." "I see no objection," Bellecour said, with slight hesitation. "I have my car here," Craft announced. "Let me drive you there. It will save taking the tram." "Excellent!" Bellecour's brow cleared. "We can commandeer your car. A thousand thanks for your kindness." 

They drove down the Boulevard Carnot to the port, and turned west along the shore road to La Napoule. Once, when they were passing through a desolate stretch, Bellecour halted Craft. On their right, the tracks of the P.-L.-M. line stretched parallel to the road. A few miniature freight cars stood idly upon a siding. Beyond them, marshy land, 184

dotted here and there with unsightly heaps of refuse, tin cans, prunings from palm trees, and the sundry débris of a dump, extended to a line of trees choked with underbrush. There was only one building of any sort in sight, a decrepit villa, very Moorish and very down at the heels, which stood a hundred yards back from the tracks, deserted and depressed-looking. "It was just here," Bellecour explained, "that the body was found. The linesman had come by just before the passage of the local, to inspect the freight cars. Almost immediately after the local had gone through he was coming back toward La Bocca, and found the body lying in the space between the uptracks and the down-tracks. His lantern-it was quite dark then, you remember-illuminated the white and bloody face." "There is no question, I suppose," Craft ventured, "that the body did fall from the train." "There is none whatever. The fact that the body was wearing M. Marshall's overcoat, which must have been on the train, and the fact that M. Gilbert himself was seen on that train, would prove that. 

There was no other way in which his body could have been brought here at the time it was discovered. 

Besides that, the linesman swore positively that he had seen no one approach the tracks on foot. He was near by, and though he did not see or hear the body fall, he would not have failed to hear a struggle if one had taken place, or seen a figure, or two figures, if there had been more than one, carrying the body. 

No, I think there's no doubt about the body's falling from the train." 

The Gilbert villa at La Napoule stood back from the sea-front, on the lower slopes of a conical hill, 185

where it was hidden in a garden of mimosas. It was an unpretentious place, such as a bachelor might occupy, who lived alone and spent most of his time elsewhere. 

When Craft had parked his car outside of the gates, Bellecour tugged at a bell let into one of the gate posts, and waited for a response. There was none.

After a while, he pushed open the gate and led the way, Porteus at his heels, through the little garden.

There was no one about. If Gilbert employed a servant, the man or woman was certainly nowhere on the premises. A hammering upon the front door echoed dully through the house, without results. 

Bellecour finally turned the knob, and found the door locked. It was a simple enough lock to open. The 

Commissary, prepared for just such an emergency, had brought a set of keys, one of which threw back the latch at once.

They entered into a small square vestibule. A door to the right opened upon a sitting-room, and one upon the left into a comfortable living-room, its ceiling supported by grooved wooden columns that had been parts of an olive press in a previous phase of their career.

It was in here that the search began. Craft, at home wherever he found himself, made himself comfortable in an easy chair, and watched the proceedings with interest. Marshall could not compose himself, and wandered about nervously, as often as not getting in the way, impatient for tangible results. 

They were slow in coming. Porteus and Bellecour, at first, limited their inspection to a comprehensive survey of the room from a vantage point somewhere near the door. It was furnished simply but 186

comfortably, and showed unmistakable traces of the absence of a neat servant. A desk stood near a corner window, and they finally began to ransack it thoroughly. 

Gilbert kept few papers there, apparently. Several tradesmen's notices, with his name upon them, seemed to be all that he kept. There was a drawer filled with fairly good writing paper stamped with the villa's name, and another drawer contained an assortment of rubber bands, pencils, fountain pens, erasers, stamps, and the other normal accessories of a writing desk. "Nothing there whatever," Porteus said disappointedly, when they had felt carefully and vainly for a secret drawer. "Take heart," Craft counseled, from his chair. "If the chap's a crook, he most certainly isn't going to have proofs of it lying about in his desk drawers." "He has a private safe, you think?" Porteus asked, eyeing a small painting hung over the desk and measuring with a glance the possibility of its concealing a safe. "Most surely, if he has been foolish enough to keep any evidences of his identity about him. I wouldn't, I know. I'd chuck everything like that into the fire at the first chance. And rake the ashes."

Porteus dug into the sides of chairs, feeling between the upholstered arms and seat. The results of his efforts were three hairpins. "Aha! A lady friend!" 

Craft observed them, unimpressed. "Miss Valery told you she'd been to tea with him. 

Perhaps they're hers." "Perhaps. Her hair is not short, is it?” 187

"No," said Marshall. "Long, and done up loose knot on the back of her head." with a "They could be hers, surely. We will preserve them." He placed them between the pages of a notebook, and put it away in his pocket. "Now this table. 

Pfah! There's no drawer!"

Bellecour meanwhile had been rummaging through the books on two shelves near the desk. He reported, after a while, that most of them were marked with the initials A. G., but that there was nothing else interesting about them. "What books has he?" Craft asked. "I'm always interested in a person's reading matter." "Mostly English works," Bellecour announced, reading a few titles. ""The Well of Loneliness.' 

Ah, I've heard of that book! 'Sons and Lovers.' 'The

Oxford Book of English Verse.' An etymological dictionary. Vasari's 'Lives of the Painters.' Petronius. 

Apuleius. 'Le Côté de Guermantes.' 'The Omnibus of Crime.' Ah, that looks odd!" "No, it doesn't, M. le Commissaire. That is the most innocent book on the shelf. The man was human, if he liked detective stories." "He was interested in crime," said Bellecour, sticking to his guns. "In the way thousands of Englishmen are, as good entertainment. Yes, his books are interesting. Shall we retire to the next room, if you are finished in here?"

The small sitting-room opposite seemed to be seldom used. Its furniture was stiffly placed and undisturbed. There was an upright piano there, and a music cabinet beside it, with a few worn sheets of 

La Bohème, "The Blue Danube," "Chanson Indue," 188

and the like. It was impossible to tell whether these were Gilbert's favorites, or whether he had found them there when he took the villa. They found nothing of interest in that room at all, and filed from there into a dining room, in good order, and finally into a kitchen. The kitchen was exceptionally clean. "If I were given to deduction, I'd say he hired a cook by the day, and did the rest of the housework himself," Craft remarked, glancing about at the orderly rows of pots and pans. "Porteus, do you wish to look into the oven? Then we might go upstairs." 

Porteus solemnly opened the oven door, peeped in, and shut it again, brushing off his hands. "Upstairs, then. There is nothing down here."

There were three bedrooms and a bath on the second floor. In the first bedroom, a large chamber over the living-room, with unpapered walls and a fireplace, they found clothes evidently belonging to Gilbert. There was a closet where four or five suits hung. They had all been made by a Nice tailor, and were marked on the inner breast pocket of the coats with the name Alfred Gilbert. The pockets, without exception, were empty. "That's unusual," Craft remarked, noticing Porteus's vain efforts to fish up anything out of them. "A valet clears pockets, but Gilbert apparently had no valet. Careful man, that. I'd hate to have any one go through my pockets before Pietro, my man, hangs them out." "I know. You collect a bunch of junk in pockets, don't you? I do," Marshall confessed. "Funny that 

Gilbert was neat in that respect, and not in others." "Self-protection, old son. If anything happened to him suddenly, there'd be no way of identifying 189

him, except that label. I say, M. Porteus, did the coat-that is, the jacket of the suit on the body have a tailor's label in it?" "No, Commander Craft. It had the label, in the neckband, of a shop in London. That was all." "I see. Marshall, I'm beginning to wonder whether our murderer did go through Gilbert's pockets. Perhaps they were already empty." "But his ticket and change. He had no change." "Blast it, you're right. I'd forgotten the ticket. 

All right. Found something, M. Bellecour?"

Bellecour had deserted the unproductive closet and had begun to inspect the top and drawers of a dresser. He had made a discovery at once. "The lady friend again," he declared, holding up a brooch. "Stuck into a pin-cushion. Look at it."

Craft hobbled over and examined it. It was a small brooch of diamonds set in platinum, in the shape of three heart-like leaflets. "Careless of somebody," he murmured, passing it back. "There does seem to be a lady friend, doesn't there?"

Bellecour continued his prying into the bureau drawers while Porteus mounted a chair and glanced under the three framed pictures hung upon the walls. 

There was no safe behind any of them. Bellecour suddenly interrupted, with the most satisfying discovery yet made. He drew out of one of the drawers a woman's negligée of flame-colored chiffon, embroidered, at the ends of flowing sleeves and around the hem, in gold thread. "The lady friend would be particularly beautiful in that," he declared, with an approving flutter of the wrap. 190

"She would seem to have done more than take tea here," Porteus opined, regarding it. "And how well it would suit Miss Valery!" "You don't know that it's hers," Marshall said icily. "In fact, you can be pretty certain it isn't hers. 

Miss Valery isn't like that." "I know, I know," Porteus nodded. "But you never can tell. Miss Valery is very attractive." "And absolutely above any rotten philandering." "Temper, temper, temper!" said Craft reprovingly, patting Marshall's arm. "You mustn't mind 

M. Porteus. He doesn't know Miss Valery very well." "Sorry." Marshall scowled. "I'm an idiot. A complete, priceless, and golden ass. But I'll lay you any odds that that isn't Miss Valery's negligée." "Probably not. Just a bad guess of M. Porteus's. 

That's that. Whoever she was, Gilbert had a friend. 

And that begins to make things interesting." "To you, perhaps," Porteus retorted, hurt by 

Craft's lack of appreciation. "But I should rather find the safe."

But a thorough search of the villa did not disclose it, if one ever existed. 191

N ERRAND of mercy took Craft, that night, to the Grand Hôtel de la Reine de Grande Bretagne, where a few minutes of private conversation with the manager satisfactorily settled the problem of Marshall's room, and his personal belongings in it. The manager was only too happy to permit the room to remain untouched for a few more days, since Commander Craft was so liberal in paying for it in advance; though he must know that it was a great favor that was being done, for observe: the season was opening, and he had need for every room in his hotel.

Craft had brought with him a small bag, and when he had left the manager's office, he betook himself to the fourth floor and let himself into Marshall's room with Marshall's key, gathered what linen and haberdashery he felt that Marshall might need, packed them into the bag, and descended once more to the lobby.

It was shortly after eight, that rather restless period of the evening when dinner is over, and the bridge tables and baccarat parties, casino bound, have not yet settled themselves to the night's business. 

Several people were taking coffee at little tables, or reading the London journals newly placed on the reading table. An orchestra, having struggled 192

IMPRUDENT GOSSIP 

through a program of Puccini and Berlioz, fondly believed to be conducive to an increased appetite, were discarding 'cello in favor of saxophone, and second violin for traps, in preparation for a discordant hour or two of dance music, which would consist, almost exclusively, of a tango or two, and last year's carnival march played in frantic one-step time, to endless repetitions.

Craft found Dukane Collins, in an evening gown of beaded jade satin, a wrap of gold cloth drooping from one shoulder. She was sitting, rather fretfully, over a cold demi-tasse at a table near the entrance to the card room. An empty cup at the place beside her gave Craft a hint of the cause of her fretfulness. "Alone, Dukane? May I join you?" "Do, Commander. I'm waiting for Lloyd. He went off somewhere with Colonel Poplin to change something about the play-I don't know what."

Dukane, very plainly, felt she was missing something, and resented it. "Sit down here. There! I've a beastly headache. Forgive me for being a bit distrait." "I have an infallible cure for headaches. A crême de menthe à la glace." "Just at the moment it sounds deadly. Are you sure it cures headaches?" "I wouldn't think of suggesting it if it didn't. 

Really not." "Very well," she said wearily. "I'll try it. Only you must have one, too." "But I haven't a headache." "You probably will have, if you talk to me very long. I'm in a perfectly rotten mood. It's the mistral, I think. It's been blowing all afternoon." She 193

She sipped it tentatively, rather pleased with its coolness. "How about our new leading man?" she asked finally. "Tell me about him."

CHAPTER 19: Deals with Tea at Lou Matagot

A 

T FOUR, on Sunday afternoon, Craft's guests began to arrive. The Collinses came early, Dukane unable to control her curiosity and impatience to meet the new leading man. She was disappointed, however, for they found Craft alone, tending to the preparation of Moloch. Dukane flung herself down on Moloch's lip and warmed her hands at the blaze. Lloyd, who always felt a little embarrassed in other people's homes, stood uncomfortably in the background until Craft urged him into a chair. 

There he sat, smiling shyly and saying little. "Your Moloch is a welcome idol, Howard," Dukane said, making herself comfortable against cushions. "What do you sacrifice in his maw? I've never pictured you bloodthirsty enough to cast the first born of the land into him.' "I cast the first born of the forest into him. Pine and oak offspring. And they serve very well." "They most certainly do. Howard, you have a most charming villa. It shows touches of a woman's hand. Tell me, who is she?" "No tangible woman, Dukane. TWO CHARWOMEN 

disappointment by being very gay indeed with 

Colonel Poplin. Mrs. Collins, eyeing the situation with the air of one responsible for it all, was inclined to be irritable. Craft was glad to escape. 

On the Boulevard Carnot he boarded an omnibus which took him to the station. There he booked a return ticket to Marseilles, and made his way to the platform to wait for the 5:47, when it should come through. He had not long to wait; it was almost that time when he reached the platform. In a few minutes the local drew into the station, discharged its passengers for Cannes, and took on others. Craft, once on, wandered through the cars until he found a vacant end compartment where he seated himself when the train drew out of the station.

He sat on the inner side of his compartment, away from the sea, by an open window, from which he could watch the tracks and the sidings as they passed them. It was on this stretch of track, at just this time of evening, from this very train, that Alfred Gilbert had fallen or been thrown. 

It was already dark-darker, perhaps, from the lighted compartment than it might have seemed outside, for there was still a faint silverish glowing in the sky behind the Esterel. From his window he could see the lights of Cannes, not mere spots of brilliance against a black curtain of darkness, but recognizable still as street lamps or lighted rooms in identifiable buildings. Once, before they had passed beyond the city limits, the train plunged into a brief tunnel, emerging almost immediately and turning southwest to the sea, veering then due west, rattling along with exaggerated thumping of wheels and 223

creaking of joints, between the Napoule highway and thick banks of trees.

They passed La Bocca, a cluster of lights, and clattered beyond it into that desolate stretch which Craft always referred to quite accurately as the town dump. Here were midget freight cars drawn up on a siding, as they had been on the previous Thursday, and Craft leaned slightly out of his window to observe them.

By now the train was fully lighted. A series of squares of light, reflected from long lines of compartments, checkered the red sides of the freight cars. 

The countryside, otherwise, was in total darkness. 

Craft, leaning out, estimated the possibility of a body, falling from a compartment ahead of him, being seen. The train, in spite of its rattling, was moving slowly-not more than forty kilometers an hour, he decided. An object as heavy as a man's body, in falling the six or seven feet to the ground, would strike before the next compartment could pass the spot where he fell. Its position would probably not be greatly influenced by the momentum given it by the train's motion. Such a body, in fact, would be thrown forward in such a manner that it would strike the ground almost directly under the window from which it was flung, since its momentum would equal the train's speed. It would fall out of sight at once.

The only evidence of its descent that the person nearest the window in the next compartment would have, would be the momentary darkening of the reflected square of light passing over the freight cars' sides a darkening that might be caused in a number of very innocent ways, and would be hardly apt to arouse suspicion-and the thud of the body striking 224

TWO CHARWOMEN 

the ground, which was almost certain to be drowned in the noise of the train. Once out, the body would lie unnoticed in the deep shadows along the tracks. 

Only by leaning out of the window could any person have been aware of the man's fall. And Craft considered it rather doubtful that any one would risk leaning out far enough, especially since a train, coming in the other direction, might pass suddenly at any moment, with possibly disastrous results.

He drew back, and settled against the plush seat, in thought. They drew into La Napoule, slowed, and stopped. Craft, rousing himself long enough to step into the corridor and peer out of the window there, noticed that only two passengers alighted. 

Apparently La Napoule was not an important stop. 

The train swung south now toward Theoule, along the rocky side of the Esterel. Craft, still thoughtful, instead of returning immediately to his compartment, pushed open the door of the washroom next to it and stepped in, bolting the door behind him, so that he might be undisturbed. The room was very small, and dimly lighted, but he inspected it curiously. 

There was a large window of frosted glass facing, as his compartment window had done, the downtracks rather than the sea. It opened easily, sliding downward almost to the sill, three feet, roughly, from the floor. Craft had no means of measuring it accurately, nor did he feel the need to. The sill was not quite waist high.

Against the partition dividing the washroom from the first compartment, and directly before the window, stood a small metal washstand, waist high, with a metal water-cooler and a mirror above it. A thin pipe, to allow melted ice to flow off from the cooler, 225

descended vertically to join the wastepipe from the washstand, a few inches from the window frame.

Craft turned his attention to the bolt on the door, a very common type of bolt indeed. Its upper edge was toothed, engaging the sprockets of a cog, so that, in sliding forward into its socket, it turned the cog on its axis, automatically registering, on the outer side of the door, the information that the washroom was occupied. Besides this bolt, which could be shot only from inside the washroom, there was a lock, similar to the locks on the coach doors, turned with a key from the outside. There was no key in the lock. 

Craft, glancing once more around the room, unbolted the door, and made his way back to his seat. 

At seven o'clock, his compartment still unoccupied except by himself, he limped into the restaurant car and dined at a table with an uncommunicative

Frenchman, whose table manners were a revelation in the dexterity with which a man may misuse such implements as he may be supplied with. Craft lingered over an unappetizing dinner to watch him, rebelling only at the last moment when the man began excavations with a toothpick. 

On his way back to his compartment, Craft had a bit of unexpected luck. He was passing through the car adjoining his when he found Biondetti, the conducteur, sitting alone in an end compartment, head thrown back, mouth open, and eyes very tightly shut. 

Craft rapped upon the door.

With an effort, Biondetti struggled out of his slumbers, eyeing Craft malevolently until he could see him clearly and recognize him. "Ah, monsieur! It is the Commander, n'est ce 226

pas?" He rose, and Craft, with a smile, slipped into the compartment and sat down. "Biondetti! This is fortunate!" "Yes? And how has the mystery fared, monsieur? I read the journals each morning and seenothing!" The journals, one gathered, disappointed

"You don't remember whether the washroom window was open after leaving Cannes?" "No, monsieur. But it was frequently opened or closed by passengers during a trip." "Thank you, Biondetti. Tell me one thing more.

On that point Biondetti could say nothing. He hadn't noticed the key, and hadn't the faintest notion whether it was in place or not. He admitted that he would not have noticed it had it been missing. He seldom looked at it; it was so familiar an article that he paid no attention to it. He took its presence for granted. 

But Craft was quite satisfied. "That's really all I have to ask, Biondetti. I'm going to Marseilles to interview the cleaners, if I can. Where can I find them?" "I'll tell you, monsieur. When the train comes into the Gare St. Charles and the passengers alight, 229

the cars are run onto a siding. Perhaps ten minutes after we arrive this is done, and the cleaners come on and clear up inside. Then the cars are washed outside, and left standing until morning. After that they are distributed to other trains. Sometimes some of them go out again at once. It depends upon the day and the season. But the cleaners are there always just after the train arrives." He thought a moment. "If you like, monsieur, after we arrive, and I have handed in my reports and my work is over, I will take you into the yards. I can pass you through the barriers without difficulty." "Thank you, Biondetti. Shall I wait for you on the platform?" "In the first-class waiting room, if you don't mind, monsieur. Near the main entrance."

So it was arranged. Shortly after nine o'clock, the train pulled into Marseilles and Craft made his way to the waiting room. Biondetti found him there a quarter of an hour later, and led him through the gates, down a long platform past a line of empty, darkened cars, to a siding. Here were the cars that had composed the Nice-Marseilles local, still lighted and haunted by wraith-like cleaners in faded, blue aprons and frayed dusting caps. "You know whom you seek?" Biondetti asked. "Their names are Rondel and Gannet, I believe; they are the two I most wish to see." "Bien! One moment and I will bring them. TWO CHARWOMEN 

conviction, boarding the train upon his errand. He returned in a little while with two women, dirty and blowsy, with faces the worse for injudicious frequenting of cafés, and not enough food. One was tall and severe, with the expression of an indignant turkey; this was Madame Rondel. The other was short and thin to the point of painfulness, with protruding eyes and a goiter. They eyed Craft with sulky suspicion, and held tightly to their mops, as if they intended to use them to good advantage if he tried to get fresh.

And when finally Biondetti and he together managed to induce them to break silence, Craft was bewildered at the flow of argot that issued from them in hoarse, scratchy voices. At times, Biondetti, with a glint of mischief in his eye, was forced to translate into recognizable French several of the richest of their idioms. Craft, if nothing else, gained several valuable additions to his French vocabulary.

Biondetti, at Craft's prompting, explained his desires. It appeared that Madame Rondel had cleaned the washroom of the particular car Craft was interested in: 61453. Yet so arduous was the task that she could not have accomplished it had not Madame 

Gannet watched her from the doorway and eased her tortured labors with encouraging gossip. Together they had discovered the Frenchman's hat in a dark corner, and had debated whether or not to turn it in at the proper office. They could agree upon one point only: that such procedure would be absurd. Naturally they would keep the hat, but the problem of which of them should have it started an argument that almost broke up a life-long companionship. They had still been arguing about it-though Madame 231

Gannet gave Craft to understand that at that stage they had not been on speaking terms-when the police unconsciously stepped in to compel an armistice by demanding the hat for themselves. 

The other articles found had been thrown out, but fortunately not carried away. After much digging in trash barrels, they had been able to rescue everything but the fish (which had unaccountably disappeared). 

The articles had been wrapped together, but had not yet been dispatched to the Cannes police.

The pair's interest in these things did not reblossom until Craft fingered a note-case. Thereafter they believed that, with diligent search in the closet where their mops were kept, in the near-by station, they might find these articles. They departed, with 

Biondetti, to search for them.

Eventually they found the package, and returned to him and opened it. "I am interested only in the articles you found in the washroom," he explained, as the contents of the package were exposed and laid out on a baggage truck standing near by. "The hat," said Madame Rondel, stiffly, "is not here." "The hat," added Madame Gannet, "is somewhere else. We seem to have mislaid it." 

Such, at least, was the purport of their remarks, if not precisely the wording. "The hat," said Craft, with an earnest desire to raise no difficulties, "is not important. It will be quite enough, for me at least, if you will describe it." 

Madame Rondel thawed appreciably, and even conjured up a cracked smile. "New. Black felt." 232

TWO CHARWOMEN 

"Elegant," contributed Madame Gannet, "with a damned fine wide brim." "—and a black ribbon around it." "-bought at a shop in Marseilles where swells go!" "Oh, but it was elegant!" concluded Madame

Rondel, with a reminiscent gleam in her eye. "Size?" asked Craft.

It developed that it was an uncommonly small size. Just, in fact, large enough to fit Madame Rondel's son. Madame Gannet opined, parenthetically, that a little stretching would render it suitable for her-her nephew, on whom it would be much more becoming.

Craft, however, was not particularly interested in the hat. To the bewilderment of the pair, he seemed much more interested in the overcoat button. It was of a mottled, brownish color, rather like a clouded tortoise shell; such a button as might have been worn on a brown overcoat. It matched exactly his memory of the buttons on Marshall's coat. "Just where did you pick this up?" he asked. "In the washroom." Then, when she found that he wished a more definite statement, Madame Rondel said, snorting, "Such nonsense over an adjectival overcoat button! I found the condemned button on the floor just under the adjectival window, between that wall and the quite unrepeatably described washstand."

He regarded the twine next, and asked concerning that. It had been found, like the button, by the washstand, one end tied to the pipe extending from the water-cooler to the wastepipe, its frayed end dangling. The other section had lain on the floor. 233

Apparently, though Madame Rondel not having given the matter a moment's thought, could not say definitely, this end had been either tied or wound about the bolt socket, which was quite large enough to form a projection to hold it. 

Craft visualized the line stretched, in that case, from one side of the washroom to the other. It would pass diagonally near the window, six inches or so above the sill. The end tied about the pipe would be about three inches from the window; the other, if the string were stretched taut, would be at least three feet from it.

He examined the twine itself and found it to be the sort often tied around heavy parcels, strong enough to resist breaking in his hands, but not strong enough to bear any heavy weight pulling against it. 

He returned the string to the little pile of débris, and directed Madame Rondel to do it up again, and ship it forthwith to the Cannes police. A crisp note passed between each of them, and a general good impression was left upon all by Craft's parting remark to Madame Gannet. "I believe the police will forget about the hat. It is unimportant. Suppose Madame Rondel present it to her son, whom it fits. And you, Madame Gannet, must buy one like it, but of the correct size, for your-nephew."

Thus was peace restored and a venerable alliance preserved. And Craft departed for the station, well pleased with himself, and the results of his day's work. 234

FTER Craft had excused himself from tea at the Collins' villa that afternoon, Marshall remained there, talking desultorily to Mrs. 

Bassington-Cowan, quite unaware of a single word she was saying, but under the impression that she was describing experiments she had begun in India years before, to determine the psychic capacity of monkeys, who, she insisted, were, in some respects, superhuman rather than subhuman. It was all very difficult to follow; and he watched Elise Valery instead, with a ferocity of expression that caused her acute uneasiness. She was trying to discuss new books with 

Colonel Poplin, but Marshall's continued staring kept her from making two consecutive remarks that made sense. 

Finally she apologized, and rose to go, pleading a mistral headache. Mrs. Collins did not press her to remain. 

But Elise had gone no farther than the vestibule before Marshall, very severe and very red, came after her. "Did you bring your car?" he asked sulkily. "No." She refused to face him, making much work of tucking a few locks of hair into place under her hat. "I'll take you back in mine." 235

"No, indeed! Don't bother," she said, in a discouraging tone. "I'll take a tram." "My car's here," Marshall insisted stubbornly, and she glanced at him and his frowning face, and felt a little sorry. "Very well. But please don't scowl so. You look like Hamlet."

He held the door open for her, and followed her down the garden path to the car. In a bitter silence he drove her to the hotel, parking his car on the Rue d'Antibes in a place where he knew it was not permitted. Not until he had reached the front door, to hold it open for her, could he summon up enough courage to say what he was bursting to say. "Miss Valery-I-can't we talk somewhere?"

She paused at the door and looked at him searchingly, wondering, perhaps, whether she had the strength just then to carry her through the scene she instinctively felt must come. Presently she sighed. "Very well. But must you look so desperate, Mr. Marshall?"

He resented her attempts to lessen a tension he had built up; this matter was deadly serious to him, and she should not try to pass it off lightly. It wasn't fair of her. And so he frowned again and said nothing.

She led the way through the lobby into the card room, which, just now, was empty. When he had seated himself uncomfortably, she said calmly: "What is it, Mr. Marshall? You're being rather mysterious, aren't you?" But she was not at ease. "Miss Valery-Elise-I want to ask you a question. Oh, I know I haven't the ghost of a right to and-Oh, hang it all! I sound like a melodrama!" 236

"You don't at all," she said helpfully. "What is it? I won't be offended." Because, of course, she knew what he intended to ask. "Miss Valery-I-M. Porteus, the Juge d'Instruction, told us you identified the body of Alfred Gilbert.' 

"Yes, I did, Mr. Marshall." And when she saw him go a little white and hesitate, she added, "You see, I once knew him."

Marshall said nothing for a long while, clasping and unclasping his hands unconsciously as they lay on his knees, his eyes averted. "It's so damned hard to say: did you know his name was really Gorman?"

Her sympathy, for the moment, was all for Marshall. She was not thinking just then of herself. She answered, "Yes," very quietly. "I knew him as Alfred Gorman, not Gilbert." "I see." "I met Alfred Gorman in Dinard more than a year ago, when I was there with Norman, my brother. He was staying under the name of 

Gilbert at a funny little hotel in Dinard, where he had managed, as he always did, to meet the people he wanted to. I had money-then-and he met me. 

I saw quite a good bit of him, at teas and at the casino. I don't know now how I stood the sight of him. After a month or so he asked me to marry him.

and went to a hotel. Norman's younger than I am, and I've always felt a sort of responsibility for him.

That wasn't so at all, because M. Chavelli had insisted upon having a drawing of the bracelet when he chose it; and, of course, Norman recognized it.

Chavelli, in examining the real one, had managed to substitute the paste one.

"I wondered. We-I just wondered, that's all." "No. He wouldn't let me come. I think he wanted people to believe we were only casual acquaintances." "Oh, heavenly bliss! Have you ever owned a flame-colored negligée?" "What on earth . . .? No, not in years." "Then-may I kiss you?"

She did not think it quite proper. But she wasn't one to bother about scruples under the circumstances.

CHAPTER 23: Treats of Dark Dealings at Lou Matagot AND 

ND NOW," said Elise, arising after a considerable time, "I've got to go. I promised the Temples I'd dine with them." "It's early." "I've got to dress. Really I have.
