They emerged from the card room without other visible signs of their interview, and its rather satisfying sequel, than an expansive air of benignity. He left her in the lobby, reluctantly.

For a while he debated with himself whether to remain at the hotel for dinner, or accept Craft's invitation to return to Lou Matagot. In either case, he faced a lonely evening; at the hotel it was possible 248

that he might run into some one with whom he could pass an hour or two. Not Temple, of course; but 

Poplin, perhaps. But the sight, just then, of Poplin himself entering the lift with Elise, and the raucous echoes of his laugh, decided him against that. He preferred a lonely but excellent dinner at Lou Matagot, and a book and the radio, and Moloch. He began to envy Craft; and after a moment, to picture himself in just such an environment as Craft had contrived for himself, embellished, in Marshall's case, naturally, by the presence of the woman he loved.

He drove through the gathering darkness toward the Route de Grasse and Craft's villa. Its gates stood open, and a light burned over the tiled court. Craft's servant, Pietro, opened the front door with a welcoming grin. "Dinner will be ready in a few moments, monsieur."

Marshall nodded, and went into the living-room, to the fire. Cosy, all this. . . . He wondered whether

Elise had any preferences when it came to home sites. 

He wasn't quite sure that he'd care to live in Cannes, himself. All right for a month or so, but permanently. no, entirely too artificial. A cocktail was brought to him, and he drank it contentedly. Dinner, when it was served, was as excellent as he had anticipated; afterwards he settled himself, with the radio bringing in a London concert, so subdued that he could identify nothing, yet heard, with a pleasurable effect of distant accompaniment to his mood. He read, and the hours began to slip by. At nine he roused himself long enough to ring for a brandy and soda. At ten he had finished the 249

novel he had skimmed through, reading, at most, the first and last sentences of each paragraph (a habit acquired in college, and vastly convenient when struggling through such literary and fossil masterpieces as 

Burke's "Conciliation" and Carlyle's "Heroes and 

Hero Worship"). He yawned and glanced at his watch. He could not expect Craft for an hour, at least.

He was becoming bored. Elise was probably dining out at some interesting place or another, dancing.

He'd rather like to dance, himself. As a substitute he switched off the radio, which had begun to describe the weather, and played the phonograph for a few moments. In the midst of it he heard the loud ringing of the doorbell. 

It couldn't be Craft returning so soon. He switched off the light and glanced out toward the terrace, but from his position he could see nothing but the red tail light of a car standing before the court. Pietro padded through the hall, and Marshall heard him opening the door. Some one asked, "Is Commander 

Craft home yet?" He recognized Elise's voice.

She came in an instant later, in a sapphire velvet evening gown and a white wrap. "Hello," she said. "Pietro says Commander Craft hasn't come back yet." "Not yet." He was surprised. "Don't tell me you came to see Craft and not me.' 

She nodded, smiling. 

"Is that very unflattering?" "I'd hoped you'd come to keep me company. I'm being bored to death." "We'll have to remedy that!" she declared. Pietro took her wrap. She came and stood with her back to 250

the fire. "But only incidently. I wonder what Commander Craft wanted." "What do you mean, Elise? I'm dense, I suppose." "Silly boy; not at all. Commander Craft telegraphed for me." "He telegraphed?" Marshall was more puzzled than ever. "Why did he do that?" "I haven't any idea. Would you like to see the telegram?" She took it from her bag and handed it to him, sinking down on the cushions on Moloch's lip while he opened it. "I've always wanted a home with a huge fireplace," she remarked parenthetically. "So have I. Always." He spread out the telegram and read it. "If you can, please come Lou Matogot tonight. Very important. Returning by eleven. 

"HOWARD CRAFT." 

"That sheds no light on the problem, does it?" he commented, handing it back to her. "No-unless. 

Do you suppose he's discovered something in Marseilles? He went there about the-murder, didn't he?" "Yes. I wonder what's turned up?" 

Pietro hovered in the background. "Is there anything I can bring you, mademoiselle? 

Something to drink?" "No, thank you, Pietro." "Coffee? A liqueur?" "Take some coffee; it's chilly out," Marshall urged her. "All right. Won't you have some, too? It's so stupid drinking it alone." 

Pietro departed. 251

"I don't know why Craft wanted you here tonight, but I'm thankful he did. And I'm very thankful indeed that you came." "So am I. I suppose, if they knew, every one at the hotel would be shocked to death; and to-morrow 

I'd be talked about terribly. However, that doesn't worry me."

She leaned back against the cushions, silhouetted against the flames which, to Marshall's very prejudiced eye, seemed to weave a halo about her. He sat opposite her on the sofa. There was no light in the room except that from the fire. Back of him, in shadows, were two French windows opening upon the terrace, where he had gone with Mrs. Collins the afternoon before. This living-room occupied one wing of the villa, and two other windows opened upon the tiled court, where there was still one lamp burning, over the entrance. 

Elise glanced up at Marshall, and hesitated. Her face was in shadows, but he sensed the sudden tensing of her body. "What's the matter?" he asked sharply, unconsciously lowering his voice. "Don't move," she said, hardly above a whisper. "Perhaps I'm-no, I'm not imagining things! Rob, there's some one out on that terrace! He's looking into the room!" "Craft, probably, coming back. No, I say, it's still too early for him, isn't it? Are you sure? I'm going to look." "Don't!" she commanded imperatively. "I'm frightened, Rob. I shouldn't be, I guess. There's a bell beside you there. Pretend everything's all right; he's watching. Ring for Pietro quickly." 252

"Nonsense! I'm going to see what's up." Before she could stop him he rose and faced the windows. 

Against the lesser darkness of the night he could see a figure outlined, a man, apparently, standing on the terrace peering into the room. 

He had no opportunity to notice more. He heard the roaring discharge of a firearm of some sort, and the tinkling of broken glass. Elise screamed in sudden alarm as a bullet struck the chimney over her with a sharp smack, and a little shower of brick dust fell on her.

Marshall, with a suddenness and gentleness that surprised her, pressed her down upon the sofa. Its high, thick back, heavily padded, offered a measure of protection. With an order to her not to move, he hurried around the side of the sofa and sprang to the window.

One of its panes had been shattered. He drew open its catch, and ran out to the court. There was no one there; it took no more than an instant to perceive that. He ran round the corner of the wing toward the terrace parapet, but he saw that no one could have escaped that way; here was only the drop of fifteen feet or so onto a grassy lower terrace, where a man would be plainly visible. 

Marshall doubled back toward the gates. Elise's 

Citröen was still parked before the court. It had not been tampered with, as far as he had time to see. 

He hurried on to the iron gates which still stood open.

Out in the road there was nothing to be seen. The road dipped down between whitewashed walls, too high to be scaled, and capped for almost their whole length with fragments of broken bottles. The en- 253

trances to two other villas were shut; there was no one on the road.

Toward the top of the hill the road dwindled into a path and vanished between the black bulks of farmhouses. He was surprised to see no lights in them.

Probably the people living there retired early. He hesitated only a moment before plunging up the path into the maze of buildings.

Hogs were squealing, and behind one high wall chickens, disturbed, were clucking with excitement. 

This wall, however, was too high to be scaled, and he pressed against a wooden gate let into it at one place, without being able to budge it.

He ran on, emerging suddenly upon a path skirting a hillside that dropped percipitously to the rooftops of the city far below. The path ended, on his right, at a barred gate. On his left it descended gradually to a thicket of mimosa trees, through which it ran, to double back on itself at a lower level, leading eventually into the town. 

He paused there, wondering whether to plunge down the hillside and attempt to intercept the man, if he had followed this path and was at present hidden among the mimosas-risking, in that case, the chance that the man might turn back and make his way to the road before Marshall could clamber up again—or whether to throw caution to the winds, and follow into the mimosas. It was dangerous; the man was armed and he was not. Moreover, at present he must make an excellent target, his dark suit outlined against the white walls. 

He waited, but heard nothing. Then, very cautiously, he dropped over the edge of the hillside, and, as quietly as he could, half crawled, half slid 254

to the lower stretch of the path, there turning up toward the mimosas. If he had, by any chance, not seen him (which seemed possible, since no other shot had been fired at him), he might be able to come upon him from the rear and surprise him. 

He reached the lower path at a point where it emerged from the grove. Silently he made his way into the blackness under the interwoven branches, gradually moving upward, his eyes unable to penetrate the dark. He was forced to pause every step or two to listen, but he heard nothing but the faint scratching of bough upon bough as the wind blew gently through the grove.

He emerged finally from the grove on the upper bend of the path, which he had but just quitted. 

There was no sign of the assailant anywhere. 255

CHAPTER 24: Contains Plottings with a Lady

A T LAST Marshall relinquished his vain search for the elusive attacker, and returned to the villa, which he found in more or less a state of siege. Elise had retired to a pantry which had no windows, where she was perched on the edge of a deal table, in the absence of chairs, while Pietro stood guard over her with a monstrous pistol which he had unearthed from a secret repository of his own. 

They had nothing to report. Pietro, returning to the living-room with the coffee a moment after Marshall had dashed out to the terrace, had found Elise stretched on the sofa wildly impatient to join the hunt, but sufficiently wise to foresee the complications that might arise had she done so. She had retired to the pantry at Pietro's suggestion, and they had remained there quietly until Marshall returned. "I don't think it would be wise to sit in the livingroom again," Pietro contributed. "He might come back." "Of course he might!" Marshall admitted. "Then what shall we do? Sit here in the pantry for the rest of the night?" Elise demanded. back," Pietro suggested. "Perhaps, if I were to sit in the window and keep watch . . ." "You don't need to do that," Marshall declared. "We won't be caught napping again."

They repaired to Craft's study, therefore, Pietro carrying a tray with hot coffee and his pistol, and made themselves as comfortable there as their excitement permitted. "Who in the world do you suppose it was?" Elise wondered, choosing her easy chair and sinking into it gratefully. "I can't imagine. Something tells me Craft will know. Funny how one comes to rely upon him. 

You'd think I was a two-year-old, the way he leads me around." "He's a dear. Did he know you were alive all the time, Rob?"

Marshall confessed that he had run to Craft the moment he could. "I see. And . . "There were dozens of questions that Elise wanted to ask, but she checked them. “Oh, I wish he'd come! I'm dying to know why he wanted me here."

CHAPTER 25: Discloses Matters of Importance 

RAFT'S activities, on the following morning,

began with a brief but profitable visit to the exhibition salon of M. Gustav Meynitz.

M. Meynitz was not personally acquainted with 

Craft, and he indulged in elaborate eye-raising and shoulder shrugging when he perceived that Craft was not a prospective collector, but merely a curious gentleman who wished to ask embarrassing questions. Meynitz thawed a trifle. He confessed that he had known Mr. Marshall, and that he had been greatly distressed to hear of his tragic death. He had heard nothing to disprove the original report. Craft left M. Meynitz in a pleasantly flustered state in the midst of his Cézannes and Laurençins and Van Goghs, and drove to the dèpôt des polices, where he asked to see M. Porteus. 

M. Porteus, that morning, was in an unpleasant mood, engendered, perhaps, by a feeling of futility insufficiently drowned in Macon. It would be an exaggeration to say that he welcomed Craft. He permitted him to enter, and conveyed a tentative invitation for him to remain, but beyond that he remained sunk in an abysmal gloom. Craft's very obvious good spirits annoyed him. "Ah, the amateur!" he remarked unpleasantly. "I suppose you come to report that you have not solved the mystery. As if that could possibly be news!" "On the contrary, I beg to report that I have solved it," said Craft genially. Porteus refused to be cheered. "Oh, the optimism of these free lances! Monsieur, 265

f when you have had our experience, not only with criminals, but with courts, and know the vagueries of judges. .! Then you can say, 'I have solved the case,' and be believed." "Oh, but I really have, M. le Juge." "Now, now," murmured Porteus soothingly. "Perhaps you have evolved a very interesting theory. 

Very probably. But have you proof?" He fairly thundered his question, and promptly repeated it, as much as to say this crux of the matter could not be too strongly emphasized. "Have you proof? When you have, then-ah, then, M. le Commander, you are a detective."

Porteus regarded him over the barricade of his great beard, a faint feeling of contrition for his harsh words assailing him. "Well," he added in a gentler tone, "perhaps you'd better tell me your theory."

Bellecour and Porteus had joined them, the subcellar was rather crowded.

With the officials as audience, the sergent-de-ville, under Marshall's guidance, began to tap upon the brickwork of the inner wall. It was a slow, monotonous process. The pulsations of his hammer beats, striking always on solid brick, echoed noisily in the chamber, and the three circles of light, converging upon one spot on the wall, produced a sort of hypnotic effect. Brick after brick felt the hammer's blow, and gave out the same thudding sound. Marshall transferred his torch from one hand to the other and shifted from foot to foot, impatiently. Porteus and Bellecour stood stolidly beside him, as unmoved as idols.

The sergent-de-ville approached that spot where 270

Marshall had heard hollow sounds just before Porey's interruption. Marshall drew in and held his breath as the hammer tappings drew closer and closer to the spot, and finally reached it. The hammer struck, and hovered, and a faint ringing sound was added to the thud. It struck again, and moved to the adjacent brick. But here there was nothing but the thudding again-solid brick in solid wall. The hammer moved on.

On that wall, eventually, they found no trace of a hidden chamber. Marshall was almost sick with disappointment.

The sergent-de-ville moved unconcernedly to the next wall and began his tappings again. The whole monotonous process was repeated, and the endless beating began to irritate him. He found himself foolishly counting the hammer strokes, and broke off. 

And began again a moment later, one-two, threefour, five-six. 

That wall, too, was barren of results. The third wall was the one facing the well, whose lower section hung over the crumbled floor. It was more difficult here to tap, without the danger of falling into the well, as Elise had done, and Marshall held his light upon the hole, rather than on the persistent hammer. 

And here, too, there was less probability of finding a concealed chamber. They had no way of measuring the wall's thickness, but they knew it to be the outer wall of the villa. Any chamber must lie over the well.

They found nothing, and the sergent-de-ville began upon the last wall, still indifferent, his strokes as even, as rhythmic, as before. Marshall's disappointment was pathetic. He hung upon each thud now 271

with a kind of desperation, almost overwhelmed by an impulse to slow the hammer's steady advance over the wall, as if, by drawing out the time, he might, in some way, increase the possibilities of success, or at least put off the moment when, the walls examined, they must admit there was nothing behind them. 

But the moment came, as if it were foredoomed, and the sergent-de-ville turned to his superiors and said, noncommitally, "There's nothing, messieurs, other than that one faulty brick. Shall I remove it on the chance. . .?” "No," Porteus decided, his own disappointment almost equalling Marshall's. "Do not bother. One brick: what is one brick?" "You intend to give up the search, then?" Marshall asked. "What else is there to do, monsieur?" "I don't know." Marshall scowled at the walls. "I say, Craft," he called up, "there's not a blasted thing down here. What'll we do?" "Have you tried the floor?" Craft asked. "No. It's cement." "Well, you might just try it." "All right." 

The hammer once more called into use, the sergent-de-ville, martyring his trousers in the cause of justice, crawled over the floor. Nothing-only dull thumpings. Except, of course, near the wall, where the floor had begun to crumble. There the blows reverberated from the depths of the well, with a ringing, hollow echo. "I think," said the sergent-de-ville, in a slightly different tone, "there may be a hollow place-very small-just here. Listen!" 272

He tapped experimentally two or three times.

There were echoes from the well, a sort of ringing echo reflected from the surface of the water, but in it an overtone, a high resonant note. "Viola! You hear?"

They heard, and crowded dangerously near the edge of the hole to watch his proceedings. It was easy to crumble and hammer away more of the cracked cement, and the sergent-de-ville lay flat on the floor to do so, to save himself from any possibility of falling.

After what seemed endless time to Marshall, he broke out a section of the flooring, four-sided, very roughly a foot by eighteen inches. It lifted out easily, revealing beneath it no solid mass of masonry, as might have been expected, but a darkish mass wrapped in sacking.

With Bellecour's help, he lifted out the mass, which fitted neatly into a cubical cavity a foot on each dimension, and set it upon the floor. Porteus poked at it curiously. "What do you imagine it is?" he asked wonderingly. "You don't expect two magnificent masterpieces to be hidden in such a small space, do you?"

Marshall shook his head. Bellecour rose to his feet and brushed off his hands. "I suggest," he said, "that we take it upstairs into daylight to open it. It would hardly be fair to M. le 

Commander not to permit him to witness his triumph."

So they made their way into the cellar, passing up the sacking-wrapped mass, which was curiously light. 

Craft glanced at it as Porteus carried it, smiling in a pleased way. 273

"You know what it contains?" Porteus asked, eyeing him. "I can guess. But we'll soon see."

They gathered in the living-room, warm and sunny in the bright morning light, the burden placed upon a table before them. With ceremonious touch, the sergent-de-ville slit the sacking and exposed, under it, heavy paper packing. "Like one of those Chinese things," Craft observed. "Every time you remove one covering, you find another underneath. Off with the paper, my lad. 

What's under it?" 

A heavy canvas wrapping was exposed next, not sewn, like the sacking, but merely wrapped about it. 

When it had been removed, they found four metal dispatch boxes fitted with locks. Porteus examined one of them curiously. "I suppose we shall have to break it open. We haven't any keys with us. Surely you didn't expect little boxes, Commander Craft?" "I rather imagined there might be boxes," Craft said placidly. "And you know what's in them?” "I-suspect." "Umm! Sergent, break them open."

The sergent-de-ville obeyed eagerly, his own unimaginative nature somehow infused with a trace of the others' excitement. A few twists with a chisel, a few taps with a hammer, and the dispatch case opened. "More containers," said Craft, glancing in at the long manila envelopes inside. Porteus, with an impatient gesture, grabbed one of them and ripped it open, pouring out its contents on the table-top. 274

One thousand dollars, in American bank notes, in denominations of hundred and fifty dollars.

The eyes of four of them bulged with surprise. 

Only Craft, watching their expressions with amusement, did not gape. He was placidly opening other envelopes and pouring out their contents. 275

CHAPTER 26: Explains Nearly 

Everything 

HE EQUIVALENT of about two hundred thousand dollars, in American money," Porteus announced, later, when he had finished a tentative count of the contents of the four boxes. It had seemed easier to reduce the lot to dollars; American bank notes predominated, though there were two or three envelopes of Bank of England notes to the amount of four thousand pounds. "The question occurs to me," said Bellecour, still regarding the money with popping eyes: "Who is entitled to this money? Whose is it?" "There you raise a pretty question," Craft remarked. "I'm curious to see how you'll settle it." "Whose was it originally? I suppose you know?" "I can't be certain; I think, in all probabilities, the money represents cash payments for paintings acquired-well, not quite honestly. Either stolen, like the Raphael and the del Sarto (and this may be payment for those particular paintings, for all we know), or smuggled out of Italy and sold to collectors who were not fussy about how they got hold of their little treasures." "But why hidden?" Marshall asked, puzzled. "For the very simple and sufficient reason, dear boy, that practically every note in this pile is traceable by number. If there were any questionable deal- 276

ings involved, the passing of these notes, so that they could not be traced to their possessor, would present a pretty problem. In the meantime, they had to be put somewhere for safe-keeping. It would be folly to try to deposit them in a bank, and not entirely safe to trust them to a safety deposit box, which might be opened on police orders. No; burying them was the only reasonable way of securing them." "I see. Then this money belonged to M. Chavins, you think?" "Ah, there's a distinction! It was in M. Chavins's possession, when he lived here. If it represents payment for stolen paintings, then, of course, the ownership of the money becomes a very involved problem indeed. If it really belonged to M. Chavins-and in that case I hardly think he'd be afraid to deposit it in some bank-then I should think it would be a toss-up between the French government, who, in the absence of heirs, acquired the estate, and the Trabeaux heirs, who bought the villa with contents intact. If such a question arises, I can imagine who will eventually claim it." "And you think-" Marshall, his mind crowded with new, confused ideas, struggled to put them into words. "You think Gilbert knew of this money here?" "No, Marshall. You've neatly hitched the cart in front of the horse. If Gilbert had known about it, there'd never have been all that hocus-pocus about paintings hidden here. Gilbert was quite sure there was nothing here. Otherwise, he'd have kept still as a mouse, and come after it himself." "Porey, then? Did he know?" "Now, I wonder!" said Craft, grinning. "Suppose 277

we wait until our little session this afternoon, to settle that question." "All right. But you're so darned mysterious about it." "Oh, let me have my fun," Craft begged. "For years I've wanted to stage a little dénouement like this. Heartless of me, I suppose; but I haven't a shade of sympathy or consideration for the person who killed Gilbert and tried his darnedest to kill you. 

Not a shade. And for the others . . . It will be something they will remember for the rest of their lives." "I suppose so. Well-until five, then."

At five, Porteus's little office was crowded. Porteus himself was there, seated behind his desk which had been moved to the end of the room by the door. 

Bellecour sat beside him, rather more pompous than Porteus, a mask for his inward excitement. Chairs had been arranged for witnesses, facing the desk, and there sat Marshall, with Elise beside him, quite openly permitting her hand to be held, but looking a trifle white and drawn, nevertheless. 

Craft sat at her left, impassive, leaning upon his cane and watching Porteus rather than the others.

The Collinses, both of them nervous-Dukane dabbing constantly at her lips with a small green handkerchief-were as close together as their chairs permitted. Next to them, Colonel Poplin loomed, a fatuous smile, as usual, on his lips, his monocle screwed into his eye. He was talking in an undertone to Major Porey, whose face was quite expressionless. 278

In the last row were Colonel Bassington-Cowan, looking very solemn; Mrs. Bassington-Cowan, who was trying desperately to appear tranquil, as if this were only a trifling incident in a dull day, but whose eye glittered, and whose fingers were so anxious to beat a tattoo on the chair arm that she was forced to clasp her hands firmly in her lap; Captain Temple, cool until you perceived the moisture on his forehead; and Mrs. Temple, as usual, perfectly at ease, looking rather lovely in a severe, gray, tailored suit and a tight, black, felt hat.

Marshall was surprised to find both M. Durand and M. Conti on little folding chairs near the door.

Porteus had risen, and was on the point of clearing his throat to open the meeting, when a sergent-deville knocked upon the door, and swung it wide open when Bellecour called to him to enter. There was a man outside, wishing to see Commander Craft: very important, he announced. Craft apologized for the interruption, and hobbled out. The room was utterly silent for the two minutes of his absence. He returned finally, and sank back in his chair. Porteus once more cleared his throat. "I am sorry," he began, "to be forced to interrupt your afternoon in this manner; but the investigation of the murder of M. Gilbert has progressed to a point where a solution, while imminent, is impossible without your assistance."

This burst of oratory concluded, he mopped his brow with a large handkerchief, and surveyed the assembled company. "There are certain questions to be asked of all of you, in the presence of the others," he continued. "If 279

each of you will kindly answer them to the best of your ability, we will appreciate it greatly."

With Bellecour he began a long examination. The ground had been covered before; nothing new was revealed. And Marshall, for one, was distinctly under the impression that the questioning merely masked some deeper purpose of Craft's. 

His guess was correct. Eventually Porteus concluded his interrogations, apologized again for disturbing them, and dismissed them all. 

But one of them was detained outside the office, led to another room, and there formally charged with the murder of Alfred Gilbert, alias Al Gorman.

Later that evening, Elise and Marshall sat in the living-room of Lou Matagot. Craft, on his sofa, oddly tired-looking, stared into Moloch's fiery throat. "I hardly know where to begin," he said, in answer to a question of Elise's. "The matter was mainly one of identity. You see, the situation with which we were faced was this: two men were attacked on the 

P.-L.-M. train, one fatally, and the other unsuccessfully, in such a manner that it seemed evident that the two attacks were part of one plot, if not actually the work of the same person. And yet the connection between the two men was of the slightest. "It was a sufficiently puzzling situation as it was, but gradually, as we investigated, more and more curious side-lights were thrown on it. "Now, Elise, I'm sorry to say that the whole affair cannot be explained without airing some rather dirty linen. I hate doing it; but in an informal session like 280

this we know it won't go any further. None of it will be news to either of you, in any case. "At first there was no trace of motive to be discovered for either attack; that, of course, was before 

Mr. Gilbert had been definitely identified, and it was necessary to allow the question to lapse, while we concentrated upon opportunity. And so I looked into the movements of each of our group on that train, and found, to make a long story short, that not one of them had any unassailable alibi for the moment when Marshall was attacked. "As for the moment when Gilbert was killedthere was no way of knowing, then, just when he was killed. So there were, of course, no certain alibis for that time, either. "At that point, then, we had the fact of the crime, no motive, and the knowledge that every one, with the exception of an art dealer in Cannes here every one who knew Marshall could have done it. "Then you, Elise, very graciously identified the body as that of Alfred Gilbert, and Marshall, with that hint, went further and identified him as Al Gorman, a jewel thief with whom he had once had an unfortunate experience. "There was no way of being certain, just then, that the two attacks were the work of the same hands. 

But the curious fact that the dead man was wearing Marshall's overcoat seemed to point to a single assailant; or, at least, some close connection between the two crimes. So I made a little mental chart on which 

I noted every point of connection between Al Gorman and Marshall. The table, as I remember it, was something like this: "Al Gorman was known to Marshall as a jewel 281

thief. Marshall had seen him, and could probably identify him if he came face to face with him (as he did later, when he was given a hint). Moreover,

Marshall had seen Gorman, though he didn't recognize him then, on Thursday morning. Gorman seemed to be curiously interested in Marshall's movements. That was the first point. The second was even more ephemeral, and was, after all, hardly more than conjecture. For your benefit, Marshall was interesting himself in the villa Chèvre d'Or, where, you believed, some valuable paintings were hidden. "I doubted the truth of the rumor that the paintings were there, for reasons you know. Later, I saw police records showing that the paintings had, in all probability, been taken to America. And when I discovered that you were somehow involved in Mr. 

Gorman's evil-doing, however innocently or unwillingly, a second connection between Gorman and 

Marshall came to light. "Now, I know you were acting in perfectly good faith, I may as well tell you at once, unaware that Gorman did not believe the paintings were in the villa, but was attempting to bring about a fraudulent sale of the villa with the nonexistent paintings as bait. "There was a third connection. You two were carrying on like a pair of silly love birds; and it looked like a serious case. "But considering these few facts led me to the conclusion that Al Gorman would have had a very good motive for killing Marshall himself. He had encountered Marshall and believed-we know—that he had been recognized. He was afraid that his plans would not only fall through, but that he would be 282

arrested as a fugitive from justice. And Marshall's interest in you could only lead to embarrassment for him. There was easily a strong-enough motive to cause Al Gorman, faced with the opportunity, to thrust Marshall through a door that was not locked, to a death that would certainly appear accidental. "And when I learned that Gorman, as soon as he discovered that Marshall had no firm belief in the existence of those paintings, immediately left his compartment on that train, to go back toward Marshall's, and that this occurred just a moment before Marshall's accident, the case looked fairly conclusive. "There only remained to discover who, subsequently, killed Alfred Gorman. And the scarf gave us our clue. Mrs. Temple insisted that she had borrowed it from her husband, and had lost it, she believed, at the hotel where she had dined with Colonel and Mrs. Bassington-Cowan. But no one had found it there; and some one had found it somewhere. 

Some one other than Mrs. Temple, I mean, because 

Mrs. Temple was not on the train Thursday afternoon. "She said she had gone, after dinner, to the casino, and had finally come home. But she had gone somewhere else that night. "I was certain of that, because on Wednesday afternoon at rehearsal she wore a pin of diamonds set in the shape of a shamrock. That pin was found later on the pin-cushion in a villa in La Napoule, which had been locked from Thursday morning on.

In other words, that pin could only have got there 

Wednesday night. And if the pin was dropped there, why not the scarf?" 

Craft cleared his throat. 283

"Incidentally, Porteus returned the pin to Mrs.

Temple this afternoon, and she identified it as hers absolutely; not that I had very much doubt about it. 

But to go on: "Colonel Poplin drew unnecessary attention of the police upon himself by warning Gorman, just before the train left Nice, that he was playing with fire. But that's by the way. The point is that the scarf could have been in Al Gorman's possession on that train. 

And its possession disclosed a man with a motive for killing him. "The question of opportunity came up then. Now, it happened that I had made a little test yesterday afternoon, and I discovered a childishly simple arrangement by which it could be made to appear that 

Gorman had been killed at some point beyond

Cannes, instead before the train reached Cannes. In the washroom on the car from which Marshall was pushed the cleaners found two pieces of cord-or, rather, one broken piece-and an overcoat button torn from the coat Marshall had dropped, and which Gorman was wearing when his body was found. "The button indicated-though, of course, it did not prove that Al Gorman, at some time after Marshall's fall, was in that washroom, and that there was, if not a struggle, certainly some violent activity to pull off that button. In support of this conclusion are two little bits of evidence-one, that Marshall himself was not in the washroom at any time, and, two, that the buttons on his coat were too well sewed to come off without a strong pull upon them. "Now, one bit of that cord had been tied about a pipe leading from a water-cooler to the wastepipe of the washstand, near the window. The two pieces to- 284

gether were just long enough to stretch from the pipe to the bolt on the door. It would then stretch directly across the window, which is large enough to permit the passage of a man's body. "So it was my belief that whoever murdered Al

Gorman drew him into that washroom and strangled him there, lifted his body to the washbowl, and held it there by that string tied across the window. It was not very strong string. It was almost certain to snap before long; and then the body would pitch out of the window to lie on the tracks. The murderer had no fear of interruption; he could bolt the washroom door from within and be undisturbed, and, in order to delay discovery of the body, for a while at least, he locked the door from the outside, with a key that always hung on the wall just by the coach door. "No passenger could enter the washroom in that case; and it was unlikely that the guard would do so. 

It was a chance the murderer must take. The first lurch of the train around a bend would throw the body against the cord, and undoubtedly snap it, and the body would hurtle through the open window.

After that it hardly mattered who entered the washroom. There'd be nothing suspicious in it but a piece of broken cord. "The case, then, was fairly complete, especially when it was found that the murder must have been committed between the time of the stopping of the train after Marshall's fall (otherwise how could the overcoat have been found on Gorman?) and the time of arrival at Cannes station. Ample time, if you figure it out, and that little cord arrangement would not take more than a moment to prepare, once its advantages had occurred to the murderer. 285

"But-and there is, I must say at once, a terrific 'but.' Last night, while Marshall was here, some one fired through the window at him. Now, Al Gorman, who had a motive for killing Marshall, was dead; either we had to assume that some other person had a motive also, and was attempting a crime independent of the others, or that our assumption concerning Al 

Gorman's guilt was erroneous, and that some one else had pushed Marshall from the Cannes train. "This promptly re-raised the specter of the single murderer, and a connection between Al Gorman and Marshall. There was the possibility of two separate murderers or intended murderers; but to assume three-counting the sniper last night as the thirdwas stretching coincidence to the breaking point. And if there were one, and only one, then Captain Temple was automatically eliminated, because he was in plain sight of a witness at the moment when Marshall was attacked on the train. Mrs. Collins tells me that at the time of the sniping last night the captain was taking a highball with her, and we had evidence that Temple was at the casino on Thursday at the time when the intruder came into Marshall's room at his hotel. "So it was necessary to review what evidence we had, and reach new conclusions. And the only conclusion to be reached was that there was some connection between Al Gorman and Marshall that made their death necessary to the furtherance of some one's schemes. "So the chart was brought out again, dusted off, and reëxamined, and some very tenuous inductive reasoning indulged in. Suppose I said to myself— 286

suppose both Al Gorman and Marshall possessed some information (unknown to themselves, perhaps) which made it necessary to eliminate both of them. 

What could that information be? It seemed, at first glance, that it could not possibly be anything in connection with the Chèvre d'Or-the only link, other than you, Elise, between them. Because, of course, you shared that knowledge, and there had been no attack upon you at all." "There was last night," Elise pointed out. "You thought there was, possibly. But you must not forget that you furnished an excellent target for a long time, judging from your description of the attack, before a shot was fired. And then it was fired only when Marshall arose from the protection of the sofa where he had been sitting. The shot, therefore, was apparently aimed at him. "So I say again that there had been no attack on you. And if the Chèvre d'Or was involved, that was inexplicable-unless the murderer was aware that your sole interest in the villa was the result of Gorman's urging and, practically, commands. The truth is that you would probably have made no effort to secure those paintings without either Marshall or Al 

Gorman to urge you." "You are quite right, Commander Craft," Elise said. "Very well; assuming all this (and just then, when there were so few facts to base conclusions on, it was necessary to assume) then it seemed likely that there was something hidden in the Chèvre d'Or which the murderer wished to secure, and which Gorman's and Marshall's interest in the villa made it difficult, or 287

impossible, to reach. It would have been fatal to his plans if Marshall, believing the pictures to be buried in the subcellar, should start digging there." "And Porey was interested in the villa, too," Marshall remarked reflectively. "With a difference, Marshall. He had no knowledge whatever of the subcellar's contents. He wished to buy the villa to live in-and I don't think I'm very wrong in saying that his interest was partially due to 

Elise's interest. He didn't care to be outdone. A trace of jealousy, perhaps. "Now, if something were buried there, and the paintings were not, as I felt convinced, then, what was buried there? And the answer occurred to me almost at once: cash. Knowing the reputation of M.

Chavins, and the rumors that his art deals were not always strictly honest, it was possible that he would have on his hands cash in large bills which it would be difficult to pass without embarrassing questions if the bills were traced by number. For reasons which the police know, it seemed likely that Chavins might have a hiding place for those bills; and his sudden death made it possible, or even probable, that the hiding place was unknown, except, perhaps, to a confidant of his. The money, in other words, might still be hidden somewhere on the premises. "These assumptions were plausible, and they led to others. If these could be shown to be correct, then a motive for murder was found, and it was possible to consider the qualifications the murderer must possess." "A very weak motive, though, isn't it?" Marshall interrupted. "I mean, what was to prevent the murderer from simply buying the villa himself?" 288

Craft laughed. "That was the weak link in my theory, and, strange to say, the one incorrect inference. But the line of reasoning I followed led straight to the truth, as I shall show you. We were speaking, however, of the murderer's qualifications. "First, he must be aware of your efforts, Elise, to interest Marshall in the nonexistent paintings. Well, nearly every one at the first rehearsal knew Marshalĺ was becoming interested in the Chèvre d'Or, even if they didn't know why. "Then, second, and almost a corollary of the first, he must know Gorman's relationship to you, so that he might be aware of the undercurrents of the whole plot in which Marshall was to be victimized. Otherwise he would certainly have included you in his list of those to be eliminated. "Third: he must be some one who had been sufficiently in the confidence of M. Chavins to know of the existence of the money. Now, there was one person who might have been in his confidence; and that was his agent, Jason Gregory. We shall return to 

Mr. Gregory. "Fourth, he must be without an alibi for the time when he was seen in Marshall's room. "And fifth, he must be without an alibi for last night's attack. "If any one could be found, possessing all these requirements, it promised to be worth while inquiring into his actions more closely. "That was how affairs stood this afternoon, when we all met at the dépôt des polices. My object was to gather every one who could possibly be suspected into that room, and then allow some one who knew 289

Gregory to see him, and, if possible, identify him. 

You remember when I was called out of the room?

Well, the door was left open purposely, and a man whom Gustav Meynitz had sent looked in and identified Gregory at once. "And so, when we left, Colonel Poplin was detained and charged with murder. "Rather foolishly, I think, he made a statement.

I say foolishly, because I doubt whether there was anything more than a case of suspicion against him; and proof was impossible. But he blustered and talked too freely." "But I still don't understand when it was done," said Elise. "I'll explain. Mrs. Collins was dozing in her compartment; you remember how vague she was about her time. Colonel Poplin stepped out into the corridor to go to the washroom. As he was coming out he saw Marshall standing by the door, and, as he told Porteus after you left this afternoon, the temptation came to him suddenly. Elise had hinted to him that. you were interested in the villa; you, therefore, threatened to interfere with his plans to obtain two hundred thousand dollars, Marshall. If you were put out of the way, even temporarily, he might still have the opportunity to find the money before the villa was sold. What he did was not premeditated, and it was not attempted murder. There you stood by an unlocked door that opened outward. He simply stepped forward, struck you, and immediately returned to the washroom. He was there, then, when the train stopped. "He waited until the guard, Biondetti, had alighted, and then slipped back to his compartment. 290

I suppose they assumed, if they noticed him at all, that he had just stepped out into the corridor for an instant, and had not gone as far as the end of the car. 

But that's getting ahead of the story." "But the scarf?" Elise moved closer to Marshall, and allowed him to hold her hand. "You remember that Al Gorman left you to go toward Marshall's compartment just before the attack? He was there. He saw the attack, standing in the connecting passage between the two cars. He jumped forward to catch Marshall before he fellan instinctive act, I think. But in grabbing for him he must have dropped the scarf which was in his pocket. Marshall's coat was lying on the platform; he picked it up." "But didn't Poplin see him?" Marshall asked. "He caught a glimpse of him as he jumped forward. Poplin had been standing by the door of the washroom. He struck Marshall with the knob of his walking stick, and immediately sprang into the washroom, and locked the door, without waiting to see what happened. He saw Gorman spring out of nowhere, and for a moment was terrified that he had been seen. He waited in the washroom in a panic. 

But when no one rapped on the door, he decided he had been wrong, and when he thought it was safe, ventured out." "And the guard didn't see him, either?” "No, because, as we've seen and been told a dozen times, he was hidden from the guard by the corner of the washroom. As for Gorman, he was, too. Probably, as the guard came forward, he stepped back into the other car. "We do know, from what Poplin said, that a mo- 291

ment after the train stopped Poplin opened the door of the washroom and started to come out. Gorman had been waiting for that. Before he could get away, Gorman had pushed him back in, and come in after him, locking the door behind him. "Blackmail, of course; that was one of Gorman's side lines. He had seen Poplin push Marshall from the train, and he demanded a neat sum to keep his mouth shut. Poplin was in a blue funk. He is a powerful man. He struggled with Gorman, but the end was inevitable. And then, having the string in his pocket, he rigged up his little arrangement, to delay the finding of the body. As for the overcoat, he thought it was Gorman's, and put it on him, to prevent any one from finding it in the washroom and suspecting the modus operandi of his little mechanism." "And his business in my room?" Marshall demanded. "That's always puzzled me as much as anything." "He hadn't much time to work. Just at the last he saw a La Napoule ticket and a folder of traveler's checks lying on the washroom floor. It was easier to stuff them into his own pocket than into the pocket of the overcoat on the body from which they'd fallen -you remember the ripped pocket. It was only later, when he returned to the hotel-his tea at the Lido, of course, was fictitious-he was amazed to discover that the checks were yours, not Gorman's. It was a comparatively simple matter to return them to your room. You never locked your door, you know." "None of us did. The maid asked us not to. She had no key of her own." "So, naturally, Poplin knew it was easier to return 292

them than to try to destroy them. He had no idea that you were in the room at that moment; he pictured you lying injured on the railway embankment. 

He must have been frightened half out of his wits. 

But I take off my hat to him for one thing. When you appeared at tea the other day, he didn't give himself away. I was watching him like a hawk. His surprise was well done. And I was still suspecting 

Temple then. "But as soon as he was certain you were still alive, he began to fear what you might say. He had no idea how much you knew, but he did know that you were the only one who could cause trouble. If you had caught a glimpse of him, and accused him of attacking you, it might lead to an exposure of the murder. 

So, in desperation last night, he made the second attack on you, and that time it was attempted murder and no less. Luckily he didn't wait to see the results of his shot, or he might have run into you outside." "And what is to become of the money?" Marshall asked. "Has any one a solution for that terrible tangle?" 

Craft laughed a little. "Yes; it didn't take the French officials long to decide that. Since the money was found in the house, and had been placed there by M. Chavins, it is not, technically, buried treasure, but rather an asset of the

Chavins estate, and as such it reverts in whole to the French government.' 

"Well, I'm glad of one thing," Elise declared with conviction. "I'm terribly thankful Rob came straight to you. You've managed to unravel our troubles so successfully. There's no shadow hanging 293

over Norman any longer. Robinson and I-but I don't have to tell you that." "No. Nor accuse me of bringing you together. It was an obliging well that did that, better than any human agency could." "Perhaps. Still, you've been an angel." "Don't call me an angel!" Craft protested in alarm. "Just now I feel anything but angelic. I've always had an idea I'd make a rather good detective. 

Well-here and now I solemnly vow: never again! 

So help me!"

THE END
